1. Welcome

SHOBS-P (Shiloh Hill Observatory Photometry) is a desktop program (Windows, macOS and Linux) for differential aperture photometry. It takes a night of raw FITS frames from a color (OSC) or monochrome camera and turns them into a calibrated light curve and a report you can send to the AAVSO or ExoFOP, all in one window.

You do not need to be a programmer to use it. If you can take a night of frames with bias, darks and flats, you can reduce them with SHOBS-P.

Who it is for

SHOBS-P is written for amateur observers who send data to the AAVSO, to Exoplanet Watch, or who just want to know whether a star varies. It works with:

  • Color (OSC) cameras. The color filter mosaic is split into its colors without mixing them (a "superpixel" debayer). The green channel is the usual choice and is reported to the AAVSO as TG.
  • Monochrome cameras with Johnson V, B, Cousins R, clear or LRGB imaging filters, and Sloan g′ r′ i′ z′ u′.
  • Frames with or without a plate solution. If your capture program writes the field's position in the FITS header, SHOBS-P uses it. If not, it finds the field from the target's name, or from a rough field center you type.

The three modes

SHOBS-P has three modes. You choose one with the buttons at the top left of the window.

Mode Use it to…
Variables Follow a known variable star over one night or many: light curve, period search, times of maximum and minimum, AAVSO Extended reports.
Transits Catch an exoplanet transit: planet lookup, transit fit with a plain verdict (detected, inconclusive or none), AAVSO exoplanet report and ExoFOP package.
Discovery Measure every star in a field and flag the ones that vary more than stars of the same brightness. No target star needed.

All three modes share the same five steps: Input → Blink → Calibrate → Photometry → Output. Part 2 of this manual covers the first four steps, which work the same way in every mode. Parts 3, 4 and 5 cover each mode's own work.

What SHOBS-P does for you

  • Calibrates your lights with master bias, dark and flat frames, and saves the masters so the next night is quicker.
  • Finds the field by itself (a plate solve) and asks the AAVSO comparison-star sequence, Gaia DR3, APASS and Tycho-2 for the stars in it. Click a star and its name and magnitude fill in.
  • Suggests comparison stars close to your target in brightness and color, with AAVSO sequence stars first.
  • Checks your choices: it warns you when a comparison star is a known variable, much brighter or fainter than the target, or very red.
  • Lines frames up through meridian flips and flags saturated frames, cloud and drift.
  • Gives honest error bars: photon and sky noise in electrons, plus atmospheric twinkling (scintillation), so the error bars match the real scatter.

What SHOBS-P does not do

Knowing the limits saves time:

  • It does not run your camera or mount. Take your frames with your usual capture program, then open them here.
  • It reads FITS and PixInsight XISF files only. Camera RAW, TIFF and JPEG files are not read.
  • It does not transform magnitudes. Results are untransformed (no transformation coefficients yet), and the report says so.
  • It does not support Johnson/Cousins I yet. The comparison-star catalogs it uses carry B, V, R and Sloan bands only.
  • It does not use other programs' master frames. Point it at your raw bias, dark and flat frames instead. It builds its own masters and reuses them.
  • It does not predict transits. It fits the transits you observed; choose the night with your usual planning resource (chapter 16).
  • It needs the internet for lookups: star and planet names, catalogs and the variable-star check. Calibration and photometry themselves run offline.
  • Windows 10 and 11 are the most tested. It also runs on a Mac and on Linux, with their own start files since 2.2.19 (chapter 2); reports from Mac and Linux users are especially welcome.

How to use this manual

  • Part 1 (chapters 1–4) gets you installed and ready for your first night.
  • Part 2 (chapters 5–8) walks through the steps every mode shares.
  • Parts 3–5 are task-based: "reduce a variable-star night", "fit a transit", "scan a field".
  • Part 6 is reference: files, messages, troubleshooting and a glossary.

Button names and other words you see on the screen are printed in bold, exactly as the program shows them.

Tip: The quickest way to learn SHOBS-P is to press Try it with sample data… on the Input page. It writes a small practice night and fills in every box. Chapter 2 shows how.

Previous: Contents · Next: 2. Installing and starting

2. Installing and starting

SHOBS-P is a Python program. You install Python once, unzip SHOBS-P, and run one file that sets everything else up. It takes about ten minutes, most of it waiting for downloads.

What you need

  • Windows 10 or 11, a Mac (macOS 12 Monterey or newer), or Linux. The Windows steps come first; Linux and the Mac have their own sections below.
  • An internet connection (for the install, and later for star and catalog lookups).
  • Python 3.11 or newer. Python from python.org is the simplest. Anaconda or Miniconda also works. On Linux, Python 3.13 is the best choice; on the Mac, Python 3.14 (see below).

Install Python (Windows)

  1. Go to python.org/downloads and download the latest Python for Windows.
  2. Run the installer. On the first screen, tick Add python.exe to PATH. This matters: the SHOBS-P start files find Python this way.
  3. Click Install Now and wait for it to finish.

Tip: If you already have Python 3.11 or newer (from python.org, Anaconda or Miniconda), you can skip this. SHOBS-P checks each Python it finds and uses the first one that has everything it needs, including Tkinter, the window toolkit. SHOBS-P makes its own environment from it (see below), so your Python is normally left as it is.

Install SHOBS-P on Windows

  1. Open the SHOBS-P releases page at github.com/SHOBS-S/SHOBS-P/releases.
  2. Under the latest release, download the file SHOBS-P_vversion.zip (for example SHOBS-P_v2.3.0.zip).
  3. Unzip it somewhere permanent, for example C:\Astro. Avoid your Downloads folder: the shortcuts point at this folder, so it should not move.
  4. Open the folder the zip made. It holds files such as clearv_app.py, Create shortcuts.bat, Run self-test.bat, run_windows.bat and README.txt.
  5. Double-click Create shortcuts.bat. A black window opens. The first time it:
    • says Looking for a Python that can run SHOBS-P... and lists each Python it tries, ending with usable: and the one it picked;
    • sets up SHOBS-P's own Python environment in %LOCALAPPDATA%\SHOBS-P\python-env (your own Python is left untouched);
    • says Checking the Python packages SHOBS-P needs... and downloads them (NumPy, SciPy, Matplotlib, Astropy, photutils, ccdproc and tzdata, the time zone list). This takes a few minutes.
  6. When it says Done. Start SHOBS-P from the Desktop icon or the Start menu., press any key to close the window.

You now have a SHOBS-P icon on the Desktop and in the Start menu.

If the window instead says The shortcuts could not be made. Leave this window open and copy the message above., the lines above it say why. If it says SHOBS-P could not find a Python it can use., every Python it tried is listed with the reason it was not usable. Install Python from python.org (tick Add python.exe to PATH) and run Create shortcuts.bat again.

Tip: To pin SHOBS-P to the taskbar, open the Start menu, right-click SHOBS-P and choose Pin to taskbar. You can also right-click the running program's taskbar button. The pin keeps the SHOBS-P icon.

If you ever move the SHOBS-P folder, double-click Create shortcuts.bat again so the shortcuts point at the new place.

Install on Linux

  1. Install Python with Tkinter, the window toolkit. Python 3.13, 3.12 or 3.11 is best. Most distributions already have Python; add Tkinter (and, on Debian-based systems, the venv package) with your package manager:
    • Debian, Ubuntu, Mint: sudo apt install python3-tk python3-venv
    • Fedora: sudo dnf install python3-tkinter
    • Arch: sudo pacman -S tk
  2. Unzip SHOBS-P anywhere in your home folder.
  3. Open a terminal in that folder and type ./run_linux.sh. The first start finds a Python that works, makes SHOBS-P's own environment in ~/.local/share/SHOBS-P/python-env (your own Python is left untouched) and downloads the packages. This takes a few minutes. The messages are the same as on a Mac (see "What the first start says" below). Later starts skip the download, unless a new version needs other packages.
  4. For an entry in your applications menu, type ./run_linux.sh --desktop once. It needs no sudo. It also puts an icon on your Desktop, if you have one. Run it again if you move the SHOBS-P folder.
  5. To check the install, type ./run_selftest.sh (see the self-test below).

If the terminal says ✖ SHOBS-P could not find a Python it can use, every Python it tried is listed above that line with the reason. When Tkinter is missing, it names the package to install. Install it and run ./run_linux.sh again.

Install on a Mac

  1. Install Python 3.14 from python.org/downloads/macos. It includes Tk, the window toolkit. Pick 3.14 even if a newer Python is offered: a brand-new Python often does not have ready-made copies of all the packages SHOBS-P needs yet (see below). Python 3.13 works too, but python.org has no Mac installers for 3.13 after 3.13.17, so take 3.13.17 if you want 3.13. (A Homebrew Python works too, with brew install python-tk@3.14 for a Python 3.14.)
  2. Unzip SHOBS-P somewhere permanent, for example in Documents.
  3. Double-click Start SHOBS-P.command. The first time, macOS may refuse to open a file from an unidentified developer. Right-click (or Control-click) the file, choose Open, then Open again. You only do this once. On macOS 15 and newer there may be no Open: then go to System Settings → Privacy & Security, scroll down and press Open Anyway. Or, in Terminal in the SHOBS-P folder, type xattr -d com.apple.quarantine *.command *.sh; that clears every SHOBS-P start file at once.
  4. The first start makes SHOBS-P's own environment in ~/Library/Application Support/SHOBS-P/python-env and downloads the packages, which takes a few minutes. Keep the Terminal window open while SHOBS-P runs; closing it closes the program.
  5. To check the install, double-click Run self-test.command.
  6. For a SHOBS-P app with its icon (from 2.3.2), double-click Create shortcut.command once. It makes SHOBS-P.app with the SHOBS-P logo in Applications (the one in your home folder if you may not write to the main one) and puts SHOBS-P on your Desktop; drag it to the Dock to keep it there. The app starts SHOBS-P from this folder through Terminal (macOS lets Terminal, but not a homemade app, read folders in Downloads, Desktop and Documents). If SHOBS-P has not been set up yet, and at the first start of a new version or folder, the Terminal window stays up so you can watch the setup; other starts put it in the Dock as SHOBS-P starts. Leave it there while SHOBS-P runs (closing it closes SHOBS-P). When SHOBS-P closes, the Terminal window comes back up (with the message, if SHOBS-P stopped with an error); then close it. While SHOBS-P runs, its Dock icon is Python's (the program is Python's window), and opening the app again only says that SHOBS-P is already running. The first time, macOS may ask whether Terminal may open files in your Downloads (or Desktop, Documents) folder: answer OK. Run Create shortcut.command again if you move the SHOBS-P folder or unzip a new version somewhere else.

SHOBS-P opens at a size that fits the screen between the menu bar and the Dock (from 2.3.2); make it bigger or press the green button to fill the screen.

What the first start says (Linux and Mac)

The Terminal window tells you what it is doing. A first start looks like this (your paths and version numbers will differ):

Looking for a Python that can run SHOBS-P...
  Trying /Library/Frameworks/Python.framework/Versions/3.13/bin/python3...
    usable: Python 3.13.1

Setting up SHOBS-P's own Python environment with Python 3.13.1 (/Library/Frameworks/...)
in /Users/you/Library/Application Support/SHOBS-P/python-env
(that Python itself is left untouched). The first time this downloads the packages: a few minutes...
Installing the Python packages SHOBS-P needs...
    getting numpy
    getting scipy
    ...
✔ SHOBS-P is set up with Python 3.13.1.
Using Python: /Users/you/Library/Application Support/SHOBS-P/python-env/bin/python3

Then the SHOBS-P window opens. A Python that cannot be used says not usable: and why, and SHOBS-P tries the next one.

Which Python SHOBS-P picks. It tries the Pythons it finds in the order 3.13, 3.12, 3.11, then newer ones. It only installs ready-made copies of the packages, so it never has to compile anything, and a Mac never asks you to install Apple's developer tools. A brand-new Python (for example 3.15) often has no ready-made copies yet. Then SHOBS-P says:

⚠ Some packages SHOBS-P needs have no ready-made copy for Python 3.15.0 yet
  (they would have to be compiled, and SHOBS-P never compiles).
  Trying the next Python.

That is not an error. SHOBS-P simply moves on. If you also have 3.13 (or 3.12 or 3.11), it uses that one. If the new Python was the only one, the window ends with:

✖ SHOBS-P could not set up its packages with any Python on this computer (each one is listed above).
  Python 3.15.0 has no ready-made copies of some packages yet.

and says what to do: on a Mac, install Python 3.14 from python.org and double-click again (your other Pythons can stay); on Linux, install Python 3.13, 3.12 or 3.11 with Tkinter. Nothing else needs to change.

When something fails.

  • ✖ SHOBS-P could not find a Python it can use means none of the Pythons found has Tkinter or is new enough (3.11 or newer). Each one is listed above that line with the reason. On a Mac, install Python 3.14 from python.org.
  • ✖ The packages could not be downloaded. Check the internet connection and try again. means just that.
  • pip, the tool that downloads the packages, writes its own detailed messages to pip_log.txt next to the environment: ~/Library/Application Support/SHOBS-P/pip_log.txt on a Mac, ~/.local/share/SHOBS-P/pip_log.txt on Linux. If you report a setup problem, attach this file. Chapter 24 has more.

Check the install with the self-test

  1. In the SHOBS-P folder, double-click Run self-test.bat (Windows) or Run self-test.command (Mac). On Linux, type ./run_selftest.sh in a terminal in that folder.
  2. Wait a minute or two. The transit fits are the slow part. The test changes no settings and no data. A SHOBS-P window may open and close by itself; that is part of the test.
  3. The last line should read RESULT: ALL PASSED.

The test writes its log to selftest_log.txt in the SHOBS-P folder, and a copy to SHOBS-P_selftest_log.txt in your Downloads folder (on Linux and the Mac, when you have one). If you ever report a problem, attach this file.

A line such as (no sample transit reports to run on: skipped) or (NASA Exoplanet Archive not reachable: skipped) is normal: that part of the test is simply skipped.

Tip: The transit part of the test runs on sample AAVSO exoplanet reports, and none come with SHOBS-P. To run it, make a folder named selftest_reports next to selftest.py and put reports named AAVSO_TrES-3b_*.txt, AAVSO_KOI-217b_*.txt or AAVSO_WASP-12b_*.txt in it.

If the result says FAILED, the lines marked FAIL say which part. See chapter 24, Troubleshooting.

Start SHOBS-P

Double-click the SHOBS-P icon on the Desktop, or choose it from the Start menu. The program opens without a console window. On Linux, type ./run_linux.sh or choose SHOBS-P from the applications menu; on a Mac, open SHOBS-P from the Desktop, the Dock or Applications (made by Create shortcut.command), or double-click Start SHOBS-P.command. Any error is shown in the program itself and written to a log file (see "When something goes wrong" below).

The first time, SHOBS-P opens:

  • in Variables mode (navy),
  • on the Input page,
  • with the status bar at the bottom saying Choose the calibration and light folders to begin.

The window fills most of the screen. You can resize it or maximize it like any other window.

Try it with sample data

Before you open your own frames, try a practice night. It shows every step without any risk.

  1. Make an empty folder, for example C:\Astro\SHOBS-P sample.
  2. On the Input page, press Try it with sample data… (bottom of the page).
  3. Pick the empty folder. SHOBS-P writes a small synthetic night into it: bias, darks, flats and 40 lights of a made-up variable star.
  4. All the folder boxes, the target, the comparison and check stars are filled in for you.
  5. Press the steps on the left in turn: 2 Blink, 3 Calibrate, 4 Photometry, 5 Output. On the Calibrate page press Build masters and calibrate; on the Photometry page press Run photometry.

The sample frames are tiny and carry no sky position, so the Photometry page will say the image is not solved. That is expected here; the stars are already marked for you.

Other ways to start

  • run_windows.bat starts SHOBS-P with a black console window beside it, using the same Python as the shortcuts. It also checks the Python packages first. Use it when the program will not start from the icon: the console shows the error, and the window stays open with The app did not start. Leave this window open and copy the message above.
  • On Linux or a Mac, use the start files described above (run_linux.sh, Start SHOBS-P.command). The terminal window shows any error, and says SHOBS-P did not start, or stopped with an error. Copy the message above.

Starting afresh

If the install gets into a muddle, delete SHOBS-P's own environment and let the next start set it up again. On Windows, delete the SHOBS-P folder in %LOCALAPPDATA% and run Create shortcuts.bat again. On Linux, delete the SHOBS-P folder in ~/.local/share; on a Mac, delete the SHOBS-P folder in ~/Library/Application Support. Your settings are not in that folder, so they are kept.

Updating to a new version

  1. Download the new zip from the releases page.
  2. Unzip it. You can replace the old folder or unzip beside it.
  3. On Windows, run Create shortcuts.bat in the new folder. (On Linux or a Mac, just start the new version with its start file; run ./run_linux.sh --desktop again for the menu entry, or on a Mac Create shortcut.command for the app.) It points the shortcuts at the new version and installs any new packages it needs. SHOBS-P's own Python environment is shared by every version, so this is quick. (The first time you run it after updating from 2.2.18 or older, it sets that environment up and downloads the packages once.)

Your settings (site, telescope, and so on) are kept in your user folder (your home folder), not in the SHOBS-P folder, so an update does not lose them. Chapter 3 lists what is remembered.

When something goes wrong

  • If something fails inside the program, a box says Something went wrong: with the error, and the details go to .shobs_p.log in your user folder (C:\Users\<you> on Windows, your home folder ~ on Linux and the Mac).
  • .shobs_p.log also holds every line of the Log window, each with the date and time in front. When it grows past 5 MB it is renamed .shobs_p.log.1 (one old file is kept) and a new log starts.
  • The version is shown at the bottom of the left-hand sidebar (for example Version 2.3.0).
  • To report a problem, open an Issue on the SHOBS-P GitHub page and attach selftest_log.txt and .shobs_p.log, with a screenshot.

Previous: 1. Welcome · Next: 3. A tour of the window

3. A tour of the window

SHOBS-P has one main window. A sidebar on the left chooses the mode and the step; the page on the right is where you work; a status bar along the bottom tells you what just happened.

The sidebar

From top to bottom:

  1. The program name and a one-line description of the current mode.
  2. MODE: three buttons, Variables, Transits and Discovery. The active one has a ▶ in front of it.
  3. STEPS: five buttons, 1 Input, 2 Blink, 3 Calibrate, 4 Photometry, 5 Output. The page you are on has a ● in front of it.
  4. At the bottom: Credits and sources, and the version number (for example Version 2.3.0).

The three modes and their colors

Each mode colors the whole window, so you can always see which one you are in:

Mode Color Banner across the top
Variables navy VARIABLES · light curves, periods, color, and AAVSO reports
Transits green TRANSITS · transit fit (MCMC), nearby-binary check, and ExoFOP package
Discovery plum DISCOVERY · measure every star in the field and flag the ones that vary

To switch modes, click another mode button. Things to know:

  • Your work carries over. Calibrated frames and marked stars are kept, and you stay on the step you were on. The status bar says, for example, Transits mode. Calibrated frames and marked stars are kept.
  • You cannot switch while something is running. SHOBS-P says Wait for the current run to finish (or cancel it) before switching modes.
  • A series belongs to one mode. If a Variables series is open and you switch to Transits (or the other way), SHOBS-P asks first. Switching closes the series in the program; the file on disk is not changed. If it has unsaved nights, you are offered a save first.

What changes between modes:

  • Input: Variables and Transits have a Target box; Transits adds a Planet (Transits) box; Discovery has no target, only a field center when your headers do not give one.
  • Photometry: Variables and Transits mark a target, comparison stars and a check star, then Run photometry. Discovery has Scan field instead.
  • Output: Variables shows Output – Variability, Transits shows Output – Transit fit, Discovery shows Output – Field scan.

The five steps

Every mode works through the same five steps:

Step What you do there
1 Input Choose the folders of frames, name the target, check your site and optics.
2 Blink Play through the raw frames and reject bad ones (trails, cloud, poor guiding).
3 Calibrate Build master bias, dark and flat frames and calibrate the lights.
4 Photometry The image is solved; mark the stars and measure every frame.
5 Output Light curve, period search and reports; or the transit fit; or the field-scan results.

You can click any step at any time; none is ever locked. Some steps also move you on by themselves: a successful calibration goes straight to Photometry, and a finished photometry run goes straight to Output.

Most pages also have Back and Next: … buttons at the bottom.

Page titles and hints

Each page starts with its title in large type (for example Calibrate) and a short gray hint under it saying what the page does. Smaller gray hints sit next to many boxes, for example "west is negative" beside the longitude. Read them the first time; they answer most questions.

The status bar

The bottom of the window has two lines:

  • The status line shows the latest message: what just happened, or what to do next. Long messages are cut off here; the full text is always in the log (see below). After you save a report, a button may appear beside the message that opens the matching upload page (for example the AAVSO's). It goes away with the next message.
  • The credits line (dimmer) names the libraries and catalogs SHOBS-P is built on.

A new version. From 2.3.3, SHOBS-P asks GitHub at start whether a newer release is out (in the background, once a day at most; nothing about you is sent and nothing is downloaded). When one is, a light-blue underlined note sits at the right end of the status line, for example SHOBS-P 2.3.4 is available: … — click for details. Other messages never replace it. Click it: the New version window lists what is new, with Open release page (the zip and the user's manual are there; install it the way you installed this one, and your settings and series are kept) and Close. A release without a list of highlights opens its release page straight away. To stop the check, untick Check for new versions at start on the Credits page. For an email at each release, on GitHub choose Watch → Custom → Releases on the SHOBS-P page (or follow https://github.com/SHOBS-S/SHOBS-P/releases.atom).

The busy bar

Whenever SHOBS-P is working on something, a green bar slides back and forth at the left end of the status line, with words beside it saying what it is doing. This is the one sign that SHOBS-P is busy, on every page. For example:

  • Looking up TOI-2046 at SIMBAD and VSX… (a Star ID lookup on the Input page)
  • Looking up TrES-3's planets at the NASA Exoplanet Archive… (the same lookup in Transits mode, finding the star's planets)
  • Building calibration masters: median-combining 40 bias frames…
  • Solving: fetching catalogs: AAVSO ✓ APASS ✓ Tycho ✓ Gaia…
  • Photometry… 12 of 38, about 1:20 left
  • Stacking… 3 of 10 (stacking frames when the comps are weak, chapter 8)
  • Fitting the transit: MCMC step… 1,200 of 3,000

A job with several steps keeps the same bar and changes the words. When SHOBS-P can count, the count is in the words; the bar itself never fills up, it just keeps moving so you can see the program is alive. Jobs that take less than about a quarter of a second never show the bar at all.

When the job ends, the bar and its words go, and the status line says how it went. Most result lines start with a sign:

  • ✔ it worked, for example ✔ TOI-2046 from SIMBAD: RA … h, Dec …°;
  • ✖ it did not, for example ✖ TOI-2046: SIMBAD and VSX did not answer within 60 s — type RA/Dec, or Look up again.;
  • ⚠ it worked, but look at something.

The moving bar is always green; its track and the strip around it take the mode's color (navy, green or plum). (The Multi-night… window of Transits mode has its own bar in its lower left, chapter 17.)

The busy bar during a photometry run.

The log

The Calibrate page has a large dark log box, and the Photometry page has a smaller copy of it (Hide log / Show log). Every message SHOBS-P writes goes there, with more detail than the status line has room for. When something looks wrong, read the log first.

Warnings, cautions and questions

SHOBS-P tells you about problems in a few ways, from mild to serious:

  • Notes in the log start with "Note:" and are for your information.

  • Cautions start with "Caution:" or "⚠", and are printed in orange or red in the panels. They mean "this result may be affected; look at it". The program carries on.

  • Questions open a small window with Yes / No (sometimes Cancel). Each question explains both answers, for example:

    Yes: calibrate without the dark this time (bias only). … No: use these darks anyway. Cancel: stop, and pick darks closer to the lights' exposure on the Input page.

    Read the whole question before answering: the right answer depends on your night, and the question says what each choice does.

  • Errors open a window with only OK. Nothing was done; the message says what to fix.

While a job runs (calibration, photometry, a solve), the busy bar (above) says what it is doing and the cursor shows that the program is busy, but the window stays live. Starting a second job meanwhile gives Another step is still running. Wait for it to finish.

The Credits page

Credits and sources at the bottom of the sidebar opens a page listing every library, catalog, service and published method SHOBS-P uses. The quoted sentences are the acknowledgements each source asks for: please include them when you publish results made with SHOBS-P.

  • Save credits as text… saves the list as SHOBS-P_credits.txt.
  • Clear cached catalogs removes the catalog answers SHOBS-P has saved: the star catalogs for your fields and the last answer from each lookup (SIMBAD, VSX, the AAVSO sequence, the NASA Exoplanet Archive). It asks Yes/No first, and then says how many files were removed. Nothing else is touched (not your settings, site or data); the catalogs are fetched again the next time they are needed. See "When a service is down" in 24. Troubleshooting.
  • Check for new versions at start (on by default, from 2.3.3) turns the new-version check on or off (see "The status bar" above). It is saved at once.
  • Back returns to the step you came from.

What SHOBS-P remembers, and what it does not

SHOBS-P keeps a small settings file in your user folder (C:\Users\<you>\.shobs_p_settings.json on Windows, ~/.shobs_p_settings.json on Linux and the Mac). It is not in the program folder, so updates do not touch it.

Remembered between sessions:

  • the mode you used last;
  • your site: Latitude, Longitude and Elevation, once you type them;
  • your Telescope name, Telescope aperture (mm), Focal length (mm), Pixel size (µm) and Mount, and the Include scintillation checkbox;
  • your Observer code;
  • the linear ADU box on the Photometry page (your camera's linear limit, chapter 8);
  • the Blink Speed and the display Stretch on the Blink and Photometry pages;
  • whether the Photometry tips are shown, and where you last put the popped-out image window;
  • a monochrome Filter you picked by hand on the Calibrate page;
  • the Discovery period range and watch list (chapters 20 and 21);
  • header values you said No to (so SHOBS-P does not ask again);
  • the folder you last used for series files;
  • the last answer from each online lookup and the star catalogs for your fields (see Clear cached catalogs above), so a lookup or a solve still works while a service is down.

Not remembered between sessions (they start empty or at their defaults each time):

  • all five folders on the Input page, including Output;
  • the target and Chart ID;
  • Binning, the Bayer pattern and the Channel;
  • the aperture sizes, marked stars and rejected frames.

All of these except the folders are saved in a series file when you save a series, and come back when you load it (chapter 10). That is why the Input page says "Settings are saved with the series". The folders you must choose again each night.

To start over for another star without closing SHOBS-P, press New reduction on the Input page (chapter 5). It clears everything that belongs to the old star; your folders, site and optics stay.

A new install starts with Focal length (mm) and Pixel size (µm) empty, like the Site boxes. While they are empty, the lights' FITS headers fill them in. When you have not saved a focal length, pixel size or mount of your own yet, the values your own lights' headers give are remembered as yours. The log says so, for example Mount: … from the lights' headers (…), saved as yours (change it in the Optics box if that is wrong). A value you typed is never replaced this way.

Never stored anywhere unless you type it: your site. SHOBS-P ships with the Site boxes empty, and it never saves a site read from somebody else's frames as yours without asking you first. A site you type that is more than 50 km from your saved one (or 100 m higher or lower) is not saved either until you answer Yes to SHOBS-P's question (chapter 5). In the same way, the optics of a loaded series or of another telescope's frames are never saved as yours.

Previous: 2. Installing and starting · Next: 4. Before your first night

4. Before your first night

A few minutes of setup makes every later night quicker: tell SHOBS-P where you observe and with what, and take calibration frames it can use. This chapter covers what to set once, which camera settings matter, and how to organize your folders.

Enter your site

Your site is needed for airmass, for accurate (BJD) times, and for the scintillation part of the error bars. Without it, AAVSO reports show the airmass as na.

  1. Go to the Input page (1 Input in the sidebar).
  2. In the Site box, type:
    • Latitude (°, north +): decimal degrees, north positive (for example 40.0).
    • Longitude (°, east +): decimal degrees, east positive, west negative. Observers in the Americas type a negative number (for example -100.0).
    • Elevation (m): meters above sea level. It is used in exoplanet reports and for scintillation.
    • Time zone: the site's time zone, as a name such as America/New_York or Europe/Madrid. It starts as this computer's own zone, so if the telescope is where the computer is, leave it alone. Change it when the site is somewhere else (a remote or hosted telescope). Type part of a city name (for example Phoenix) to narrow the list, then pick the zone.
  3. That's all. SHOBS-P remembers these values on this computer from now on.

Every local time SHOBS-P shows (Blink, the Output page, the transit planning windows, the Trim boxes) is the site's clock time in this zone, with that night's daylight saving, and is labeled with its offset from UTC, for example Local time (UTC−4) before the US change on 1 Nov 2026 and Local time (UTC−5) after it. Times you type (After, End by, Trim from and to) are read as the site's clock too. UTC, JD and BJD times in reports and files do not depend on the time zone.

Tip: If your capture program writes the site into the FITS headers (keywords such as SITELAT and SITELONG), SHOBS-P offers to fill the Site box from them when you press Scan folders. It always asks before saving a header's site as yours, because the frames may come from someone else's telescope.

Enter your optics

In the Optics box on the Input page:

Box What to type Why it matters
Telescope Your telescope's name, for example "0.25 m Newtonian". Goes into reports. Leave it blank to use the FITS header's name (often the mount's name, so typing your own is better). Remembered.
Telescope aperture (mm) The clear aperture in millimeters. Used for the scintillation error. Remembered, and remembered per telescope (see below).
Focal length (mm) The effective focal length, with any reducer. With the pixel size, gives the plate scale the solve needs. Remembered.
Pixel size (µm) Your camera's pixel size. As above. Remembered.
Binning 1, 2 or 4. SHOBS-P's own binning (see below). Usually 1.
Mount German equatorial or Alt-az. Meridian flips, pier sides and the flats-angle check depend on it (see below). Remembered.

Frames from more than one telescope. The aperture is a property of the telescope, not of the night. When the lights' header does not give the aperture, SHOBS-P remembers which aperture you measured each telescope with (it knows a telescope by the header's TELESCOP, INSTRUME and focal length). When you scan lights:

  • from a telescope you measured before with another aperture, it asks: "… Last time you measured frames from that telescope the Telescope aperture was 203 mm; the box says 152 mm. Use 203 mm for these frames?"
  • from another telescope than the last frames you measured, it asks whether the box's value is right: "These lights come from …, another telescope than the last frames you measured (…). The Telescope aperture box still says 152 mm from those frames. Is 152 mm right for this telescope?" No puts the cursor in the box.

It only asks: the box changes only when you answer Yes or type a new value, and the same question is not asked again for the same telescope and value. Each night records the box's aperture when it was measured (the settings file's Telescope aperture box when the night was measured line). The error bars use the box's value when the report is saved; the settings file's Scintillation line says which.

Leave Include scintillation (atmospheric twinkling) in the error bars ticked. It makes the error bars match the real scatter, especially for bright stars and short exposures.

Tip: A new install starts with Focal length (mm) and Pixel size (µm) empty. If your capture program writes FOCALLEN and XPIXSZ into the headers, Scan folders fills them in while the boxes are empty, and asks if your own values differ from the header's. If your headers don't have them, type them before you solve: with neither the boxes nor a plate solution in the header, the solve stops and says the boxes are empty. Once set, both are remembered. Wrong values are the most common reason a solve fails, so check them once. Optics read from a loaded series or from another telescope's frames are never saved as yours.

About Binning. Leave your camera unbinned (bin 1) when you capture, and use this box if you want bigger pixels. SHOBS-P bins only pixels of the same color, so the colors of an OSC camera stay separate. Some capture programs mix red, green and blue when they bin a color sensor; SHOBS-P notes this in the log when it sees camera-binned color frames. (MicroObservatory frames come binned 2×2 by design, and a monochrome camera has no colors to mix, so neither gets the note.)

Your camera's linear limit

Every camera's response bends before it saturates: above a certain level, a pixel collects fewer counts than the light deserves, so a bright star reads too faint. On some cameras this starts well below full scale. If you know your camera's linear limit (from its data sheet, or from a linearity test), type it in raw ADU in the linear ADU box beside raw sat ADU on the Photometry page. SHOBS-P remembers it.

With the box filled, SHOBS-P checks each star's brightest raw pixel against it: when you pick a comparison star or the target (chapter 8), when it measures every frame (the log counts the frames over the limit for each star), and in Comp health (chapter 11). Leave the box empty if you don't know the limit; then only the saturation level is checked, as before.

Your observer code

If you report to the AAVSO, type your observer code in Observer code (the AAVSO report box on the Input page). SHOBS-P remembers it between sessions, and it is also saved in every series file.

Color (OSC) or monochrome camera

Color (one-shot-color) cameras

SHOBS-P needs to know the color pattern of your sensor (the "Bayer pattern": RGGB, BGGR, GRBG or GBRG).

  • Most capture programs write it into the FITS header (BAYERPAT). SHOBS-P reads it when you press Scan folders and the status bar says, for example, Bayer pattern from header: RGGB.
  • If your headers don't say, choose it on the Calibrate page under Bayer pattern. Your camera's manual or your capture program's settings give it.

You then choose which color to measure with Channel on the Calibrate page:

Channel AAVSO filter code Comparison-star magnitudes
green (the normal choice) TG V
red (suits red stars, such as red dwarfs) TR R
blue TB B
luminance (all colors together) CV V

Monochrome cameras

Set Bayer pattern to MONO on the Calibrate page. SHOBS-P does this by itself when it finds no color mask in your raw flats. Without flats, it looks at the sky background of the lights instead. That test needs either bias or dark frames on the Input page (they tell it the camera's pedestal) or a fairly bright sky. If it still cannot tell, SHOBS-P asks once for those lights: Color or monochrome camera?, with the buttons Color camera and Monochrome camera. Your answer counts as a pattern you picked by hand.

With MONO, the Channel box becomes Filter. SHOBS-P reads the filter from the FITS FILTER keyword, so with a filter wheel each night is labeled correctly by itself. If your headers have no filter, choose it in the Filter box (SHOBS-P remembers that choice). The choices are:

  • V (Johnson V), B (Johnson B), R (Cousins R)
  • Clear / L (CV)
  • Green imaging (TG), Red imaging (TR), Blue imaging (TB)
  • Sloan g′ (SG), Sloan r′ (SR), Sloan i′ (SI), Sloan z′ (SZ), Sloan u′ (SU)

Sloan filters. SHOBS-P understands the usual ways capture programs spell Sloan filters in the header, such as g', gp, sdss g, Sloan g or Astrodon Sloan g'. The comparison stars then get magnitudes in the same band: from the AAVSO sequence when it has that band, otherwise APASS or Gaia. Sloan u′ has few catalog stars, so expect fewer comparison stars to choose from.

Color images that are already debayered

Some programs save lights that are already debayered and calibrated: color images with three channels (red, green and blue). SHOBS-P finds these when you press Scan folders and asks once per lights folder which channel to measure: Green (TG) (closest to V; the usual choice), Red (TR), Blue (TB) or Luminance (CV) (all three combined, as a clear filter).

For these lights SHOBS-P:

  • sets Bayer pattern to MONO and does not debayer them again;
  • treats them as pre-calibrated: the bias, dark and flat entries are not used for them;
  • shows the channel in the Filter box on the Calibrate page. To measure another channel, pick Green imaging (TG), Red imaging (TR), Blue imaging (TB) or Clear / L (CV) there. This cannot be changed while a step is running.

Each night of a series remembers its channel, and SHOBS-P tells you when you add a night measured in another channel. Color (B−V) is not measured on these images, because one channel is measured per run.

Filters SHOBS-P cannot use. Johnson/Cousins I, H-alpha and other narrowband filters have no catalog magnitudes here. Frames taken through them are reported as clear (CV) with V comparison magnitudes, and the log says so.

Calibration frames

SHOBS-P uses bias, darks, dark flats and flats. Each is optional (a missing one is skipped, and the log says so), but a good flat matters most for precise photometry.

Bias frames

  • The shortest exposure your camera allows, with the same gain, offset and temperature as your lights.
  • 20 to 50 frames is plenty.

Dark frames

  • The same exposure as your lights, at the same gain, offset and sensor temperature.
  • SHOBS-P scales a dark to the lights' exposure if it must, but if the darks are more than 3 times shorter or longer than the lights, it asks whether to leave the dark out. Scaling a dark up also scales up its noise.
  • The Input page tells you as soon as you choose the folders when the darks' exposure differs from the lights', so you can pick the right darks before you calibrate (chapter 5).
  • 10 to 30 frames.

Flat frames

  • Taken through the same optics, the same filter, the same focus, and with the camera in the same rotation on the telescope as the lights.
  • Exposed to roughly a third to half of full scale, not saturated.
  • 20 to 50 frames. You do not need separate darks for the flats if you have bias frames: SHOBS-P takes the bias (and the dark, scaled to the flats' exposure) out of each flat before combining them.
  • Fresh is best. Dust moves. SHOBS-P adds a caution to the log when a saved master flat is more than 30 days from the lights.

Dark flats (optional)

  • Darks taken at the flats' exposure (same gain, offset and temperature), with the telescope covered.
  • Many capture programs offer them instead of bias frames. Put them in the Dark flats row on the Input page (chapter 5): SHOBS-P takes them off the flats, and with no bias frames they also stand in for the bias (the camera's offset), so the Bias row can stay empty.
  • With no bias and no dark flats, a dark scaled to another exposure scales the camera's offset too, and the calibration is off. SHOBS-P warns about this. Either add bias frames or dark flats, or take darks at the lights' exposure.
  • 10 to 30 frames.

Flats and the camera's rotation

Dust shadows and vignetting from the optics in front of the camera turn with the camera. A flat taken with the camera turned to another angle corrects the wrong pattern, often by about 1%: enough to fake or hide a shallow transit.

So take flats with the camera in the same rotation as the lights. If your capture program writes the rotator angle into the header (ROTATOR or a similar keyword), SHOBS-P checks this for you:

  • If the flats and lights were taken at angles more than 2° apart, it asks before calibrating.
  • If the Flat folder mixes flats from different angles, it asks too, because one master flat built from all of them matches none of them exactly.

A meridian flip does not need new flats. On a German equatorial mount, the rotator reading changes by 180° at the flip, but the camera has not turned on the telescope. SHOBS-P compares rotator angles modulo 180°, so readings of 30° and 210° are the same camera position, and the same flats fit both sides of the flip. You only need new flats when the camera or rotator is actually turned to a new angle on the telescope.

Caution: If your rotator really turns the camera 180° during the night (rather than the mount flipping), the flats fit only one side. SHOBS-P tells a flip from a turn using the PIERSIDE keyword when your headers have it.

Alt-az mounts and derotators

An alt-az mount never flips, so set Mount to Alt-az in the Optics box. SHOBS-P then shows no flip segments, lets the frame alignment follow the field rotation, and uses the alt-az rules for flats:

  • Without a derotator, the rotator reading is the camera's angle on the telescope. Readings 180° apart are a real turn, not a flip. If the flats and lights are more than 5° apart, SHOBS-P asks before calibrating.
  • With a derotator, the camera turns against the optics all night anyway. Flats then fully correct dust on the filter, window and sensor whatever their angle, so SHOBS-P writes one caution in the log and never asks.

SHOBS-P also reads the mount from the lights' headers. It trusts them only with clear evidence (for example a PIERSIDE that changes sides, a mount-type keyword, or a derotator's smooth drift); otherwise the Mount box decides. The hint under the box says what the headers show, and the log says which mount was used.

Saved masters

The first time you calibrate, SHOBS-P builds a master bias, dark and flat and saves each in a subfolder called SHOBS-P masters inside the bias, dark or flat folder. The next time you use the same folder, it reuses the master instead of stacking the frames again, as long as the frames, the binning and the bias and dark they were built with have not changed. This saves a lot of time when you use one set of darks or flats for many nights.

  • To force a fresh stack, tick Rebuild masters (ignore saved ones) on the Calibrate page.
  • You can also point a calibration box at one saved master file with Master file… on the Input page.
  • Masters made by other programs (PixInsight and others) are not accepted, because how they were scaled and normalized is not known. Give SHOBS-P the raw frames instead.

A folder layout that works well

SHOBS-P reads one folder per kind of frame. Any layout works, but this one keeps things simple:

C:\Astro\
  Calibration\
    Bias_gain100\
    Darks_gain100_-10C_120s\
    Darks_gain100_-10C_60s\
  SZ Lyn\
    2026-10-14\
      Lights\
      Flats\
    2026-10-21\
      Lights\
      Flats\
    Reports\
  TrES-3 b\
    2026-09-04\
      Lights\
      Flats\
    Reports\

Why this works:

  • Bias and darks in a shared Calibration folder, named by what makes them match (gain, temperature, exposure). They don't change from night to night, so one set serves many nights and its saved master is reused.
  • Flats go with the night they were taken. Flats record that night's focus, dust and camera angle, so each night's flats sit in a Flats folder next to its Lights. The master flat SHOBS-P builds is saved in that folder's SHOBS-P masters subfolder, so it stays with the night too. If a night has no usable flats of its own, point the Flats box at another night's Flats folder taken at the same camera angle (SHOBS-P checks the angle and the flats' age and tells you if they don't match).
  • One night, one field, one Lights folder. Keep each night's lights of one target in their own folder. SHOBS-P can sort out a folder that holds two fields (it asks), but it is quicker not to need that.
  • Calibration frames that end up in a Lights folder are never measured as lights, so a capture program that saves everything together still works. When the matching Bias, Dark or Flat box is empty, SHOBS-P offers to use them (chapter 7). Dark-flats are never taken as flats.
  • A Reports folder per target to use as the Output folder: every Save button then writes there without asking, and never overwrites a file.

Previous: 3. A tour of the window · Next: 5. Input

5. Input

The Input page is where every night starts. You tell SHOBS-P where the frames are, which star (or planet) you observed, and where reports should go. When you press Scan folders, SHOBS-P reads your FITS headers and checks that everything fits together.

The page at a glance

From top to bottom:

  • The series line, with New reduction, Load series… and Close series on the right.
  • Frames: six rows, Bias, Dark, Dark flats, Flat, Lights and Output, with hint lines under them.
  • On the left: Target (Variables and Transits), Planet (Transits) (Transits only), or Field center (only needed when the headers do not say) (Discovery, only when needed).
  • On the right: AAVSO report, Site and Optics.
  • At the bottom: Scan folders, Try it with sample data… and Next: Blink.

Choose the folders

Each row of the Frames box has a path box, a Browse button, a ✕ button that empties the row, and a count on the right.

To choose your frames:

  1. Press Browse on the Bias row and pick the folder of bias frames.
  2. Do the same for Dark, Flat and Lights, and for Dark flats if you took them (see below).
  3. Optionally, choose an Output folder (see below).
  4. Press Scan folders.

You can also type or paste a path into the box. The count updates by itself a moment after you stop typing (and at once when you press Enter or leave the box), just as after Browse. While the path does not name an existing folder yet, the count says no such folder.

What the counts mean:

  • 51 files: 51 frames SHOBS-P can read.
  • 51 files + master: the folder also holds a master SHOBS-P built earlier; it will be reused if the frames have not changed.
  • master file: the row points at one master file (see below).
  • On the Lights row after scanning: (+3 cal skipped) means three bias, dark or flat frames were found among the lights and are not measured as lights (when the matching Bias, Dark or Flat row is empty, Calibrate offers to use them; chapter 7); (12 set aside: another field) means frames of another field were set aside (see "The checks SHOBS-P makes" below).

SHOBS-P reads FITS (.fits, .fit, .fts, also gzip and fpack-compressed) and PixInsight XISF files. If a folder holds only files it cannot read, such as camera RAW or TIFF, the log says so.

Darks of another exposure. When the darks' exposure differs from the lights', an orange line under the Frames box says so as soon as you choose the folders, for example ⚠ Darks 60 s, lights 120 s: the master dark is scaled ×2 to the lights' exposure. Fine for a cooled camera; 120 s darks are better. When the dark would be scaled more than 3 times, the line is red (✖) and Calibrate will ask about it. Once you have chosen at Calibrate to scale these darks, the line turns gray (✔).

Any of Bias, Dark, Dark flats and Flat may be left empty. That step is simply skipped. Press ✕ to empty a row; the status bar says, for example, Dark folder cleared. Calibration will skip it.

Dark flats. The Dark flats row sits between Dark and Flat and works like the others (Browse, Master file…, ✕ and a file count). It is optional, like Bias. A hint line under the Frames box says: "Dark flats (optional, like Bias): darks taken at the flats' exposure. They are taken off the flats, and with no bias they also stand in for it, so Bias can stay empty. Leave it empty if you have none." If you have bias, darks and flats, leave it empty: nothing changes. Like the other frame folders it starts empty every time SHOBS-P starts, and it is not saved in a series. Chapter 7 says how the dark flats are used.

Two more lines can appear in the same place as the dark-exposure line:

  • ⚠ No bias and no dark flats: darks can't be scaled correctly without bias or dark flats (110 s darks to 55 s lights scales the camera's offset too). Add bias frames or dark flats, or use darks at the lights' exposure.
  • ⚠ Dark flats 2 s, flats 0.5 s: dark flats should match the flats' exposure. Take dark flats at 0.5 s.

Using a saved master. Bias, Dark, Dark flats and Flat each have a Master file… button. Use it to pick one master file that SHOBS-P built on an earlier night (they are in the SHOBS-P masters subfolder of your calibration folders). Masters from other programs are refused with a message explaining why.

Tip: Folders are not remembered between sessions. If you reduce the same target often, load its series (chapter 10) instead: it brings back most of the setup.

The Output folder

The Output row chooses where every Save button writes: reports, light curves, plots and packages.

  • With an Output folder, each Save writes straight into it, without a dialog. Files already there are never overwritten: a new copy gets _2, _3 and so on. The status bar says Save buttons now write into … when you choose it.
  • Without one, every Save asks where to put the file.

Name the target

In Variables and Transits mode, the Target box names the star you observed.

  1. Click in Star ID and type the star's name, for example SZ Lyn or HD 219134.
  2. Press Enter (or just click somewhere else). SHOBS-P looks the star up in SIMBAD and the AAVSO's VSX.
  3. RA (hours) and Dec (degrees) fill in, with the same position in hours-minutes-seconds beside them. The status bar shows where they came from and, for a known variable, its VSX period and type, for example ✔ SZ Lyn from SIMBAD: RA … h, Dec …° ….

Things to know:

  • The lookup happens only when you press Enter or leave the box, not as you type.
  • While it runs, the busy bar at the bottom of the window says Looking up SZ Lyn at SIMBAD and VSX… (chapter 3, "The busy bar"). In Transits mode it goes on to the star's planets at the NASA Exoplanet Archive. If SIMBAD and VSX have not answered within 60 seconds, a red line under RA/Dec says so, for example ✖ SZ Lyn: the SIMBAD and VSX did not answer within 60 s — type RA/Dec, or Look up again. Type RA and Dec yourself, or press Look up again later. (If the answer still comes in after that, it is used.)
  • If the name is only spelled a little differently from the AAVSO's version (spaces or capitals), an orange line appears, such as AAVSO spells this "HD 219134", with a Use it button. Using the AAVSO spelling keeps your reports matching the AAVSO's records.
  • Look up again repeats the lookup, for example after the internet was down.
  • If the lookup fails, the status bar says Could not look up … You can type RA and Dec yourself, or leave them empty and let the plate solution fill them in later.
  • RA is in decimal hours and Dec in decimal degrees (J2000).

Changing to another target. Press New reduction on the series line. It starts over for another star, as if you had just started SHOBS-P: the open series is closed (you are offered a save first), and the target, planet, comparison stars, check star, watch stars, Use comps choice, VSX period and rejected frames are cleared. The Notes box on the Output page goes back to its default, OSC reduced; not transformed (or Monochrome camera; not transformed), so the old star's notes cannot end up in the new star's report; if you had typed your own notes, the log lists "the Output page notes" among what was cleared. The last run's line on the Photometry page ("Photometry done in …") is cleared too. The frame folders, site and optics stay. SHOBS-P asks first: Start a new reduction? Then type the new name and press Enter.

This matters: without it, one star's comparison stars (or its Use comps choice) could be applied to another star's night. Clear target still empties only the Star ID, RA and Dec and the comparison and check stars.

Transits mode: the planet

In Transits mode, a Planet (Transits) box appears under the Target. Type the planet's name under any of its names (for example TrES-3 b, TOI-2046 b or KELT-16 b) and press Enter. SHOBS-P fills in the period, the transit time, duration, depth and shape from the NASA Exoplanet Archive, and also the host star's name and position. While it looks the planet up, the busy bar says, for example, Looking up planet K2-29 b at the NASA Exoplanet Archive…; the boxes stay as they are until the values come. You can also type the host star in Star ID and press Enter: the busy bar then looks up the star, its planets and the planet in turn, for example Looking up TrES-3's planets at the NASA Exoplanet Archive…. Chapter 16 covers this box in detail.

If the planet orbits another star than the one on the page and a series is open, SHOBS-P asks whether to start a new reduction. No keeps the old star and its series; check the planet's name.

Discovery mode: the field center

Discovery mode has no target. SHOBS-P finds the field from the plate solution or pointing in your FITS headers. Only if your lights have neither does a Field center (only needed when the headers do not say) box appear. Type roughly where the frames point; a few arcminutes off is fine.

The AAVSO report box

  • Observer code: your AAVSO observer code. It goes into every AAVSO report. It is remembered between sessions, and saved with a series too.
  • Chart ID: the AAVSO chart (sequence) your comparison stars come from. You usually don't need to type it: when you pick an AAVSO sequence star as a comparison or check star, SHOBS-P fills it in. When none of your comparison or check stars are AAVSO sequence stars, it is na.

Site and Optics

These boxes are described in chapter 4, including the Mount choice. They are usually filled already: the site, time zone, telescope, focal length, pixel size and mount are remembered, and Scan folders fills empty boxes from your FITS headers. When you have not saved a focal length, pixel size or mount yet, the values from your own lights' headers are remembered as yours (never those of someone else's frames, MicroObservatory's or a loaded series).

Time zone (in the Site box) is the site's time zone, for example America/New_York. Every local time in SHOBS-P uses it, labeled like Local time (UTC−5). Type part of a name (Phoenix, Warsaw) to narrow the list, then pick one. A name SHOBS-P does not know is not used: the status bar says so, and this computer's zone is used until you pick one from the list. A series file keeps its site's time zone; a series saved before version 2.2.21 has none, so the box keeps the zone it already shows (the log says so).

Press Scan folders

Scan folders reads your folders and the first light's FITS header. Always press it after choosing your folders. It is what:

  • counts the frames and skips calibration frames found among the lights;
  • reads the Bayer pattern (or detects a monochrome camera from the flats or the lights' sky) and the filter;
  • finds color images that are already debayered and asks which channel to measure;
  • reads the mount from the headers when they make it clear;
  • fills empty Site and Optics boxes from the header;
  • runs the checks below.

When it is done, the status bar says Folders scanned. (or what it found).

Caution: Next: Blink does not scan. If you skip Scan folders, the checks below are not made until you calibrate: from 2.3.2, Build masters and calibrate runs them first when the Lights folder was not scanned (after a restart, say), and the log says so.

The checks SHOBS-P makes on your headers

Most nights, none of these appear. When one does, read the question; each one says what Yes and No do.

Frames of two fields in one folder. If the Lights folder holds frames pointing at more than one place, SHOBS-P lists them, for example "40 frames at … (OBJECT SZ Lyn)" and "12 frames at …", and asks whether to use only the frames near your target. Yes sets the others aside as rejected frames; you can bring any of them back on the Blink page.

The frames are another star. If the lights point well away from the target on the Input page (at least a degree, and more than the frame's own size), SHOBS-P asks as soon as you choose the Lights folder, for example:

These frames are DY Peg, not BL Cam. … Start a new reduction? …

Yes starts a new reduction (see "Changing to another target" above): the open series is closed (with a save offered), the old star's target, planet, comps, check and watch stars are cleared, the Output page notes go back to their default, and the star named in the frames' OBJECT keyword is looked up. Rejected frames of the new Lights folder are kept. Answer No if you meant it (for example, the target is near the edge of a wide field); you will not be asked again for that pair. If OBJECT names the Input page's own star but the frames point elsewhere, SHOBS-P asks nothing and the log suggests checking the RA and Dec instead.

A mislabeled OBJECT. Capture programs often write a plan name or an old target name into OBJECT. If the frames point at your target anyway, SHOBS-P only notes in the log that "the OBJECT keyword looks mislabeled" and carries on. If the OBJECT names a different star in another place, it asks the same These frames are …, not … question (the default answer is No).

Frames from another site. If the header's site is more than 50 km from yours, SHOBS-P asks whether to use the site and optics in the frames for this night. This is handy for remote telescopes or a friend's data. Yes borrows their setup; your own comes back automatically the next time you scan a folder of your own lights. FITS headers do not say the site's time zone, so the log reminds you to check the Time zone box: pick the frames' site zone there so their local times are right. When the header has no elevation either, the status line adds Elevation not in the header — type it on the Input page (from 2.3.3): the scintillation error and the AAVSO exoplanet report use it, and it must be typed again for each run of those frames.

Header values that differ from yours. If the header's latitude, longitude, elevation, aperture, focal length or pixel size differs from your boxes, SHOBS-P lists the differences and asks Use the header values? No keeps yours, and SHOBS-P will not ask again about those same values.

Your Site boxes are empty. If the header has a site and yours is empty, SHOBS-P asks Is that your own site? Yes saves it as yours; No uses it for these frames only.

A site you typed that is not your saved one. When you type a site into the Site boxes for someone else's frames (a friend's data, or frames whose headers have no site), SHOBS-P does not save it while you type. When you leave the box (or press Scan folders, Calibrate or Run photometry) and the typed site is more than 50 km from your saved site, or its elevation is more than 100 m off, SHOBS-P asks:

The Site boxes now hold latitude …, longitude … (… km from your saved site; yours is …).

Is this your own site from now on? …

Yes saves it, with the Optics boxes, as your setup (SHOBS-SCHED reads the same setup). No uses it for these frames only: your saved site, optics and camera settings are kept, and nothing you type into the Site or Optics boxes is saved while the typed site is in use. If you saved an Optics value earlier in the same session (for example the other telescope's aperture before its site), it goes back too, and the log names it. The typed site stays in use for that Lights folder when its header has no site; your own setup comes back the next time you scan a folder of your own lights. Small changes at your own site (a corrected elevation, a fourth decimal) are saved without a question.

Monochrome camera. If there is no Bayer keyword and the raw flats show no color mask, the status bar says Monochrome camera found (no color mask in the flat): Bayer pattern MONO. Without flats, SHOBS-P tests the lights' sky background instead (it needs bias or dark frames, or a bright sky); then the status bar says Monochrome camera found (no color mask in the lights' sky background): Bayer pattern MONO. If neither can tell, SHOBS-P asks once for these lights: Color or monochrome camera? If your camera really is a color camera, choose the pattern on the Calibrate page.

Color images, already debayered. If the lights have three color channels, SHOBS-P asks which channel to measure: Green (TG), Red (TR), Blue (TB) or Luminance (CV). It asks once per lights folder; the status bar then says, for example, 40 lights: color images, green (TG) channel measured. These lights are not debayered or calibrated again (chapter 4).

The mount. If the headers clearly show the mount (for example a meridian flip, or a derotator), that mount is used for these lights, and the hint under Mount says so. If it differs from your Mount box, the hint suggests changing the box when this is your own telescope.

MicroObservatory frames are recognized, and SHOBS-P offers to use that telescope's known setup for them. Newer MicroObservatory files have neither MOBS_ in the name nor a MicroObservatory keyword; SHOBS-P recognizes them by their site, focal length and frame size. Their site and optics are never saved as yours. Their time zone is set to America/Phoenix (Arizona, no daylight saving), and yours comes back with your own setup.

Binning. SHOBS-P never takes Binning from the header: it is SHOBS-P's own setting. When your camera itself binned the color frames, the log notes it after calibration (chapter 7). Changing Binning after calibrating means calibrating again; the masters must match.

The series line

The line under the page title says which series the night will join:

  • No series loaded — this run will be a single night. To add a night to a series, Load series… before running.
  • or the name of the open series, how many nights it has, and its last date.

Load series… opens a series file you saved before. Close series puts the open series aside (the file on disk is not changed; you are offered a save first if it has unsaved changes). Chapter 10 covers series in full.

Closing the SHOBS-P window asks the same way, but only when a series file has unsaved changes or a series of two or more nights has never been saved: Yes saves first, No closes without saving, Cancel keeps SHOBS-P open.

Tip: To add tonight to an existing series, load the series before you run photometry. After the run, SHOBS-P asks whether to add the night.

Try it with sample data…

Try it with sample data… writes a small practice night into an empty folder you choose and fills in every box. See chapter 2.

Previous: 4. Before your first night · Next: 6. Blink

6. Blink

Blink shows your raw frames one after another, like a flip book, so you can spot and reject the bad ones: satellite and aircraft trails, passing cloud, frames where the guiding slipped. Rejected frames are left on disk; SHOBS-P just skips them.

Blink is optional. SHOBS-P also flags cloud and saturation by itself during photometry. But a minute here catches trails and bad guiding, which the flags may not.

The page at a glance

  • Lights / Flats: which folder to show. Lights is the usual choice.
  • Previous, Next: one frame back or forward.
  • Blink, Stop: play the frames automatically, and stop.
  • Speed: how long each frame stays on screen: 0.25 s/frame, 0.5 s/frame, 1 s/frame or 2 s/frame.
  • Reject, Keep: reject the frame on screen, or bring it back.
  • The Stretch row: how bright the picture looks (display only).
  • The frame line, the rejected-frames line, and the picture.

The frames shown are raw: not calibrated. Hot pixels, gradients and dust shadows are normal here.

Look through the frames

  1. Go to 2 Blink in the sidebar (or press Next: Blink on the Input page). The first frame appears.
  2. Press Blink to play the frames. Press Blink again to pause, or Stop to stop.
  3. Use Previous and Next to step one frame at a time. At the end, the list wraps round to the start.

SHOBS-P reads frames ahead in the background, so after the first pass, blinking is quick.

The line above the picture describes the frame on screen, for example:

Frame 222  21:53:12 UTC−4   keep   (61 of 300 in the list, 3 rejected)   Light_SZ Lyn_120s_0222.fit  exp 120.0s
  • Frame 222 is the frame's own number, taken from the digits at the end of its file name. The same number is used in the log and in the light-curve file, so you can find a frame again later.
  • 21:53:12 UTC−4 is the site's local time the exposure started, with its offset from UTC (the site's time zone is set in the Site box on the Input page, chapter 4).
  • keep or REJECTED shows whether the frame will be used.
  • (61 of 300 in the list, …) is its place in the list and how many frames are rejected so far.

A rejected frame's title over the picture turns red: REJECTED — Frame 222 …

Reject a frame

  1. Stop on the bad frame (pause with Blink, or use Previous / Next).
  2. Press Reject.

The status bar confirms it, for example Rejected frame 222 (21:53:12): Light_SZ Lyn_120s_0222.fit. Pressing Reject again on a frame that is already rejected changes nothing; it only reminds you that Keep brings it back.

What makes a frame worth rejecting:

  • a trail across or near your target, comparison or check stars;
  • obvious cloud: the stars fade or the background brightens suddenly;
  • elongated or doubled stars from a guiding error or wind;
  • a frame taken while the mount was still settling, for example right after a meridian flip.

Don't reject frames just because the star looks a little fainter; that may be the variation you are measuring.

Tip: You may reject the frame you marked the stars on (for example all the frames before a meridian flip). The next photometry run then makes the first kept frame its reference and carries the markers over to it, and the log says so: Reference: the frame the stars were marked on (…) is rejected, so the first kept frame (…) is the reference; the markers were carried over to it…

If you press Calibrate again after rejecting frames, the first kept frame becomes the reference right away. From 2.3.4 the markers follow it, on either side of a meridian flip, and the log says Reference: frame … replaces … (frames rejected in Blink); markers carried over to it …. If the two frames cannot be lined up, the markers are taken off but the comp and check names and magnitudes stay, and the solve places them again.

When the new reference is on the other side of the meridian flip, the whole image is turned 180° from what you saw before, so the stars (and their markers) sit in different places on the screen. They are still on the same stars: the log, and the line under the step bar, say Frame … is on the other side of the meridian flip: the image is turned 180° from before, and the markers moved with their stars. The red variable-star marks move the same way. While the new frame is being solved, the step line says Solving the image… wait for it to finish before Run.

Bring a frame back

There are two ways:

  • The frame is on screen: press Keep. The status bar says Kept frame 222 (…): … is used again.
  • From the list: the line Rejected frames (3): 63, 64, 65 lists every rejected frame by number. On the right, pick a number in the Rejected frame box, then:
    • Show jumps to that frame so you can look at it again;
    • Keep this one brings it back without showing it.

Frames set aside on the Input page (frames of another field) appear in the same list and can be brought back the same way.

Change how the picture looks

The Stretch row changes only the display. Measurements always use the original pixels.

  • Stretch: linear, log or asinh. log and asinh show faint stars and the background better.
  • black: the percentage of pixels shown as black. Raise it to darken the background.
  • white: the percentage below which pixels are not yet pure white. Lower it to brighten faint stars.
  • Reset: back to linear, black 5%, white 99.5%.

The stretch is remembered, separately for the Blink and Photometry pages.

Blink the flats

Choose Flats at the left of the tool row to look through the flat frames the same way. This is a quick way to find a flat with a shadow (a hand, a cable) or one taken at the wrong brightness. Flats you reject here are left out of the master flat. The list then reads Rejected flats ….

Dark flats saved in the Flat folder are not flats, so this view leaves them out, and the status line says, for example, "4 dark flat(s) in the Flat folder are not shown (they are not flats)." (see chapter 7).

Things to know

  • Rejections are not saved on their own. They are kept while SHOBS-P is open, and they are saved with a series (chapter 10). If you close SHOBS-P without saving a series, they are gone next time.
  • The Blink speed and stretch are remembered between sessions.
  • Blink always shows what is in the folder now. If you change the Lights folder on the Input page, Blink loads the new list next time you open it.
  • Rejected frames belong to their folder. When you choose another Lights or Flat folder, the frames you rejected in the old one are forgotten, so a new night never starts with another night's rejections. New reduction on the Input page clears every rejected frame.

Previous: 5. Input · Next: 7. Calibrate

7. Calibrate

Calibration removes the camera's own signature from your lights: the bias pedestal, dark current, and the uneven illumination and dust shadows that the flat records. SHOBS-P builds a master bias, dark, dark flat and flat (from the rows you filled in), calibrates the first light so you can see it, and then moves on to the Photometry page by itself.

Calibrate a night

  1. Go to 3 Calibrate in the sidebar (or press Next: Calibrate on the Blink page).

  2. Check the Camera and channel box:

    • Color camera: Bayer pattern shows your sensor's pattern (RGGB, BGGR, GRBG or GBRG), and Channel is usually green.
    • Monochrome camera: Bayer pattern shows MONO, and Filter shows the filter (from the FITS header, or the one you chose).
    • Color images that are already debayered (three channels): Bayer pattern shows MONO, and Filter shows the channel you chose when you scanned the folders (see "Already-debayered color images" below).

    The two gray lines to the right say what will be reported, for example "AAVSO filter code TG" and "Green channel, V comps". Chapter 4 explains the choices.

  3. Press Build masters and calibrate.

  4. Watch the log, and the busy bar at the bottom of the window (chapter 3). Its words follow the steps: Building calibration masters…, then for example Building calibration masters: median-combining 40 bias frames… (and the same for the darks and flats), then Calibrating the first light…. Building masters from scratch can take a few minutes for a hundred full frames; reusing saved masters takes seconds. The window stays usable meanwhile.

  5. When it is done, SHOBS-P switches to the Photometry page by itself and starts solving the image. The status bar says Calibrated and debayered. The image is being solved; …

You normally do not need Next: Photometry: a successful calibration takes you there.

To stop a calibration, press Cancel. SHOBS-P stops after the current frame, and any masters you had before are left unchanged.

What happens

  1. The plan. Before the masters are built, one log line says how the lights and flats will be calibrated, for example "Calibration: lights = light − dark (the darks match the lights' exposure, so no bias is needed); flats = flat − dark flat." Indented notes and ⚠ warnings may follow it (see "Dark flats" below).
  2. Masters. For each of bias, dark, dark flat and flat, SHOBS-P either reuses the master it saved last time, or median-combines the raw frames into a new one and saves it in the SHOBS-P masters subfolder of that folder. The log says which, for example "Using saved master flat …" or "Median-combining 30 flat frames".
  3. Each light is then calibrated as (light − bias − dark) / flat, with the dark scaled to the light's exposure. Without bias frames, the dark flats take the bias's place (below).
  4. The first light is calibrated and split into its colors (for a color camera), and shown on the Photometry page. The other lights are calibrated the same way, one by one, when you run photometry.

The log ends with lines such as:

Masters ready: bias n=51 saved master, dark n=20 exp=120s, flat n=30
Preview debayered: green from RGGB, 3120×2080 px. Star positions are on this image.

The line beside the buttons (No preview yet. before the first calibration) then shows the picture you will work on, for example "Preview: green from RGGB → 3120×2080 (superpixel)".

Why the picture is half size (color cameras). SHOBS-P combines each 2×2 group of the color mask into one pixel (a "superpixel"), so each color keeps its own pixels and is never mixed with its neighbors. The calibrated image is therefore half the width and height of the raw frame. This is normal.

Masters and reusing them

SHOBS-P reuses a saved master as long as nothing it was built from has changed: the same frames (names, sizes, dates and count), the same Binning, and the same bias and dark underneath it. Change any of these and the master is rebuilt and replaced.

  • Rebuild masters (ignore saved ones): tick this to force a fresh stack, for example if you replaced some frames and are not sure SHOBS-P noticed.
  • Binning: masters are made for one binning. If you change Binning on the Input page, calibrate again; the masters for the other binning stay on disk for later.
  • One master file: on the Input page, Master file… points a row at one master SHOBS-P built earlier, instead of a folder of frames.

Before using a master, SHOBS-P checks it against the lights. A master of the wrong size or binning cannot be used, and calibration stops with a message ending "Pick the matching frames, or tick Rebuild masters." Smaller differences are noted as cautions in the log: a different Bayer pattern, gain, offset, a sensor temperature more than 3 °C apart, a flat taken at another rotator angle, or a flat more than 30 days from the lights.

Dark flats

Dark flats are darks taken at the flats' exposure. Many capture programs offer them in place of bias frames. Put them in the Dark flats row on the Input page (chapter 5). The row is optional: with bias, darks and flats and no dark flats, nothing changes.

What SHOBS-P does with them:

  • Flats: each flat becomes flat − dark flat, exactly.
  • No bias frames: the dark flats also stand in for the bias (the camera's zero-exposure offset).
    • With darks that match the lights' exposure, the lights become light − dark exactly, with no warning.
    • With darks that must be scaled, the lights become light − dark flat − (dark − dark flat) × t_light / t_dark, so only the darks' own signal is scaled. The log says so in a note under the "Calibration:" line.
    • The log line "Note: no bias frames. That step is skipped." does not appear when dark flats are given.
  • Bias and dark flats both given: the dark flats are used for the flats, and the bias for the lights and darks. The log says "Bias and dark flats are both given: …".
  • Flats that are already calibrated (for example PixInsight _c files): the dark flats are not used, and the log says so.

The master is saved as SHOBS-P masters/SHOBS-P_master_darkflat_bin<b>_<exp>s.fits in the dark-flat folder and reused like the other masters; Master file… on the row takes this file. A master flat built with dark flats is reused only with the same dark flats. The log says, for example, "Median-combining 15 dark flat frames" and "Saved master dark flat in the dark flat folder's 'SHOBS-P masters' subfolder: …", or "Using saved master dark flat …".

Dark flats in the Flat folder. Some programs save the dark flats beside the flats. SHOBS-P knows a dark flat by its IMAGETYP header ('Dark Flat', 'DARKFLAT', 'Flat Dark', 'FLATDARK', 'Dark-Flat', 'dark_flat' and the like, in any case) or, with no IMAGETYP, by "darkflat" or "flatdark" in the file name. Such frames are never stacked into the master flat.
