With the Dark flats row empty, they are used as the dark flats: "4 dark flats found in the Flat folder (IMAGETYP or name): used as the dark flats, and left out of the master flat." Blink's Flats view leaves them out too (chapter 6).

Dark flats in the Bias row. The older way, dark flats put in the Bias row, still works exactly as before. When the Bias row's frames say they are dark flats (IMAGETYP), the log adds a note that the Dark flats row is now the place for them.

No bias and no dark flats. A dark scaled to another exposure also scales the camera's offset it holds, so the wrong amount of offset comes off. SHOBS-P warns about it on the Input page (chapter 5) and in the log, for example:

  • "⚠ Darks can't be scaled correctly without bias or dark flats. The darks (110 s) are scaled ×0.5 to the lights (55 s), and the camera's offset (pedestal) inside them is scaled too, … Add bias frames or dark flats, or use 55 s darks."
  • "⚠ The flats (0.5 s) get the master dark (55 s) scaled ×0.00909, and with no bias that scales the camera's offset too, so the wrong amount of offset comes off and the master flat is wrong. Put dark flats (darks at the flats' exposure) in the Dark flats box, or add bias frames."

And when the dark flats do not match the flats: "⚠ The dark flats are 2 s and the flats 0.5 s. Dark flats should match the flats' exposure; … Take dark flats at 0.5 s."

Camera binning and the gain

SHOBS-P works out each point's error bar from the photons it collected, so it needs to know how many electrons one image unit holds. It starts from the camera gain in the headers (EGAIN, in electrons per ADU).

When the camera binned the frames (XBINNING above 1), that is not the whole story. EGAIN is the gain of one native pixel, and capture programs write the same value at every binning. Some cameras (CCDs) sum the binned pixels, and then EGAIN is right as it is. Many CMOS cameras average them instead: a stored ADU of a 3×3-binned frame then holds 9 × EGAIN electrons. Used as it is, EGAIN would make the error bars about 2–3 times too big (and the transit fit's reduced χ² well under 1).

SHOBS-P tells the two apart from the sky's own noise, which it always measures on the reference frame. The log's Photon noise: line then adds one sentence, for example:

  • "The camera binned 3x3 and averaged the binned pixels (the sky's noise says so), so each stored ADU holds 9 x EGAIN electrons: about 6.91 electrons per image unit."
  • "The camera binned 2x2 and summed the binned pixels (the sky's noise says so): EGAIN is used as it is."
  • With no sky estimate: "The camera binned 3x3; with no sky estimate SHOBS-P cannot tell whether it summed (EGAIN as it is, used) or averaged (9 x EGAIN) the binned pixels. If the Transit fit's reduced chi-square is well under 1, the camera averaged."

The Transit fit's settings record then says, for example, "Gain 0.768 e-/ADU (header EGAIN); camera bin 3x3 averaged, so 6.91 e- per stored ADU used (from the sky's noise)". SHOBS-P's own Binning box (processing bin) was already handled correctly, and unbinned cameras, mono or color, are not affected at all. Nights reduced before 2.3.0 keep their old error bars until you reduce them again.

Changing the channel

To measure another color of a color camera, choose it in Channel and press Apply channel to the preview. The image is split again with the new color and you go back to the Photometry page. Nothing is recalibrated, so this is quick. The image is solved again, because it is a new picture.

Already-debayered color images

When the lights are color images that another program already debayered and calibrated, SHOBS-P skips the debayer and the calibration for them. The log says These lights are color images (already debayered and calibrated): the bias, dark and flat entries are not used for them. No question is asked.

The Filter box chooses the channel: Green imaging (TG), Red imaging (TR), Blue imaging (TB) or Clear / L (CV) (luminance). Pick another one and press Build masters and calibrate again to see it in the preview. While a step is running the channel cannot change; the log says The channel of the color images cannot change while a step is running; choose it again when it has finished. A Bayer pattern picked for these lights is put back to MONO, because it would debayer them a second time.

Questions you may be asked

These questions appear before the masters are built. Each one explains what Yes and No do.

Calibration frames in the Lights folder. Some downloads (MicroObservatory's, for example) put the darks in with the lights. When bias, dark or flat frames are found in the Lights folder and the matching row on the Input page is empty, SHOBS-P asks, for example:

2 dark frames (60 s) are in the Lights folder, and the Dark box is empty.

Use them as darks? (Yes: used for this run; the files stay where they are. No: the run goes on without them.)

The answer is kept for that folder, so you are asked once. Frames you rejected in Blink stay out. Dark-flats are never taken as flats (or as darks). A master made by another program in the Lights folder is not used, and the log says why.

The darks don't match the lights' exposure. If the darks are more than 3 times shorter or longer than the lights, SHOBS-P asks (the Input page already warned you, chapter 5):

  • Yes: calibrate without the dark this time (bias only). A cooled camera's dark current is usually tiny, and the bias still removes the pedestal.
  • No: use these darks anyway, scaled.
  • Cancel: stop, so you can choose darks closer to the lights' exposure.

The flats were taken at another camera angle. If your headers record the rotator angle and the flats differ from the lights by more than 2°, SHOBS-P asks whether to calibrate with them anyway. No stops, so you can pick flats taken at the lights' angle or clear the Flat row. On a German equatorial mount a meridian flip does not count as a different angle (chapter 4).

On an alt-az mount without a derotator there is no flip, so readings 180° apart are a real turn; SHOBS-P asks when the flats and lights are more than 5° apart. With a derotator it never asks: the log says Caution: flats taken with the derotator at sky angle … and explains that this is fine.

The Flat folder mixes camera angles. One master built from flats at several angles matches none of them exactly. No stops so you can keep only the flats taken at the lights' angle.

The lights look already calibrated. If the lights were calibrated by another program (file names with cal or PixInsight's _c, floating-point pixels, or calibration keywords in the header), SHOBS-P asks Skip bias, dark, and flat for this run? (Recommended). Answer Yes; otherwise the bias and dark are taken out twice and the flat is divided in twice.

Some files can't be read. If a folder holds files SHOBS-P cannot read (RAW, TIFF, JPEG), it lists them and asks whether to calibrate without that step. If no light can be read, it stops.

Messages in the log

You may see these after calibration. None stops you; they explain what SHOBS-P noticed.

  • "Calibration: lights = …; flats = …": how the lights and flats are calibrated, from the rows you filled in (see "Dark flats" above). Any ⚠ under it says what to add.
  • "Flats look already calibrated …: used as they are": your flats came from another program already calibrated; SHOBS-P does not subtract bias, dark or dark flats from them again.
  • "Meridian flip before frame 222 (30° to 210°) …": the rotator reading changed by 180° at a flip. The same flats fit both sides. Each star may show a small step at the flip; SHOBS-P lines this up later.
  • "Note: the camera rotator moved during these lights …": the camera really turned during the night. One set of flats can only match one angle, so expect steps in the light curve there. On an alt-az mount every change over 2° counts as a move, never as a flip.
  • "Mount: Alt-az …" (written when you scan the folders): which mount is used for these lights, and whether it came from the headers or the Mount box.
  • "Zenith keyhole: the field reached altitude …" (also written at the scan): on an alt-az mount the field went above 85°, where the mount's azimuth speed and the field rotation get extreme. Check those frames on the Blink page.
  • "Note: the camera binned these frames (XBINNING=2) …": your color camera did the binning. Some capture programs mix red, green and blue when they bin a color sensor; capture unbinned and set Binning in SHOBS-P instead. (Not shown for MicroObservatory frames, which are binned by design, or for a monochrome camera.)
  • "The lights carry a plate solution in their FITS headers: Solve uses it directly.": good news; the solve will be quick.
  • "Caution: the FITS FILTER is 'Ha', which SHOBS-P cannot calibrate …": see chapter 4, "Filters SHOBS-P cannot use".

When calibration fails

A window says what went wrong. It starts Calibration failed:, and the same line goes into the log. The most common messages:

Message What to do
No light frames found. Check the Lights row on the Input page, and that the frames are FITS or XISF.
The masters do not fit these lights: … A master of another size or binning. Pick the matching calibration frames, set Binning back, or tick Rebuild masters (ignore saved ones).
… is not a master SHOBS-P built. A row points at another program's master. Choose the folder of raw frames instead.
… is a master dark, not a master flat. (or similar) A row points at the wrong kind of master. Pick the right one with Master file….

The full details are always in the log on this page.

Caution: A monochrome camera that writes no Bayer keyword and has no flats is detected from the lights' sky background, which needs bias or dark frames (or a bright sky). If SHOBS-P still cannot tell, it asks Color or monochrome camera? when you press Scan folders. If you skipped the scan, Build masters and calibrate runs its checks first (from 2.3.2), so the question comes then; still check that Bayer pattern says MONO before calibrating, or every frame is split as if it had a color mask.

Previous: 6. Blink · Next: 8. Photometry page basics

8. Photometry page basics

The Photometry page is where you choose which stars to measure and then measure them on every frame. This chapter covers what works the same in every mode: the automatic solve, the image view, marking stars, apertures, and running photometry, and stacking frames when the comparison stars are weak. Choosing good comparison stars for a variable is in chapter 9; transits are in chapters 16 and 17; Discovery's Scan field is in chapter 20.

The page at a glance

From top to bottom:

  • The tools row. On the left, what a click on the image does: ● Target, ● Comps (C1–C10), ● Check, ● Watch, and Clear selected. On the right: Circles, Solve again and the orange Run photometry button.
  • The options row. On the left, one line under the Target line, the aperture boxes Aperture r, sky in and sky out, the Suggest… button, raw sat ADU, linear ADU, and Also measure R and B (color index; slower). On the right, the View box. Under the aperture boxes, beside the View box, these lines appear only when they have something to say (when the window is narrow and the View box moves to a line of its own, they go on that line, left of the View box, if they fit there):
    • The Stack row (after the first run only): a suggestion line, then Stack N [box] frames Precision goal [box] ppt per point (optional), the Stack button and the small Points box (see "Stacking when comps are weak" below).
    • The "Measured with" line: after a run, a gray line says what the photometry was measured with (see "Apertures" below).
    • An orange line when the run skipped frames or left a comp out of some frames (see "Comps near the frame edge" below).
  • The step strip: what is done and what to do next.
  • The image on the left, with its legend in a column at its left, and the Stars and Watch stars panels on the right.
  • The run row: a gray line with the last run's summary (for example "Photometry done in 1:52. 38 points."), Cancel run, and Hide log / Show log, with the log under it. While a run is going, its progress is on the busy bar at the bottom of the window (chapter 3).
  • Back and Next: Output at the bottom.

In Discovery mode the page is called Photometry: field scan, the left of the tools row reads Click a star to ● Watch it / ✕ Exclude it, and Scan field replaces Run photometry.

The image solves itself

You don't need to press anything to solve the image. As soon as the page opens with a freshly calibrated image, SHOBS-P:

  1. works out where the frame points: from the plate solution in the FITS header if there is one, otherwise from the target's RA and Dec on the Input page (or the header's pointing), and matches the star pattern;
  2. asks every catalog at once (the AAVSO comparison-star sequence, Gaia DR3, APASS and Tycho-2) and merges them into one list of stars;
  3. checks the field against VSX and SIMBAD for known and suspected variables;
  4. places the Target marker on your target, if the Input page gives its position.

While it works, the busy bar at the bottom of the window (chapter 3) says what the solve is doing. It starts with, for example, Solving: fetching AAVSO, Gaia, APASS and Tycho stars (…′ field, V band)…, then ticks off each catalog as it answers: Solving: fetching catalogs: AAVSO ✓ APASS ✓ Tycho ✓ Gaia…. On a hard field you may also see Solving: only N stars on this frame, stacking M frames to find more…, Solving: no lock at the target marker, trying N nearby stars as the target… or Solving the field from star patterns…. You can start marking stars once the AAVSO sequence and APASS are in; Gaia's faint stars finish in the background.

The first label of the step strip shows how it went: 1 Solving…, ✓ 1 Solved or 1 Not solved.

When solved, the title over the image reads, for example, "Solved · AAVSO chart X12345ABC 43/60 matched rotation 12.3° …", and a small yellow N and E compass appears in a corner. Before a solve, the corner says N/E: orientation unknown until the image is solved.

Solve again repeats the solve, for example after a catalog did not answer, or after you corrected the focal length.

When the AAVSO sequence is slow. The AAVSO chart server (VSP, at apps.aavso.org) sometimes answers late or not at all. SHOBS-P does not hold up the solve for it: it asks again in the background a few times, and the log says exactly what happened, for example "AAVSO sequence did not answer (503 from apps.aavso.org); asking again in the background (…)". If it answers later, the log says so and the status bar says The AAVSO sequence answered after the solve: Solve again to use it. Press Solve again; the sequence is used at once, with no new request.

When the target is not on the first frame. Sometimes the first frames miss the target, for example while the telescope was still settling. If the plate solution puts the target off the first frame, SHOBS-P looks at the next frames (up to 10) for the first one that holds it, without solving each one (the busy bar says, for example, Looking for SZ Lyn on the next frames… 3 of 10). That frame becomes the reference: the image is solved again and the Target marker placed. The log says, for example, "Target: SZ Lyn is on frame 6 (…), not on frames 1–5. Frame 6 is the reference now; the run skips the 5 frames without SZ Lyn and lists every frame that misses it." If it cannot find the target, the log says to reject the frames without it in Blink and calibrate again.

Tip: A solve needs only a rough idea of where the frame points: the target's name looked up on the Input page is enough. Frames without a plate solution or RA/Dec in their headers are fine.

When the image is not solved

The automatic solve stays quiet when it fails: the step strip shows 1 Not solved and the log says why. Press Solve again to see the full explanation in a window. The usual causes:

What you see What to do
Solve needs to know roughly where the frame is: … Your headers give no position and the Input page has no RA/Dec. Type the target's name on the Input page and press Enter (or, in Discovery, the field center), then Solve again.
Star catalogs did not answer, so the frame could not be solved … with "Gaia DR3 (HTTP 503) and APASS DR9 (no stars came back) did not answer: a catalog service outage …" A catalog server is down (not your settings). Solve again in a few minutes. Meanwhile you can mark stars and type comp and check magnitudes by hand.
The catalog stars did not lock onto this frame … Usually wrong optics. Check Focal length (mm), Pixel size (µm) and Binning on the Input page; the window shows the plate scale it used. Also check that the Target marker is on your star.
"Only N stars were found on this frame" (in the same window) Very short exposures or a small telescope. Mark the stars by hand and type their names and magnitudes.
Not solved: the catalog request failed. The internet or a catalog server was down. Solve again later.

You can still mark stars on an image that is not solved; their names and magnitudes fill in as soon as a solve succeeds. Or type the magnitudes yourself.

The Circles box

Every catalog is always used. The Circles box only chooses whose circles are drawn on the image: AAVSO sequence, Gaia DR3, APASS DR9 or Tycho-2. Where a field has no AAVSO sequence (most exoplanet hosts), SHOBS-P draws another catalog's circles instead, and the legend says so.

The image view

Zoom and pan

  • Mouse wheel over the image zooms in and out around the pointer.
  • Drag with the left mouse button to pan.
  • In the View box: − and + zoom around the middle of the view, and Fit shows the whole frame.

Zoom and pan stay as they are while you mark stars or change the display. Only a new image (a new calibration, binning or channel) goes back to the whole frame.

On a trackpad without a wheel, use −, + and Fit, or pop the image out.

Pop out the image

Press Pop out in the View box to put the image in its own window, which you can make as large as you like or move to a second monitor. The window has the same ● Target, ● Comps (C1–C10), ● Check, ● Watch and Clear selected controls, and Zoom, −, +, Fit and Go back. The + and − keys also zoom there.

Small image? On a small frame (MicroObservatory's, for example), or with Windows display scaling at 125 % or 150 %, the image area can get short and wide. SHOBS-P then gives the image room first: the log under it shrinks (down to 3 lines). If the image is still small, press Pop out in the View box, or Hide log. (Up to 2.3.1 a line under the image said so; from 2.3.2 that line is gone, so the image gets its height.)

While the image is out, the page shows Image is in its own window (Bring back in the View box). and the Pop out button reads Bring back. The image comes back when you press Go back or Bring back, close the window, start a run, switch modes or leave the Photometry page. SHOBS-P remembers where you put the window.

What you can show

The View box has four switches:

  • Show variables (red ○ ×): known and suspected variables from VSX and SIMBAD. A red circle is a catalog star that is a variable; a red × is a variable with no catalog circle.
  • Show apertures: the measuring circle and sky ring around each marked star.
  • Show catalog stars: the catalog circles.
  • Show labels: the labels next to circles, and the compass.

(Discovery adds Candidates only; see chapter 20.)

Under the switches are the same Stretch, black, white and Reset controls as on the Blink page (chapter 6). They change only the display.

What the marks mean

The legend at the left of the image (one entry per line) lists only the marks drawn right now. The most common:

Mark Meaning
Orange + with T the target
Green + with C1, C2 … comparison stars
Blue + with K the check star
Solid, dashed and dotted rings around a marked star the aperture, and the inner and outer edges of the sky ring
Thin dashed cyan ring with a number an AAVSO sequence star; the number is its V magnitude without the decimal point (91 = V 9.1)
Yellow circle a star of the catalog chosen in Circles
Thick green ring with S1, S2 … a suggested comparison star
Small light-blue diamond a spare comparison star SHOBS-P picked
Cyan diamond with W a watch star

Hold the pointer over any star to see a small box with its name, catalog magnitudes, color and pixel position, and a warning if it is a known variable.

Suggested comps and AAVSO sequence stars are easy to mix up on a busy field, so a small key on the image itself (in the corner beside the compass) shows the two: S1, S2 … suggested comp and 91 = V 9.1 AAVSO sequence. Like the compass, it follows Show labels.

The step strip

The step strip under the options row shows your progress: 1 Solve → 2 Target → 3 Comps → 4 Check → Run (Discovery: 1 Solve → 2 Scan field). Done steps get a ✓. The line under it says what to do now, for example Solved. Pick Target and click the target star. and finally Ready: Run photometry.

Hide tips hides the strip once you know your way around; Show tips brings it back. SHOBS-P remembers your choice.

Mark the stars

You mark stars by choosing what a click means (● Target, ● Comps (C1–C10), ● Check or ● Watch), then clicking the star on the image.

The target

If the Input page knows your target's position, SHOBS-P puts the Target marker on it after the solve, and the log says "Target: marker placed on … Click with Target to move it if it is not your star." Check it is the right star.

In Variables and Transits mode, the click choice then moves on to ● Comps (C1–C10) by itself, and the log adds "Next: click the comparison stars (Comps is picked)." If you already picked a choice yourself on this image, SHOBS-P leaves it alone.

To place or move it by hand: choose ● Target and click the star.

The target's name in the Stars panel is the Star ID from the Input page. Clicking does not change it.

Comparison stars and the check star

  1. Choose ● Comps (C1–C10) and click a comparison star. It becomes Comp 1 (C1 on the image). Click more stars to add Comp 2, Comp 3 and so on, up to ten.
  2. Choose ● Check and click a check star: a steady star that is not one of the comps. It tests whether the comps are behaving.

Each click snaps the marker onto the star nearest to where you clicked. For comps and the check star, SHOBS-P also finds the star from a little further out, so a click that is a few pixels off still lands on the star's center. The status bar says, for example, " Centered on the star (2.3 px from the click)." If there is no star there, it says " No star found right at the click; the marker is where you clicked." Watch for that one.

How names and magnitudes fill in. On a solved image, clicking a comp or the check star fills its row in the Stars panel: the star's name (its AAVSO identifier or catalog name) and its catalog magnitude in the band you are measuring (the column header says which, for example V magnitude). AAVSO sequence magnitudes are used first, then APASS, Gaia and Tycho-2. If the check star has no catalog name (for example when a catalog did not answer), SHOBS-P names it by its sky position when you press Run photometry, the way APASS names its stars, for example J020351.8+464115. The log says so, and that name goes into the series, the reports and the settings file instead of a bare "check" (a night added to a series whose check was saved as "check" before 2.3.2 keeps the series' name). The line under each row says where the magnitude came from and adds checks:

  • its color (B−V) and a warning if it is very red;
  • how much brighter or fainter than the target it is, with a warning if it is more than about 1 magnitude away;
  • "not a known or suspected variable (VSX + SIMBAD)", or a red ⚠ if it is one;
  • with a linear ADU set, a caution when the star's brightest raw pixel is over or near the linear limit, for example "⚠ raw peak 41,200 ADU is over the linear limit (36,000): the camera's response bends there, so this star reads too faint — pick a fainter comp." (for the target: "— shorter exposures or more defocus");
  • for a comp or check star near the frame edge, how far in a star must be, for example "⚠ only 12 px from the frame edge: its sky ring (out to 22 px) needs the star at least 27 px in, with room for drift — pick a star further in". For the check star it also names a suggested comp ring farther in, for example "…; S2 (TYC 3089-883-1) is farther in and would make a good check".

Suggested comparison stars are drawn as thick green rings labeled S1, S2 …: up to four catalog stars close to the target in color and brightness, not variable, not near saturation or the linear limit, well inside the frame, AAVSO sequence stars first. They are good first choices. Under the Stars panel, one line per suggestion says what it is and why, for example "S1 = … V 11.20 (AAVSO sequence 112): 0.3 mag fainter than the target, B−V 0.62 (target 0.60, measured), raw peak 18,400 ADU (51% of the linear limit)", and "— now C2" once you use it. Chapter 9 says more about choosing comps.

How S1–S4 are chosen

From 2.3.4 the rules are:

  • Not a known or suspected variable (VSX and SIMBAD), and labeled in a catalog with a magnitude in your band.
  • Brightness: within 1 mag of the target (for a faint target, 0.5 to 2 mag brighter, about 1 mag brighter best).
  • Colour first: within 0.3 in B−V of the target, and colour counts twice as much as brightness when they are ranked. The target's colour is its measured B−V when the loaded series has nights with Also measure R and B, otherwise the catalog's (the line says which). When too few stars pass, stars up to 0.6 apart fill in, and their line says the closest color left here (no more within 0.3). A star with no colour in the catalogs only fills in (when the target's colour is known), and its line says so.
  • Linear: with a linear ADU set, the star's brightest raw pixel on the reference frame must be under 75 % of it, so the sharpest frames of the night stay linear too.
  • Inside the frame: at least 3 × the sky-out radius from every edge, so a framing shift between nights does not drop it. Nearer stars only fill in when the field has too few, and their line says how far in they are.
  • AAVSO sequence stars that pass come first, then other catalog stars.

What a suggestion cannot know yet is how steady the star is: that shows after the run, in Comp health… (chapter 11).

Variables are caught. If you click a known or suspected variable as a comp or check star, SHOBS-P asks whether to use it anyway. No removes the marker.

Place a star by name

You can also type a star instead of clicking it:

  1. Click in the empty name box of a comp row (its line reads type a name or RA/Dec and press Enter, or click the star) or the Check row.
  2. Type a name, for example an AAVSO identifier, TYC 4006-1310-1, a Gaia ID, or a position such as 23 13 48.1 +57 07 34.
  3. Press Enter. SHOBS-P finds the star on the image, places the marker and fills in the magnitude.

The image must be solved for this to work.

Remove a mark

  • Right-click a marker on the image to clear it.
  • Or press ✕ at the end of its row in the Stars panel (it clears the marker, the name and the magnitude).
  • Or press Clear selected: it clears the marker of the current choice (for Comps, the last one you placed). With ● Watch chosen, it stops watching the star selected in the Watch stars list (or, with none selected, the watch star you added last on this frame).

To replace a comparison star, remove it first and then click the new one; clicks in Comps mode always add.

Comps follow Use comps. When you choose a subset of comps with Use comps… (chapter 11), the comp rows and markers on this page change to the comps in use. The others are not lost: they are still measured on every frame as spares (small diamonds), and choosing the original set again puts them back where they were. The log says, for example, "Photometry comps now match Use comps: C1 …, C2 …; … still measured as spare".

Watch stars

● Watch adds a star you want to follow without using it as a comparison star, for example a suspected variable in the field. Watch stars are measured on every run and get their own light curves on the Output page. Use Add a star… in the Watch stars box to add one by name, and Remove selected to stop watching it.

Apertures

SHOBS-P measures each star inside a circle (the aperture) and estimates the sky from a ring around it. The three boxes are in pixels on the calibrated image:

  • Aperture r: the radius of the measuring circle.
  • sky in, sky out: the inner and outer radius of the sky ring.

The rings are drawn on the image as you type, so you can see whether a neighbor star falls inside them.

What was measured. After a run, a gray line under the aperture boxes says what the photometry was measured with, for example "Measured with r 8, sky 14–22 px (Bin 1)". If you then change the aperture, sky or Binning boxes, the line turns orange: "⚠ Aperture changed (now r 6, …): run photometry again to apply it." The new sizes are used only by the next run.

Aperture kept from another target. The aperture boxes keep their values from the last target. On a new image, the log cautions when they don't fit these stars: an aperture larger than 2.5 × the stars' FWHM (it adds sky noise and neighbors), or, on defocused stars, one that clips the donut. Each caution says what Suggest… would give.

Defocused stars (donuts)

Many transit observers defocus on purpose, so each star becomes a ring (a "donut"). SHOBS-P decides once per run whether the field is defocused. If it is, the log says, for example, "Defocused stars (donuts, about 24 px across on 12 of 15 test stars): each star is centered on its light centroid, not on the bright side of its ring." Then:

  • every star is centered on the middle of its light, the same way on every frame;
  • Suggest… sizes the aperture to hold 95 % of the star's light (never smaller than the donut), instead of using the FWHM, which means little on a donut;
  • Run photometry asks first when the aperture would clip the ring (see the questions below);
  • after the run, the log reports how much each aperture's center jittered from frame to frame (the rms, in pixels), with a caution over 0.5 px.

In-focus fields are measured exactly as before.

Let SHOBS-P suggest the sizes

  1. Press Suggest…. SHOBS-P measures the star size (FWHM) on bright, isolated stars and opens the Suggested apertures window. (On defocused stars it measures the radius that holds 95 % of the light instead.)
  2. Read the bold line: the suggested Aperture r (1.8 × FWHM, or the 95 % light radius on donuts) and sky ring. Notes below it warn about neighbors inside a marked star's aperture.
  3. Optional, but worth it on a new setup: press Test 4 apertures on 30 frames (or 60 frames (slower, surer)). SHOBS-P measures real frames with four aperture sizes and tells you which gave the least scatter on the check star, for example "Clear winner: r = 4.8 …" or "No clear winner: … Keep the suggested r = 5.8". The test needs at least one comp, and ideally a check star. Without a check star it judges on the target, and says so ("⚠ No check star: judging on the target, which may truly vary …"). In Discovery, which has no comps, it uses bright, isolated, unsaturated field stars instead. While the test runs, the busy bar counts the frames, for example Aperture test… 12 of 30.
  4. Press Use these aperture sizes. The boxes are filled and the window closes.

Tip: Use the same aperture sizes for every night of a series. SHOBS-P reminds you of this when you set them.

Raw sat ADU

raw sat ADU is the level at which your camera saturates, in the units of the raw frame (for most 16-bit cameras, about 60000 or a little more). SHOBS-P checks every star's peak on the raw frame against it and flags saturated frames. Set it to your camera's real full-well value if you know it.

Linear ADU

linear ADU is your camera's linear limit, in raw units (chapter 4). Leave it empty if you don't know it; nothing changes then. With a value set below raw sat ADU, SHOBS-P cautions when you pick a star whose raw peak is over or near it (see "Comparison stars and the check star" above), counts after each run the frames where each star went over it ("⚠ Linear limit 36,000 ADU: raw peak at or above it in … frames …"), and Comp health shows an Over linear limit column (chapter 11).

Color

Also measure R and B (color index; slower) measures the comps and target in the other colors too, so SHOBS-P can work out color terms. It makes the run slower; leave it off unless you need colors.

Run photometry

  1. Check the step strip says Ready: Run photometry.
  2. Press Run photometry.
  3. Answer any questions (below).
  4. Watch the busy bar at the bottom of the window. It says Photometry on 300 lights…, then counts the frames with the time left, for example Photometry… 120 of 300, about 3:15 left. The log shows one line per frame with the target's magnitude, the star size and how far the frame moved. When the run ends, the gray line beside Cancel run says how long it took, for example Photometry done in 1:52. 38 points.

Every frame is calibrated, lined up with the first one (through shifts, small rotations and meridian flips), and measured. On an alt-az mount (the Mount box on the Input page, or clear evidence in the headers) there are no flips: the alignment follows the field rotation instead, and the log says how far the field turned, for example "Field rotation: the field turned by 12.4° on the sensor through the night (alt-az mount); the apertures followed it." If the field turns by more than 5° on a German equatorial mount, the log suggests setting Mount to Alt-az. The other way round, when Mount is Alt-az but the field turns by about 180° between two frames, the run warns: "⚠ The field turned by 180° between frames 314a and 1b — that looks like a meridian flip. Is the Mount set right (German equatorial)? An alt-az field turns smoothly, not by half a turn between two frames. If it was a flip, set Mount to German equatorial in the Optics box (Input page) and run photometry again, so the frames after the flip are treated as after it." An alt-az field turns smoothly, well under a degree per frame, so ordinary field rotation never sets this off, not even across a long gap or near the zenith. Frames that cannot be lined up (cloud, trailing) are skipped and listed in the log. When the run is done, SHOBS-P switches to the Output page.

After a meridian flip, SHOBS-P checks the alignment of each flipped frame on up to 15 stars' own centers, so the comp and check apertures land on their stars again. If the frame you marked the stars on was rejected in Blink, the first kept frame becomes the reference (chapter 6). Frames that miss the target are skipped and listed at the end, for example "SZ Lyn not on the frame in 5 of 142 images (frames 1–5); those frames were skipped."

To stop, press Cancel run. Nothing is added: the gray line beside Cancel run says, for example, "Photometry done in 0:12. Canceled; nothing was added."

Comps near the frame edge

When the field drifts during the night, a comparison star near the edge can slide partly off the frame. SHOBS-P then leaves that comp out of that frame only, and measures the frame with the other comps. This happens when the comp's aperture is partly off the frame, when its sky ring is mostly (over half) off the frame, or when it has no light above the sky in that frame. A clipped comp is never measured, so it cannot leak into the results.

  • No step in the light curve. In a frame without some comps, the comparison is rebuilt from the comps that are there, each first put on the night's scale by its own median offset from the others. So the comparison level stays the same when a comp leaves or comes back; such a frame is only a little noisier. Frames with every comp are measured exactly as before.
  • A comp missing most of the night. A comp measured in fewer than half of a night's frames (or in fewer than 5) is left out of the whole night, and the night uses the other comps from start to end. The log says so.
  • The check star off the edge: that frame has no check value, and the frame is kept.
  • Spares and watch stars off the edge are simply not measured in that frame. They never cost a frame.
  • The target off the edge: the frame is skipped, and listed.
  • Every comp lost in a frame: the frame is skipped, with the reason "every comp lost (off the frame edge)".

After the run, an orange line under the "Measured with" line lists what happened, for example:

  • "⚠ 16 frames skipped: every comp lost (off the frame edge) (frames 25–40)."
  • "C3 left out of 14 frames (off the frame edge; frames 27–40); those frames use the other comps."
  • "check star left out of 18 frames (off the frame edge; frames 23–40); no check value there."
  • "…; out of the whole night (missing in most frames)." when the whole-night rule applied.

A warning box after the run lists the same lines, and the log has them too, each with its night.

Measure skipped frames

Since 2.3.0 a comp that leaves the frame (or is clipped, or has no flux) is simply left out of those frames, and the other comps carry them, so a single edge comp no longer costs frames. A frame is skipped only when every comp was lost in it, for example on a one-comp night when that comp drifts off the edge, or when all the comps sit near the same edge.

Skipped frames stay skipped when you later drop the comps that caused them with Use comps… (chapter 11): Use comps recalculates the measured points, it does not measure the skipped frames. The orange line then says "⚠ N frames were skipped because of …, which is no longer a comp — Run photometry again to measure them.", and a Measure skipped frames button appears at the right of that line. The log and the status line say it too.

Measure skipped frames measures only those frames, with the comps now in use, and adds them to the night. An open series with a file is saved automatically; in Transits mode the old fit is cleared (press Fit transit to fit again). It needs that night's Lights folder on the Input page and its image loaded; otherwise a box explains this and says to use Run photometry. Once the frames are back, the line and the button go away.

Questions it may ask

You will not see most of these on most nights. Each window explains its choices.

  • The aperture is much larger (or smaller) than the stars. Common after switching telescopes. No stops so you can press Suggest…; the window says what it would use.
  • Defocused stars: the aperture clips the ring. On a defocused field, an aperture smaller than the donut loses a different part of the ring in every frame. The question gives the smallest safe r and what Suggest… would use, and asks Run with the current aperture anyway?
  • There is no star under a marker. A marker on blank sky. No stops so you can move it.
  • Names without markers. A comp or check row has a name but no marker on this image (often left from a series). Yes clears those names and continues; No continues and leaves them; Cancel stops so you can mark them.
  • Wrong mode. In Transits mode with a target that looks like a variable star and no transit predicted, SHOBS-P offers to Run in Variables mode instead?. In Variables mode, when the Planet (Transits) box holds a planet of this star, or the target is a known planet host (SHOBS-P looks its planets up in the NASA Exoplanet Archive after the Star ID lookup), and a transit is predicted during your frames, it offers Run in Transits mode instead? (not asked while a Variables series is open). Yes fills in the planet and runs in Transits mode. Answer the question rather than canceling: the series and reports are kept per mode.
  • An ensemble without a check star (Variables mode, two or more comps, no check): AAVSO requires a check star for ensemble reports. Continue without one?
  • Color can't be calibrated. Also measure R and B is ticked but too few catalog stars have colors. Run anyway with uncalibrated color?
  • The open series is in another mode. SHOBS-P offers to switch modes so the night can be added to it.

A marker off its star. Before the run, SHOBS-P checks that each marker sits on its star's center. If one would be measured beside the star (so it would read faint), the log says, for example, "⚠ Check marker at (512.0, 340.5) is 6.2 px from the center of the star there (…)", and the status bar says Check the Log: Check is marked off the center of the star. The run goes on; if that is not what you meant, clear the marker, zoom in and click the star again.

Some problems stop the run with a message instead, for example Mark at least one comparison star (Comps, then click the star), Need aperture r < sky in < sky out, or two markers on the same star. Fix what it says and press Run photometry again.

If your Site boxes are empty, the log notes that there will be no airmass and no scintillation in the error bars. Type your site once on the Input page to fix this for good.

After the run

If a series is open in the same mode, SHOBS-P asks whether to add the night to it (chapter 10). Otherwise the night is a single night. Either way, nothing is written to disk until you save, unless the open series is saved automatically (the status bar then names the file).

The run's results are on the Output page: the light curve for Variables (chapter 9), the transit fit for Transits (chapter 17), or the scan results for Discovery (chapter 20).

Stacking when comps are weak

The comparison stars set a floor under your error bars. When they are much fainter than the target, their noise, not the target's, decides how good each point is. More comps help a little; brighter comps help most. When there are none, SHOBS-P can stack the frames: it combines groups of N calibrated frames and measures the stacks. Each stacked point then has about √N times less photon noise, at the cost of N times fewer points.

Caution: A stack hides anything faster than itself. A stack of 20 one-minute frames cannot show a change that takes less than 20 minutes. SHOBS-P keeps its suggestion short enough for the signal you are after (below), but check it against your target.

The suggestion line

After every per-frame run (or Scan field in Discovery) the Stack row appears under the aperture boxes, beside the View box. Its first line says whether stacking would help. SHOBS-P compares the target's photon noise with the photon noise of the comp ensemble, over every good frame, from the run's own error model. The log gets the numbers, for example "Noise check (per frame): target 5.4 mmag (SNR 202), comp ensemble 21.5 mmag (SNR 50; each comp 36, 35) over 60 frames; comp noise 4.01× the target's."

The line reads (N and the numbers are examples):

  • The comps are weak (orange, with ⚠): "⚠ Comps are weak (comp noise 4.7× the target's per frame). Stack 20 frames (60 s each) → comps SNR ~193; 200 frames → 10 points?" It may add why N is what it is: "Capped at 57 frames by the time resolution (1/50 of the 2.40-h period = 2.9 min); the comp rule wanted 100.", "Kept to 20 frames so the night still has at least 10 points.", "The 2 ppt goal is not met (about 2.6 ppt per point)." or "(3 frames left out of stacks: 2 sat, 1 cloud.)".
  • The comps are weak but the time cap allows no stacking: "⚠ Comps are weak (…), but the time resolution allows no stacking (…). Brighter comps, or longer exposures, would help."
  • The comps are fine, but your precision goal is not reached per frame: "⚠ Per-frame points are about 3.1 ppt; your 2 ppt goal needs stacks of 3 frames (9 s each); …"
  • The comps are fine (gray): "Comps are fine per frame (comp noise 35% of the target's): stacking is not needed. Type N to stack anyway."
  • Discovery has no comps, so the line is never orange: "Stacking (optional): stacks of 6 frames (18 s each); 60 frames → 10 points. Time cap 5.0 min: a stack hides events faster than itself."

How N is chosen

  • The comp rule (the default): the smallest N at which a stack's comp-ensemble noise is no more than half the target's noise in one frame.
  • Precision goal (optional, empty by default, in ppt per point): N grows until a stacked point's whole error, scintillation included, meets the goal.
  • The time cap, so a stack does not blur the signal:
    • Transits: 1/5 of the ingress (or egress) time, from the planet on the Input page (its period, a/R*, Rp/R* and b, or T14);
    • Variables: 1/50 of the VSX period;
    • Discovery: 5 minutes;
    • with no planet or no period known: 2 minutes (Transits) or 5 minutes (Variables), and the line says this is "a conservative default".
  • At least 10 points are always left in the night.

The Stack N box fills in with the suggestion. If you type your own N, it is kept. Typing a precision goal fills in the new suggestion.

Stack

Press Stack. It asks first only when it needs to:

  • stacks longer than the time cap: "Stacks of N frames last about …, longer than the time resolution allows (…): they may blur the signal. Stack anyway?";
  • in Transits mode, fewer than 20 stacks: "That makes only K stacked points; the transit fit needs 20 or more. Stack anyway?";
  • in Discovery, fewer than 10 stacks stops with "That makes only K stacks; a field scan needs at least 10. Use a smaller N."

Then the busy bar shows two steps: Stacking… k of K (each stack is made), then Photometry on K stacks… (the same photometry runs on the stacks; in Discovery, the field scan does). The log says what was done, for example:

Stacking 200 frames into 10 stacks of up to 20 (sigma-clipped mean; whole-pixel shifts from the measured
drift; never across a meridian flip or a gap).
Stacks made: 200 frames → 10 stacks of 20 frames (effective exposure 40 s); 0.012% of the pixel values
clipped (cosmic rays, satellite trails, hot pixels); shifts up to 2 px.
Photometry on the stacks done: 10 points (stacks of 20 frames (effective exposure 40 s)), 0 flagged.
Points: Stacked is selected; Per frame (on the Stack row) switches back.

When some stacks are short, the log adds "The last stack of a run (before a flip, a gap, or the end) can be partial." When the field turned inside a stack (an alt-az mount), it adds a note on how far, for example "Note: the field turned by up to 0.30° inside a stack (about 1.4 px at the frame's corners) …".

The aperture on the stacks. Stacked stars can be a little wider than in one frame. The log line "Aperture check on the stacks: …" compares the stacks' star FWHM with one frame's and gives the suggested r for each. When the stacks' stars are more than 15 % wider, the line starts with ⚠ and says to run Suggest… with Points: Stacked: the aperture test then measures the stacks. If you choose a new aperture, press Stack again. The stacks are reused, so only the photometry is redone.

After a stack run SHOBS-P goes to the Output page, with Points: Stacked selected.

What goes into a stack

  • Rejected frames (Blink) are not in the run, so not in a stack.
  • Frames the per-frame run flagged (sat, compsat, drift, cloud and the other flags) are left out before stacking.
  • Saturation is judged per exposure, so a sum of unsaturated frames stays linear.
  • A stack never spans a meridian flip, a rotator move, or a gap of more than 3 frame spacings. Each side of a flip is stacked on its own, so the last stack before a flip or gap can be short (partial).
  • There is one way to combine: a sigma-clipped mean (a plain mean below 5 frames). The clip removes cosmic rays, satellite trails and hot pixels, and leaves alone starlight that only changes with the seeing. There is no median, no weighting and no drizzle.
  • The frames are lined up by whole pixels, from the drift the run already measured (on a color camera, by whole 2×2 color cells). Mono and color cameras both work, and no plate solution or header pointing is needed.
  • Stacks are 32-bit floating point, so they have no 16-bit ceiling.
  • Each stack records its mid-time (the mean of its frames' mid-times), its frame count N and its effective exposure (the sum of its frames' exposures).
  • Stacks are kept in SHOBS-P's cache folder (~/.shobs_p_cache/stacks), never in your Lights folder. Only the newest set is kept, because each stack is a full frame.

The Points box: Per frame or Stacked

Once a night has been stacked it has two sets of points. The Points box, on the Stack row after the Stack button, chooses which set is the night's:

  • Per frame and Stacked (grayed until there are stacks). Switching is instant.
  • Under them, Results use: Per frame or Results use: Stacked (20 frames). The Output page shows the same text at the right end of the row with the Airmass-corrected night means box (empty until the night is stacked).

Everything after photometry uses the chosen set: the Output page, the period search and Time of max/min, Comp health…, the AAVSO report and light-curve CSV, Save series, the transit fit and the ExoClock, EXOTIC and ExoFOP files. A Use comps… choice is applied to whichever set is switched in. With a series file open, a switch saves the series with the chosen set, as a run does. The series keeps only the chosen set.

When a series is loaded or closed, or the night is removed, the Points box goes back to Per frame and Stacked grays out.

What the reports say

  • AAVSO report: the NOTES get "points are stacks of 40 frames (effective exposure 30 s)", after any notes you typed.
  • Light-curve CSV: the exptime column holds the effective exposure, and the file and frame columns name the stack (stack_0003 is stack 3).
  • Transit fit: the settings record says "Stacking: stacks of 40 frames (effective exposure 30 s) (sigma-clipped mean, whole-pixel shifts, never across a flip)", and the fit uses the effective exposure (chapter 17).
  • Series file: a stacked night carries its stack size and effective exposure (chapter 22).

Previous: 7. Calibrate · Next: 9. Reduce a variable-star night

9. Reduce a variable-star night

This chapter takes one night of a variable star from folders to a light curve. It assumes you have read chapters 5 to 8, which cover the steps every mode shares. Here we stay in Variables mode and focus on what matters for variable-star work: the target, the comparison stars, the check star, and reading the Output – Variability page.

If you want to add this night to nights you already have, load the series before you run photometry. See chapter 10, Build a multi-night series.

Before you start

  • Choose Variables in the mode list on the left (navy).
  • On the Input page, fill in the AAVSO report box once: your Observer code, and the Chart ID if you know it. SHOBS-P fills in the chart ID for you when you use AAVSO sequence stars as comps (see below), so you can leave it empty.
  • Type your site in the Site box once. It is remembered. Without it there is no airmass, so the AAVSO report shows AMASS na, the error bars leave out scintillation, and the airmass check of the period (chapter 13) cannot run.

Set the target

  1. On the Input page, type the star's name in Star ID, for example SZ Lyn.
  2. Press Enter (or click into another box). SHOBS-P looks the star up in SIMBAD and VSX; the busy bar at the bottom of the window says Looking up SZ Lyn at SIMBAD and VSX… meanwhile (chapter 3). Then the status bar shows where it found the star (✔ SZ Lyn from SIMBAD: …), its RA and Dec, and its VSX period and type when VSX has them.
  3. If the lookup failed (no internet, unusual name, or no answer within 60 s), press Look up again, or type RA/Dec yourself. You can also go on without them: the plate solution can fill them in later.

Tip: Reducing a different star than last time? Press New reduction on the Input page first (chapter 5). It clears the old star's comps, check star and other settings, and puts the Output page's Notes back to the default (OSC reduced; not transformed), so the old star's notes do not slip into this star's report.

Tip: If an amber line appears under Star ID saying how AAVSO spells the name (for example AAVSO spells this “HD 219134”), press Use it. WebObs matches reports to stars by name, so the AAVSO spelling saves trouble later.

Then point the Input page at your frames, blink them, and calibrate, as in chapters 5 to 7.

Mark the target, comps and check star

When the Photometry page opens, the image is solved on its own (chapter 8). The step strip above the image tells you what to do next: 1 Solve → 2 Target → 3 Comps → 4 Check → Run.

Target

If the Star ID lookup found RA/Dec, SHOBS-P usually places the Target marker (orange T) for you after the solve. The log says which star it used, and the click choice moves on to ● Comps (C1–C10) by itself (unless you already picked a choice on this image). Check that the marker is on your star. If it is not, or if no marker appeared:

  1. Pick ● Target in the tools row.
  2. Click the target on the image. The marker snaps to the nearest star.

Comparison stars: AAVSO sequence first

For AAVSO work, the best comps are the stars of the AAVSO comparison sequence. They are drawn as thin dashed cyan rings, labeled with their V magnitude without the decimal point (for example 112 means V 11.2). When you use them, the report names them by their AUID and other observers' data can be compared with yours.

  1. Pick ● Comps (C1–C10).
  2. Click a comp star. Thick green rings labeled S1, S2 … mark suggested comps: stars near the target's brightness and color, with AAVSO sequence stars preferred. A line per suggestion under the Stars panel says why it was picked.
  3. Look at the comp's row in the Stars panel at the right. Its name (AUID or catalog name) and magnitude fill in from the catalogs. The line under it says where the magnitude came from, whether the star is a known variable, and how far it is from the target in brightness and color.
  4. Repeat for more comps. Two to five good comps is typical.

Why several comps? With two or more, SHOBS-P averages them into an ensemble. One comp's noise or quirks then matter less. Chapter 11 shows how to check them afterwards and swap a bad one out without reducing again.

What to aim for:

  • Within about 1 magnitude of the target. A ⚠ note in the panel tells you when a comp is much brighter or fainter. If the field has only much fainter comps, see "Stacking when comps are weak" in chapter 8.
  • Not too close to the frame edge. A comp that drifts off the edge is left out of those frames only (chapter 8, "Comps near the frame edge"), but a comp missing in most frames is left out of the whole night.
  • Similar color (B−V within about 0.3; from 2.3.4 the ⚠ note and the suggestions both use 0.3). A comp of another color dims differently with airmass and leaves a slow trend.
  • Not a known variable. If you click one, SHOBS-P asks whether to use it anyway. Answer No unless you have a good reason.

If a comp has no magnitude in any catalog, the panel says so. Type its magnitude in the box beside the name, or pick another star.

To remove a comp, right-click its marker, or press ✕ in its row.

Check star

The check star is a steady star measured exactly like the target. It does not go into the comparison. If the check star stays flat, your comps and reduction are behaving. AAVSO requires a check star for any report made with two or more comps.

  1. Pick ● Check.
  2. Click a steady star, ideally another sequence star of similar brightness. Its name and magnitude fill in like a comp's.

Run photometry

  1. Check the aperture boxes (chapter 8 covers Suggest…). Use the same aperture sizes for every night of a series.
  2. Press Run photometry.

SHOBS-P may ask a few questions before it starts. The ones you will meet most in Variables mode:

  • With N comps the AAVSO report is an ensemble, and AAVSO requires a check star… You have no check star marked. Answer No and mark one, unless you will never report this night.
  • These have a name in the Stars panel but no marker on this image… A name is left over from an earlier night. Yes clears those names, Cancel lets you mark the stars.
  • … is a known or suspected variable … about a comp or the check. See above.
  • Run in Transits mode instead?, if your target hosts a known transiting planet (from the Planet (Transits) box, or looked up in the NASA Exoplanet Archive after the Star ID lookup) and a transit is predicted during your frames. You do not need to have used Transits mode first. For variable-star work, answer No (run in Variables mode anyway).
  • A ⚠ line in the log saying a marker is some pixels from the center of the star. See chapter 8.

Chapter 8 lists the other checks. The busy bar at the bottom of the window counts the frames as they are measured (for example Photometry… 12 of 38, about 1:20 left), and you can stop with Cancel run. Nothing is added to a series if you cancel.

After the run, SHOBS-P may ask more questions:

  • Add these N points as the night of … appears only if a series is open. See chapter 10.
  • A question about dew or haze, or about frames during a planet transit. See chapter 12, Flags and bad data.
  • A warning box listing frames skipped, comps left out of some frames because they went off the frame edge, and a comp or the check near the frame edge or a weak part of the flat (it says how far in a star must be to be safe, and for the check, which spare would have been better). The skipped-frame lines stay in orange on the Photometry page. See chapter 8, "Comps near the frame edge", and chapter 12, "Stars near the frame edge".
  • This night's meridian-flip step is … mag ⚠ … appears only when a series is open and saves itself, and the night has a large step at the flip that looks like an aperture problem rather than the star: the check star steps too, or the comp and check apertures jumped together at the flip (or, with no check star, the step is far beyond the night's scatter). Yes saves the night into the series file. No keeps the night on screen but leaves the file on disk as it was; no automatic save writes it later either. Press Save series to keep it, or Remove night… to take it out. Chapter 12 explains flip steps.

Then the Output – Variability page opens.

Weak comps? The Photometry page now shows a Stack row with a suggestion line. When it says "⚠ Comps are weak …", you can press Stack there and measure stacks of frames instead of single frames. The Points box on the Stack row then switches between Per frame and Stacked, and a Results use: … line says which set the Output page, the period search and the reports use. Chapter 8 explains it, under "Stacking when comps are weak". A stack hides changes faster than itself, so SHOBS-P keeps a stack to 1/50 of the VSX period.

Read the Output page

The page heading names the star, for example Output – Variability: SZ Lyn. The page has, from top to bottom:

  1. Three summary boxes: Series, Period and Color.
  2. The night table, one row per night.
  3. Cautions, in amber, each starting with ⚠.
  4. Three rows of settings (period search, quality control, tools) and one view switch. After a night is stacked, the row with Airmass-corrected night means ends with Results use: Stacked (N frames) or Results use: Per frame (see chapter 8).
  5. Four plots.
  6. Two button rows: Series and, under it, Results.

The Series box

  • Star: the star's name, and its AAVSO AUID when VSX knows one.
  • Points: how many points in total, and how many are used. The rest are flagged (chapter 12).
  • Nights and the time span.
  • Comps: the comparison stars in use.
  • Check: the check star and its scatter (σ). A check σ close to the target's scatter on a constant star tells you the noise floor of your setup. If the check star has no catalog magnitude, it says (instrumental).
  • Filter: the AAVSO filter code (for example TG for the green channel of a color camera, V for a V filter), and the camera binning.

The night table

Column What it shows
Night The night as an evening–morning date, e.g. 1–2 Oct 2026
JD The night's key, used in file notes and messages
Pts / used Points measured, and points used
Mean mag ± err The night's mean magnitude
Target σ The target's scatter that night
Check σ The check star's scatter that night
Side Pier side(s), when known; — when unknown, and always on an alt-az mount
Flip step The jump at a meridian flip; ⚠ when it is large
B−V The night's color, if you measured it
Flags Counts of flagged points, e.g. 12 cloud, 3 sat
Sent Where and when the night's report was sent, e.g. ✓ WebObs 2026-10-18, or — (chapter 15)
Note Your night note (chapter 10)

Click a row to zoom the light curve and check-star plot to that night. Click it again to show all nights.

Caution: While a night is highlighted, Save AAVSO report and Save light-curve CSV ask whether to save that night only or the whole series, and Save plot PNG saves what is on screen. Click the row again when you want the whole series.

The four plots

  • Top left, the light curve. One color per night. Gray × are flagged points, left out. Points ringed ○ come after a meridian flip. If you choose a Bin (for example Night or 30 min), black ◆ show the binned means. The Time axis box switches between JD, UTC and Local time. Local time is the site's (the Time zone in the Site box), and the axis says its offset, for example Local time (UTC−5). Hover over a point to see its details.
  • Top right, the phase plot. The used points folded on the best period from the periodogram. Its title says Folded only when the period is significant; otherwise it says Best peak (not significant). Chapter 13 explains.
  • Bottom left, the check star. It should be flat. Its title gives its scatter and catalog magnitude.
  • Bottom right, the periodogram (Lomb-Scargle). See chapter 13.

On a single night, the fold and periodogram are often grayed with a note that one night covers under two cycles. That is normal: a light curve from one night is still useful, but a period needs more nights.

What a good night looks like

  • The check star plot is flat, with no steps or trends.
  • Check σ is small and similar to the target's σ if the target is steady over the night.
  • Few flagged points, and no ⚠ cautions you do not understand. Chapter 23 explains each caution.

Save your results

The Results row has:

  • Save AAVSO report: see chapter 15. Open AAVSO WebObs… beside it opens the AAVSO's upload page. When VSX knows your star but has no AUID for it, an orange line under this row says ⚠ No AUID: WebObs will reject this report. … (chapter 15).
  • Save light-curve CSV: every point, including flagged ones with their flag word. A long series can take a few seconds; the CSV is written in the background, the busy bar says, for example, Saving the light-curve CSV (412 points)…, and then the status bar says ✔ Saved ….
  • Export…: a CSV for other programs, with a choice of time system (JD, HJD or BJD_TDB), magnitudes or flux, binning, and extra columns. After Export… the window itself says what it wrote, for example Exported 245 rows to …\GSC_03308-00462_HJD_UTC_mag.csv (from 2.3.3), or why it could not write (a file open in Excel, say).
  • Save plot PNG: the four plots with the summary boxes and cautions above them.
  • Save field image: a picture of the reference frame as shot (not turned), with yellow N and E arrows showing where north and east lie, a scale bar, and the target, comps and check star marked with their apertures. (On an unsolved image the arrows are replaced by "Orientation unknown (the image is not solved)".) The AAVSO chart of the same field is fetched and saved beside it when the AAVSO's chart server answers, so you can compare the two. Save AAVSO report saves both by itself, next to the report.
  • Refit period: runs the period search again.

If you set an Output folder on the Input page, these files go there without a dialog, and the report, CSV, plot and field image of one set of results share a number so they belong together. Without an Output folder, the save dialog opens in the folder you last saved to (this session) with the next free name already filled in. A name typed with a number after the extension (such as …_SHOBS-P.txt.2) is tidied to …_SHOBS-P_2.txt, and never overwrites a file. Chapter 22 lists every file SHOBS-P writes.

To keep this night for later, press Save series (chapter 10). Nothing is written to disk until you save.

Caution: A single night that was never saved as a series closes without a question. If you want to keep it, press Save series before you close SHOBS-P. (A series file with unsaved changes, or an unsaved series of two or more nights, is offered a save when you close the window.)

Previous: 8. Photometry page basics · Next: 10. Build a multi-night series

10. Build a multi-night series

Most variable-star questions need more than one night: a period, a time of minimum, a slow trend. In SHOBS-P a series holds all the nights of one star, in one mode, in a single file ending in .cvseries. This chapter shows how to start one, add nights to it, and look after it.

What a series keeps

A series file keeps every point of every night, with its flags, plus the settings you used: the Star ID, comps and check star with their names and catalog magnitudes, the aperture sizes, the period search range, your night notes, which comps you chose with Use comps…, your answers about dew/haze and planet transits, and which nights you have already sent in a report (the Sent column, chapter 15). Each night also keeps its mount (alt-az nights have no pier sides) and, for color images that were already debayered, the channel measured.

It does not keep star positions on the image, but it does keep each comp's and the check star's position on the sky. On every new night the solve places the target from its RA/Dec and, from 2.3.4, the series' comps and check star from their sky positions, so a series night is usually: calibrate, wait for the solve, Run photometry.

Starting a new series

The first night sets the rules for every night after it. Changing the aperture, the comps or the check star later means redoing every night, so choose them with care on night 1:

  1. Aperture. Press Suggest… (chapter 8) and keep the aperture radius at least about 1.5 × the FWHM (2 × for bright or defocused stars). Then keep it for the whole series. An aperture of only about 1.1 × the FWHM loses light that changes with the seeing (on GSC 03308-00462 it cost about 0.2 mag and SHOBS-FLUX called it not photometric).
  2. Colour. Tick Also measure R and B on night 1, so the series has the target's own B−V. The suggested comps then use it (see "How S1–S4 are chosen" in chapter 8).
  3. Comps. Within about 0.2 in B−V and about 1 mag of the target, steady (check them with Comp health… after a few nights, chapter 11), and well inside the frame, so a small framing shift on another night does not push them off.
  4. Check star. Colour-matched too: a check of another colour drifts with airmass on hazy nights and looks like a problem that is not there.
  5. Linear limit. Comps and the check under the linear ADU limit on the sharpest frames, not just the reference frame: a night of better seeing makes the same star peak higher.
  6. Lock it in. Press Save series. If you change any of these later, redo every night with the new choice.

Start a series

  1. Reduce the first night as in chapter 9.
  2. On the Output page, press Save series.
  3. Choose a folder and a name. The default name is the Star ID with spaces turned into underscores, for example SZ_Lyn.cvseries.

The status bar then reminds you: Next night, load this file before reducing.

Add a night

The order matters: load first, reduce second.

  1. On the Input page, press Load series… and open the series file. (The Output page has the same button, Load series.)
  2. A message tells you how many points and nights were loaded, and that the comps and check are placed on their stars after the image is solved. If the Photometry page already has a solved image (say you pressed New reduction and then loaded the series), SHOBS-P solves it again at once, so the target, the series' comps and check and the suggested comps are placed without pressing Solve again.
  3. The line at the top of the Input page now reads something like Series: SZ_Lyn.cvseries — 8 nights, last 13–14 Oct. After the run you'll be asked to add the night.
  4. Point the Input page at the new night's bias, dark, flat and lights, and blink and calibrate as usual.
  5. On the Photometry page, wait for the solve. From 2.3.4 it places the target, the same comps and the same check star as before, and each row says ✓ … placed after the solve …; magnitude … kept as loaded (the series' magnitude is kept, never replaced by a catalog's). A star that is not on tonight's frame says so in its row (⚠ … is not on this frame): click another star, or clear the slot. A name with no saved position (a series from before 2.2.3) asks you to press Enter in its box or click the star. Clicking a comp that the series already knows puts it back into its old slot. If the series has a Use comps… choice, the comp rows show the comps in use; the series' other comps are found after the solve and still measured on every frame as spares, so a later Use comps… can bring them back (chapter 11).
  6. Use the same aperture sizes as the other nights.
  7. Press Run photometry.
  8. When asked Add these N points as the night of … to the M points already in the series?, answer Yes.

Yes adds the night and keeps every earlier night. Because the series file is open, SHOBS-P saves it at once (the status bar says Auto-saved to …). The one exception is a night with a large meridian-flip step that looks like an aperture problem: SHOBS-P asks before saving it (chapter 9, "After the run"), and if you answer No, the file is left as it was until you press Save series.

Stacked nights. If you stack a night (chapter 8, "Stacking when comps are weak"), the series keeps only the set the Points box has chosen, Per frame or Stacked. Switching the Points box saves the series again with the chosen set, as a run does. Loading or closing a series puts the Points box back to Per frame. Nights of one series can differ: a stacked night carries its stack size and effective exposure, a per-frame night none.

No starts a new series with this night only. The old file on disk is not changed, but the new series is not saved until you press Save series and give it a new name.

Tip: If the Input page says No series loaded — this run will be a single night, you forgot to load the series. You can still add the night afterwards: save tonight as its own series, load the main series, and use Add series file… (below).

The warnings in the add-night question

The add-night question may carry one or more warnings. Each one is about something that can put a step between nights that is not the star.

Careful: the series is V and this night is TG. Different filters do not belong in one series. Answer No unless you mean to mix them.

Careful: this night used different stars than the series. It lists the series' comps and check and tonight's. Different comps put each night on its own zero point. Usually it is better to answer No, go back to the Photometry page, mark the series' comps, and run again. If a comp was missing tonight (off the frame, for example), you can add the night anyway and then use Use comps… (chapter 11) to pick a set every night shares. Comps that simply follow the series' Use comps… choice do not count as different stars: the night is put into the series' comp layout by name.

One star, one name: … The same star appears under another catalog's name tonight (for example a Gaia name tonight and a Tycho name in the series). Yes uses the series' names and catalog magnitudes for this night, so every night stays on one zero point. This is almost always what you want.

Careful: this night comes from other equipment or another observer. Untransformed nights from different instruments can sit tenths of a magnitude apart. A separate series is usually better: answer No.

Careful: this night was reduced differently from the series. It lists what changed, for example the binning, the aperture, or the channel of already-debayered color images (for example channel of the color images: green (TG) on this night, red (TR) on …). Nights reduced differently can sit on different zero points. Consider reducing again with the series' settings.

The night of … is already in the series. Yes replaces that night's points with this run. The other nights stay.

Night notes

A note travels with a night: in the night table's Note column, in the light-curve CSV and in exports. Use it for anything you want to remember: thin cloud after 2 a.m., focuser slipped, moon nearby.

  1. On the Output page, press Night notes….
  2. Type the number of the night from the list.
  3. Type the note, or leave it empty to remove one.

Other series tools

All of these are in the Series row on the Output page. When the series has a file, each of them saves the file straight away.

  • Add series file… merges another saved series into the one that is open, for example a night you saved on its own. You see how many points and which nights will be added, with warnings if the comps or reduction differ. Series from Variables and Transits modes cannot be merged.
  • Remove night… removes one night. The list shows each night by its dates and key, for example 22–23 Oct 2026 (JD2461336). Type its number and confirm. This cannot be undone, except from the backup copy described below.
  • Close series sets the series aside. The next run is a single night again. The file on disk is not changed.

In Transits mode, a series of one planet with two or more nights can be fitted together: Fit the series' nights… on the Transit fit page's Results row opens the Multi-night window with every night of the series (chapter 17). The series also remembers each night's saved fit CSV and AAVSO exoplanet report, and the alternative ephemeris you typed there.

If you load another series, close this one, switch modes, or close the SHOBS-P window while there are unsaved changes, SHOBS-P offers to save first: Yes saves, No goes on without saving, Cancel keeps the series open. When you close the window, it asks only if a series file has unsaved changes or a series of two or more nights has never been saved.

Switching to another star

Press New reduction on the Input page before you start on another star (chapter 5). It closes the open series (offering a save first) and clears the old star's target, planet, comps, check and watch stars, Use comps… choice and rejected frames, and puts the Output page's Notes back to the default ("OSC reduced; not transformed"). Night notes stay with their nights in the series file. SHOBS-P also offers a new reduction by itself when the lights you choose are another star. This keeps one star's comps from being applied to another star's night.

As a last guard, a Use comps… choice whose stars were never measured on a new night is not applied to it. The log says, for example, "✖ Use comps choice (…) not applied to …: … were not measured on any of its frames (another field's comps?). … for another star, use New reduction on the Input page."

When you load a series of a different star, SHOBS-P clears the current target's comps, check star and watch stars, so names from one field never end up in another. If it cannot tell from the positions whether the two names are the same star, it asks; No (the default) keeps what is marked.

Report saves and the series file

Saving a report while a series file is open (Save AAVSO report, an AAVSO exoplanet report, Save for ExoClock, an ExoFOP package, or marking nights sent) notes the report in the series, so the series file is rewritten. This is never silent: the log says "Series file updated: …", and the save's status line ends with Series file updated: SZ_Lyn.cvseries.

The series keeps its own site (latitude, longitude, elevation) and AAVSO observer code; a report save does not replace them with the Input page's. When the two differ (for example after you switched to another profile), SHOBS-P asks once:

This series was observed from 35.37, -81.96, 300 m, observer JABA; the Input page has 40, -100, 500 m, no observer code. Keep the series' site?

Yes (usually right) keeps the series' site and observer code in the file. No writes the Input page's into the series. The answer is kept for the rest of the session for that series. Save series always writes what the Input page shows.

The field image of a loaded series

Save field image draws the frame on the Photometry page, so it needs a reduction of the series' own star. After Load series… of another star than the one you last reduced (or with no frame at all), it does not draw the other star's field under this series' name; it says "Reduce a night of SZ Lyn first; the field image comes from a reduction (the Photometry page's frame)." Calibrate a night of the series' star, then save the field image.

The backup copy (_prev)

Every save replaces the series file. To protect you from a mistake, the first time SHOBS-P writes a series file in a session (whether you pressed Save series or it saved on its own), it first renames the old file to the same name with _prev added, for example SZ_Lyn_prev.cvseries. Later saves in the same session leave that copy alone.

So _prev is always the series as it was before this session. To go back to it:

  1. Close SHOBS-P, or close the series.
  2. In your file manager, rename or copy SZ_Lyn_prev.cvseries to a new name.
  3. Load that file.

There is only one _prev. The next session replaces it. For a long campaign, it is worth keeping your own dated copies now and then.

Caution: A single night that was never saved as a series closes without a question, and its points are lost. If your series has a file, adding a night saves it for you; a new series must be saved with Save series.

Previous: 9. Reduce a variable-star night · Next: 11. Check your comparison stars

11. Check your comparison stars

Every magnitude you report is measured against your comparison stars. If one of them is quietly variable, or sits on a dust shadow in the flat, it puts its own changes into your target's light curve. SHOBS-P gives you two tools to catch that and fix it without reducing any night again: Comp health… and Use comps…, both in the Series row of the Output page.

Spare comps: measured just in case

When the image is solved, each run also measures up to eight spare comps: catalog stars near your comps' brightness that are not known variables. You do not pick them. They show on the Photometry image as small light-blue diamonds (◇ Spare comp in the legend).

Spares cost nothing on the night, and they give you choices later. If a comp turns out bad after ten nights, you can swap in a spare that was measured on all ten.

If the field is thin, SHOBS-P widens the brightness window to find spares and says so in the log. Such spares can be much fainter than the target. Treat them with care (see below).

Comp health

Comp health… measures each comp, each spare and the check star against the average of the other stars in the same frame. Clouds and airmass affect all stars alike and cancel out. What remains is each star's own behavior.

  1. On the Output page, press Comp health….
  2. The Comparison star health window opens with a plot and a table.

With two or more nights, the plot shows each star's nightly median, relative to its own overall median. A steady star is a flat line. Spares are drawn dashed. With only one night loaded, the plot shows each star through the night instead, and a purple dashed line marks a meridian flip.

Click a row in the table to highlight that star's curve.

The table
