Column Meaning
Star The star's name
Role comp, spare or check
Night offset How far the star's nightly level moves (mag)
Scatter in night The star's typical scatter within a night (mag)
Flip step Its jump at a meridian flip
Both pier sides Whether it reads the same on both pier sides, over all nights with a flip
Nights Nights it was measured
Over linear limit Frames where its brightest raw pixel reached the linear ADU limit, for example ⚠ 12 of 99 frames (chapter 8). Without a linear limit set, the column is Over saturation and counts against the saturation level. — for nights measured before 2.2.19, which kept no per-star peaks
Pixel (x, y) Its median position on the frames
From target Its distance from the target in pixels (does not change with framing)
Verdict SHOBS-P's judgment

Verdicts

  • steady — good. On one night: steady tonight (one night).
  • SUSPECT: drifts night to night — its level moves between nights more than the others'. Replace it.
  • SUSPECT: jumps … at the flip — it changes at the meridian flip while the others do not. Often a flat-field problem at its position on one side of the pier.
  • SUSPECT: noisier than the others tonight — perhaps a faint star, a close neighbor, or a bad pixel.
  • pair drifts; add a 3rd star to tell which — with only two stars, SHOBS-P can see that they disagree but not which one is at fault. Add a spare or a third comp. (On one night the same pair reads one night; steady pair or one night; pair jumps at the flip, add a 3rd star to tell which.)
  • too few points — not enough data to judge.

Each star is put on its own zero point before the others are averaged, and the stars that make up the reference are chosen night by night (a star in most of a night's frames counts for that night). From 2.3.4, a star missing from part of a night therefore no longer shifts the other stars' level for that night (before, a comp on half the frames of one night could fake a 0.05 mag night offset in all the others).

When a star is clearly SUSPECT, SHOBS-P leaves it out of the reference for the others and says so (Left out of the reference so their drift does not echo in the steady stars: …). Otherwise one bad star makes all the good ones look as if they drift the other way.

On an alt-az mount there are no pier sides or flips, so Flip step and Both pier sides have nothing to show. The pier-side line under the table names the stars that read the same on both sides of the pier (good comps for a campaign with meridian flips) and those that step at the flip. If the target steps at the flip while steady comps do not, look at the field position (flat, a ghost, a bright star's halo) rather than the comps.

Watch a spare

If a spare looks interesting, select its row and press Watch selected spare. It becomes a watch star with its own light curve (Output page, Watch stars…). Only spares can be added here: comps and the check star are already measured on every run.

Use comps: change the comps without reducing again

SHOBS-P stores each comp's and spare's measurement in every frame. Use comps… recomputes every point of the series from a different set of comps, in seconds.

  1. On the Output page, press Use comps….
  2. The Comparison stars in use window lists every comp and spare with its Catalog mag, Nights measured (for example 7 of 8), Night offset, Scatter in night and Comp health verdict (SUSPECT in red).
  3. Tick the stars you want in the comparison. Untick a SUSPECT comp; tick a steady spare measured on every night.
  4. Press Apply.

The light curve, period and summary update. If the series has a file, it is saved with the new choice. The log lists the comps now in use, and nights you add later use the same choice.

The Photometry page follows the choice too: its comp rows and markers change to the comps in use. The comps you left out are still measured on every new night, as spares, so choosing them again later never leaves a night without them (chapter 8).

To return to the comps the nights were reduced with, open Use comps… again and press Back to the original comps, then Apply.

Things to know:

  • Pick stars measured on every night. A frame missing only some of the chosen comps is kept: its point is worked out from the chosen comps that are there, put on the night's scale, so no step appears. Only frames with none of the chosen comps (or nights that never measured a chosen star) are flagged nocomp and left out; SHOBS-P tells you how many before it applies. The log line "Comps in use: … N points re-derived" adds "(M of them from the comps present in that frame)".
  • Dropping a comp that cost frames. If the run skipped frames because every comp was lost in them (for example a one-comp night whose comp drifted off the frame edge) and your new choice drops those comps, the Photometry page says "⚠ N frames were skipped because of …, which is no longer a comp — Run photometry again to measure them." Use comps only recalculates frames that were measured; press Measure skipped frames on that line to measure the skipped ones with the comps now in use (chapter 8).
  • Every chosen star needs a catalog magnitude. If one has none, you get No catalog magnitude for …. You can type one in its Catalog mag box (below).
  • The check star cannot be a comp.
  • Colors (B−V) stay tied to the comps each night was reduced with.
  • Spares from a widened window are marked in the Role column with how much fainter than the target they are. Their noise can dominate the ensemble. Prefer comps within about a magnitude of the target.

Correct a catalog magnitude

The Catalog mag column is editable, for every comp, every spare, and the check star (its row says check (not a comp)). Use it when a star's catalog value is wrong, or when you want the value from another catalog than the campaign's:

  1. Type the new magnitude in the star's Catalog mag box.
  2. Press Apply.

Every point that used that comp shifts by the change in its comps' catalog mean; nothing is measured again. The new value is used for the whole series, the Photometry page's boxes for the same star follow, and an open series file is saved. The log lists each change, for example "Catalog magnitudes changed for the whole series: … 8.711 → 8.728. 412 points re-derived (shift +0.0042 mag)." A box that does not hold a number gives "…" is not a catalog magnitude.

Tip: Changing comps changes your magnitudes. Do it before you report, and report the whole series with one comp set. The AAVSO report names the comps actually in use.

The ensemble zero-point warning

An ensemble of comps is only as good as its catalog magnitudes. If one comp's catalog magnitude is off, the whole ensemble shifts the target's level. SHOBS-P checks this: it compares the level your chosen comps give with the level from the comp(s) nearest the target's brightness, night by night.

When they differ by more than 0.025 mag, you see a caution on the Output page (and in Comp health…):

⚠ Ensemble zero point: the 4 comps in use put the target off the level the comp(s) nearest its brightness give (…) by more than 0.025 mag (…). Compare both sets in Use comps… before reporting.

What to do:

  1. Open Use comps….
  2. Try the set without the comp that is far from the target in brightness or color, and Apply.
  3. Compare the light curves and the check star. Keep the set that gives the flatter check star and the smaller night-to-night spread (shown under the night table).

The caution does not stop you from reporting. It tells you the answer depends on the comps, which is worth knowing.

Previous: 10. Build a multi-night series · Next: 12. Flags and bad data

12. Flags and bad data

Not every frame deserves to be in your light curve. A passing cloud, a saturated star or a dewed-up corrector can each make points that are not the star. SHOBS-P flags such points rather than deleting them. Flagged points stay in the series, are drawn as gray × on the plots, and are left out of the period search and the AAVSO report. You can always change your mind.

This chapter explains each flag, how to change the cloud limit, the dew/haze question, frames taken during a planet transit, and pier-side steps.

Leaving flagged points out

On the Output page, the box Leave flagged points (sat, drift, cloud) out of the period and report (planet-transit frames never go in a report) is ticked by default. Leave it ticked.

If you untick it, flagged points go into the period search and the AAVSO report too, each with its flag word in the report's notes. Planet-transit frames stay out of reports either way.

The light-curve CSV (Save light-curve CSV) always contains every point, flagged or not, with its flag word, so you can study the flagged points elsewhere.

The flags

The night table's Flags column counts them per night, for example 12 cloud, 3 sat.

Shown as Meaning What to do
sat The target's peak reached the saturation level (raw sat ADU on the Photometry page) Shorter exposures next time, or slight defocus. Check that raw sat ADU fits your camera. (Frames over the linear ADU limit are not flagged; the log and Comp health count them, chapter 8.)
compsat A comp's peak reached the saturation level Choose a fainter comp.
drift The target was found farther from its expected position than the aperture radius Often trailing, wind or a bad alignment. Look at those frames in Blink.
noflux No light measured for the target Usually a frame with no stars (cloud, dome).
cloud The comps' light fell more than the cloud limit below the night's median See below.
cloud edge A point just next to a cloud run, far off the night's level Left out with the cloud frames.
dew/haze? All stars faded together, smoothly, with a flat sky See "Dew and haze" below.
in planet b transit The frame falls in a known planet's predicted transit See "Planet transits" below.
nocomp None of the comps chosen with Use comps… was measured on this frame (a frame missing only some of them is kept, from the comps present) See chapter 11.

Cloud

A cloud dims all the stars, but not quite equally: the target and comps pass under the cloud's edge at slightly different times. So SHOBS-P flags frames where the comps' light drops too far below the night's median.

The limit is the box Cloud flag: comp light more than … % below the night's median. The default is 20.

To change it:

  1. Type a new percentage in the box.
  2. Press Apply.

The flags of every night are worked out again. A lower number is stricter (more frames flagged). A higher number keeps more frames but lets more cloud-affected points in. On a night with thin, uniform cirrus, a higher limit can save useful data; on a night with patchy cloud, a lower one cleans up the light curve. Watch the check-star plot as you change it: the right limit is the one that keeps the check star flat.

The limit is saved with the series.

Dew and haze (transmission loss)

Not all lost light is cloud. When dew forms on the optics, or a uniform haze comes over, every star fades by the same amount, slowly and smoothly, and the sky does not brighten. Differential photometry handles that well: the magnitudes stay right, only the noise grows.

SHOBS-P looks for such stretches. It calls them transmission loss (dew/haze?): the question mark is because it cannot be sure that a smooth cloud bank is not to blame.

The Keep question after a run

When a night has such a stretch, SHOBS-P asks after photometry:

N frames of … lost light (…, down to 0.94 mag), but every measured star (comps, check, spares) faded by the same amount, smoothly, with a flat sky: transmission loss (dew/haze?), not cloud.

Use these points in the light curve (errors scaled for the lost light), or leave them out?

  • Use these points keeps the points. Their error bars grow with the light lost, and each kept point also gets the extra star-to-star scatter measured in that stretch, so the errors stay honest.
  • Leave them out (the default; also Escape or closing the window) leaves them flagged, like cloud.

If you are unsure, choose Leave them out. You can change it later. Each answer is written to the log with the night and the time, for example "… transmission loss (dew/haze?) question (after photometry) answered “Leave them out” at 2026-10-16 23:41:07."

If only one star was measured, SHOBS-P cannot tell dew from cloud, so it does not ask: the points are left out, and the log says so.

Changing your answer later

  1. If the series has more than one night, click the night in the night table first.
  2. Press Transmission loss….
  3. The message says how many frames, when, how deep, and whether they are now used in the light curve or left out. Answer the question again with Use these points or Leave them out.

Your answer is saved with the series. If no night has transmission loss at the current cloud limit, SHOBS-P says so.

Planet transits in a variable's frames

Some variable-star targets host transiting planets (HD 219134, for example). During a transit the star dims a little for reasons that have nothing to do with its variability. SHOBS-P follows a simple rule for such campaigns: no data while a planet transits.

In Variables mode, after each run and after Load series…, SHOBS-P looks up the Star ID's transiting planets at the NASA Exoplanet Archive, in the background. If frames fall inside a predicted transit, it asks:

Tonight's run (…): 5–6 Oct 2026: 9 frames fall in planet b's transit (frames 05:02–05:25 local time (UTC−4); transit 05:17–06:14 ±1 min).

Leave those frames out (flagged "transit", like cloud)?

  • Yes (the default) flags those frames transit. They stay in the series but never go in a report.
  • No keeps them.

The message also says what was assumed, for example T14 not published; 2.4 h assumed, or plus 15 min for JD vs BJD when the target's RA/Dec are not known (the window is widened to be safe).

Nothing is said if the star has no known transiting planet, if no frame falls in a transit, or if the archive cannot be reached.

To check or change it later:

  1. Click the night in the night table if the series has more than one night with transit frames.
  2. Press Planet transits….

If no night has been checked yet, the button runs the check on every night. Your answer is saved in the series file at once.

Pier side and meridian flips

After a meridian flip, the camera sees the field rotated by 180°. Each star now falls on a different part of the sensor and the flat. Small flat-field errors can then make a step in the light curve at the flip, even though the star did not change.

SHOBS-P finds the flip in each night (from the frames' ROTATOR or PIERSIDE header, or from the image itself) and rings points after the flip with ○ in the light curve. With ROTATOR readings, camera angles within 10° of each other count as one pier side (30° and 37° are one side, 210° and 217° the other), since the angle a solve or the camera software reports can wander a few degrees from night to night without the camera being turned. The Flip step column shows each night's step, with ⚠ when it is large.

You have three ways to take steps out. Each is a checkbox on the Output page. They affect the plots, the period search and Export… files. They do not change the stored points or the AAVSO report.

  • Line up flip only (keep nights) shifts the after-flip part of each night to match its before-flip part. Night-to-night changes are kept. Use it for one night, or when flips happen at different times each night.
  • Pier side as segments lines up all the frames taken on one pier side with all the frames on the other, across the whole series. Use it for a campaign where every night has a flip. If the pier side is unknown on some nights (no ROTATOR, no PIERSIDE, no flip), those nights are left as they are, and a caution says so.
  • Line up segments (nights, meridian flip) sets every night and every flip segment to the same level. It removes all night-to-night changes, including real ones. Use it only to search for a period much shorter than a night.

If more than one is ticked, Line up segments wins over Pier side as segments, which wins over Line up flip only.

An alt-az mount has no meridian flips and no pier sides, so there is nothing to line up: the Side column shows —, and Pier side as segments says the mount is alt-az (no pier sides).

When the series has nights on both pier sides and none of these is ticked, a caution tells you how much each side reads against the other and suggests Pier side as segments.

When the flip step is the apertures, not the star

A step can also come from the apertures landing beside their stars after the flip. SHOBS-P checks each flipped frame's alignment on up to 15 stars' own centers, so this is rare now. After each run it also compares how the comp and check apertures moved at the flip. When they were off their stars together while the target's was not, the log says so:

⚠ After the meridian flip the C1 and check apertures were off their stars by about (+3.1, −2.4) px together, while the target's was not: an aperture-placement error at the flip, not a change in the stars …

The check star then looks normal and hides the problem. Look at comp1_x/comp1_y and check_x/check_y in the light-curve CSV; if they sit off the stars, reduce the frames after the flip on their own.

Such a night, or one whose check star steps along with the target, is not saved into an open series without asking (chapter 9, "After the run"). A step the check star and comps do not share is taken as the star's, and the series is saved as usual.

Caution: On a star that changes a lot within a night, a "flip step" read from the target's light curve is mostly the star changing. SHOBS-P warns you in that case. Look at Comp health… instead: it shows each comp's step at the flip (chapter 11).

Stars near the frame edge

After a run, SHOBS-P may warn that a comp or the check star sat near the frame edge or a weak part of the flat. Such stars can drift out of the good part of the image as the night goes on. The warning says how far in a star must be, for example "comes within 9 px of the frame edge (stars at least 66 px in are safe)". Choose other stars next time if you can. The warning shares one box with the frames skipped and the comps left out of some frames (chapter 8).

When the check star leaves the frame in some frames, those frames have no check value. The line says which frames, and it names the spare nearest the check's brightness that stayed farther in all night, for example "check Gaia DR3 4609129005355614848 comes within 0 px of the frame edge (stars at least 66 px in are safe): … The check star was off the frame in 19 frames (194-195, 197-213), which have no check value. A better check star stays farther in: TYC 2620-391-1 (V 12.21), 0.9 mag brighter than Gaia DR3 4609129005355614848, at least 194 px in all night (measured as a spare). Pick it as the check next time."

A spare near the edge gets only a line in the Log: spares are measured but not used, so there is nothing to do.

Frames without the target

When the first frames of a night miss the target (the telescope still settling, for example), SHOBS-P makes the first frame that holds it the reference (chapter 8). The run then skips every frame on which the target is off the frame or too near its edge, and lists them at the end: "SZ Lyn not on the frame in 5 of 142 images (frames 1–5); those frames were skipped." When that is more than a fifth of the night, the line starts with ⚠ and adds a hint: a run of frames at the start points to the pointing, scattered ones to tracking or clouds.

Centring of defocused stars

On a defocused (donut) field, the log reports after each run how much each aperture's center jittered from frame to frame, for example "Centring (defocused stars): frame-to-frame jitter of each aperture's center (rms): target 0.12 px, C1 0.15 px, …". Over 0.5 px it becomes a ⚠ caution: the aperture cuts a different part of the donut each time, which adds noise the comps do not cancel. Try a larger aperture (Suggest…).

Previous: 11. Check your comparison stars · Next: 13. Look for a period

13. Look for a period

SHOBS-P searches every light curve for a period with a Lomb-Scargle periodogram, the standard tool for unevenly spaced data. It runs on its own each time the series changes. This chapter explains how to set the search, how to read the result, and, most of all, how SHOBS-P decides whether a peak is real. Ground-based data from one site are full of false periods, and SHOBS-P is built to tell you when it does not trust one.

Where to look

On the Output – Variability page:

  • The Period summary box gives the numbers.
  • The top-right plot is the light curve folded on the best period.
  • The bottom-right plot is the periodogram: power against period, on a log scale. The red line marks the best period.

Set the search

  1. In Period search (days), type the shortest and longest period to try. The defaults are 0.02 to 20 days. (Before 2.2.19 the shortest was 0.05 d, which missed many short-period pulsators. A series saved with that old default now searches from 0.02 d, and the log says so.)
  2. Press Enter, or click into another box. The search runs again with the new range. (It does not rerun while you are still typing, or when you leave a box without changing it.) Refit period also runs it again.

Choose a range that fits your star and your data:

  • The shortest period needs frames closer together than half of it.
  • The longest period your data can really test is about half the span of the data.
  • When the VSX period, or a period you marked, lies outside the range, the Period box says so with a Range ⚠ line and a caution, and a button such as Search 0.02–20 d instead widens the search in one click. A period longer than half the data span gets no button: no search can find it yet, so add nights. On the periodogram, longer periods are shaded gray and labeled longer than the data can test. The search still runs there, but a peak in the gray zone is not to be trusted.

Period from

The Period from box chooses what the search uses: Every point, or 5 min bins, 10 min bins (the default) or 30 min bins. Bins make each night count by the hours you observed, not by how many frames you took, and they stop thousands of correlated frames from making a peak look more certain than it is. Keep the default unless you are after a period of less than about an hour; then use 5 min bins or Every point. If there are fewer than 8 bins, SHOBS-P uses every point. The Period box's Basis line says what was used.

Bins smear a period shorter than about ten bins. So when the VSX or a marked period is that short, or the best peak found from bins is, SHOBS-P searches every point instead, and a Bins ⚠ line in the Period box says so (for example "⚠ 0.039 d spans only 5.6 10-min bins: searched every point instead").

Check-star correction

Check-star correction (fold and period) is ticked by default. It uses the check star to take out any shift in level between nights, or between the two sides of a meridian flip. If the check star moves by 0.02 mag on one night, the target's values for that night are moved back by the same amount before the search. This only works with a good, steady check star. If your check star is doubtful, untick the box and see whether the period survives.

The pier-side and line-up checkboxes from chapter 12 also affect the search.

Read the Period box

Line Meaning
Best (or Peak) The best period in days and hours. Peak … (not significant) means SHOBS-P does not trust it.
Power The peak's height (0 to 1), and its FAP, false-alarm probability, worked out two ways: from the points, and counting nights
Basis Every point, or how many bins
Alias The one-day alias of the best period (see below)
Epoch The time of the fold's zero phase
Fold Whether the check-star correction is on
VSX The catalog period, when VSX has one. Also drawn as a green dashed line on the periodogram.

Further lines appear when they apply: Check ⚠ depends on … alone, Airmass ⚠ nightly means follow airmass, and Marked … for periods you asked about.

What "significant" means here

A period is shown as Best, and the fold is titled Folded, only when all of these hold:

  1. Its false-alarm probability is 1% or less, counting the points.
  2. It is still 1% or less counting nights. Points from one night share that night's offset (a slightly different zero point, sky, or airmass). A thousand points from five nights are not a thousand independent measurements. SHOBS-P works out a second FAP that allows for this.
  3. No single night carries it. SHOBS-P repeats the search leaving out each night in turn. If the period disappears without one night, that night alone made it, usually by being offset from the others.
  4. The data cover at least two cycles on a single night.
  5. It does not follow airmass (see below).

When one night is too short for even the shortest period searched (under two of its cycles; a 50-minute night with the search starting at 0.02 d), there is nothing to test. The Period box then holds one line and no peak:

  • Too short for a period search: one night of 0.8 h covers under two cycles of even the shortest period searched (0.02 d = 0.5 h). Add nights, or observe longer.

The fold and the periodogram say the same; no best period is marked.

If any test fails, the Period box starts with a plain sentence saying why, for example:

  • No reliable period yet: one short night covers under two cycles of the best peak. Add nights. (On such a night the day-alias and search-edge ⚠ marks are left out: the peak is not reliable either way.)
  • No reliable period: the peak depends on … alone (often an offset between nights, not the star).
  • No reliable period: the peak follows the nights' airmass; it is not shown to be the star.
  • No significant period: counting nights, the peak's false-alarm probability is … (a night's points share offsets).
  • No significant period: the star looks constant at this precision (scatter … mag per point, … mag night to night).

The last one is a result too. For a suspected variable, "constant to 0.005 mag" is worth knowing.

Caution: A periodogram always has a highest peak. Best peak (not significant) is not the star's period. Do not report it as one.

Aliases

Observing from one site, you see the star at about the same time each evening. That regular gap of one day creates false peaks: at 1 day, ½ day and ⅓ day themselves, and at aliases of the true period. If the true frequency is f cycles per day, peaks also appear at f + 1 and f − 1.

SHOBS-P helps in two ways:

  • The Alias line gives the one-day alias of the best period. If the periodogram has two peaks of similar height, one at the best period and one at its alias, your data cannot yet tell them apart.
  • If the best period is within 5% of 1, ½ or ⅓ day (solar or sidereal), it adds ⚠ near a day alias and a caution: treat it as a likely sampling alias unless a single night shows the variation itself.

Ways to break an alias:

  • Observe at a different time of night: start earlier, or run later.
  • Make longer runs, so that one night covers more of the cycle.
  • Combine with another observer at a different longitude (as a separate series; see chapter 10 about mixing equipment).

Mark periods

To see how a particular period fares, for example the VSX period or an alias:

  1. Type one or more periods in Mark periods (days, comma separated), for example 0.12053, 0.13790.
  2. Press Show.

Each appears as a purple dotted line on the periodogram, and the Period box gives its power (Marked … d → power …).

Other cautions about the period

  • ⚠ at the edge: the best period is at the end of your search range. The true period may lie beyond it, or this is a slow trend. Widen the range and press Refit period.
  • The data span only … d, under 1.5 cycles of the best period; it is not pinned down yet. Add nights.

The airmass check

At higher airmass, a star of a different color from the comps dims a little more or less than they do. If some nights were observed lower in the sky than others, their means can differ for that reason alone, and a period search may fit the pattern of which nights ran low.

SHOBS-P compares each night's mean with that night's median airmass. If they follow each other, the Period box shows Airmass ⚠ nightly means follow airmass (… mag per airmass) and a caution gives the slope and the night-to-night spread before and after taking the trend out. SHOBS-P then tries the search with the trend taken out, and without the high-airmass nights. If the period does not survive, it is demoted (; period demoted) and shown as not significant.

The check needs airmass: from the frames' headers, or worked out from the target's RA/Dec and your site. Without them, the log says the check was skipped. Type your site on the Input page to enable it.

Airmass-corrected night means (display only)

Tick Airmass-corrected night means (plot only, never in reports) to see the effect on the light curve. Small filled orange diamonds show each night's mean; large hollow ones show the same mean with the airmass trend taken out. Hover over one to see both values. These values are only drawn: they never go into the period search, a report or a CSV.

Previous: 12. Flags and bad data · Next: 14. Time a maximum or minimum

14. Time a maximum or minimum

For eclipsing binaries and short-period pulsators, the most valuable single number from a night is often the time of a minimum or maximum. Compared with the time predicted by the star's elements (period and epoch), it gives O − C: observed minus calculated. Collected over months and years, O − C values show whether the period is changing.

SHOBS-P times an extremum from one night of your series and keeps the results in a CSV file per star, which grows night by night into an O − C diagram.

Before you start

  • The night must cover the maximum or minimum, with points well before and after it. SHOBS-P needs at least 12 usable points.
  • For the O − C, you need the star's elements. SHOBS-P can fetch them from VSX.
  • For heliocentric or barycentric times (HJD, BJD_TDB), the target's RA/Dec must be on the Input page. They are filled in when the Star ID is looked up. Without them, times stay in JD, and the O−C is taken in JD (up to 8 minutes off).

Measure a time

  1. On the Output page, press Time of max/min…. The Time of maximum / minimum window opens.
  2. In Night, choose the night. It starts on the highlighted night, or the last one.
  3. In Type, choose maximum or minimum. SHOBS-P sets this from the VSX variable type: minimum for eclipsing binaries, maximum for pulsators and the rest.
  4. Leave Half-window (min) blank. SHOBS-P then picks how much of the light curve on each side of the extremum to fit. Type a number of minutes only if the automatic window catches something it should not, such as a nearby shoulder of the light curve.
  5. Check Period (d) and Epoch. They hold VSX's elements from the Star ID lookup; if they are empty, press Elements from VSX. For a star with times already saved in its times CSV (below), Elements from saved times (from 2.3.3) fills Period with the one used last and Epoch with the first saved maximum (or minimum, by the Type box), and the label says from the times CSV. That is often better than VSX's elements, whose epoch can be decades old. A time saved against them is marked saved in the CSV's elements column. The label beside it shows VSX when the elements are VSX's, or typed when you typed your own. VSX epochs are HJD, so leave the system box on HJD for VSX elements. Choose BJD if your elements are in BJD.
  6. Press Measure.

The O − C beside each saved time is the one stored when it was saved, against the elements used then; the O − C diagram recomputes every point against the elements in the boxes. They differ when the elements have changed, as for the first time here (saved against an earlier period).

Read the result

The result text gives, for each extremum found that night:

  • Tmin or Tmax: the adopted time in HJD with its error in days and minutes, plus the same moment in BJD_TDB and JD.
  • polynomial deg N: the time from a local polynomial fit, with how many points and what window it used. This is the adopted method.
  • Kwee-van Woerden: the classic method for eclipse timings, shown for comparison (it assumes mirror-image branches, so on a lopsided maximum it can read a minute off).
  • Fourier: the time from a Fourier series fitted at the period, also for comparison.
  • O−C: in days and minutes, with the cycle number E and the calculated time C it was compared against. For a minimum, E can be a half cycle (a secondary eclipse).
  • Any notes and ⚠ warnings.

On the left plot, the red curve is the polynomial fit and the red line the adopted time. The blue dashed line is Kwee-van Woerden, the green dotted line Fourier, and the purple dash-dot line the predicted time C.

Where does the error come from? SHOBS-P refits the polynomial 500 times with stretches of the night's own noise added, and allows for noise that lasts minutes (red noise). The error is never smaller than half the difference from the Fourier time (and, for a minimum, from Kwee-van Woerden). So if the methods disagree, the error says so. Without elements there is no Fourier check and the error can be low by a factor of 2–3; the result says so. (The details are for the methods document, planned.)

If no elements are given, the result says No elements: type the period and epoch (or Elements from VSX) for the O−C. You still get the time.

Save the time

  1. Press Save to times CSV.
  2. The first time, if the series has no file and no Output folder is set, SHOBS-P asks where to save.

The file is called <Star>_times_of_extremum.csv (for example SZ_Lyn_times_of_extremum.csv). It goes next to the series file, or in the Output folder. The line under the result shows which file is in use and how many times of this star it holds, and, from 2.3.3, lists the saved times themselves (type, HJD and error, O − C, night; the last eight), so you need not open the CSV to find an epoch.

Measuring the same extremum again and saving replaces that row (same star, same type, within 0.01 d), so you can refine a timing without making duplicates.

To keep times in another file, for example one shared between several series of the same star, press Choose times CSV… and pick or name a file.

The O − C diagram

The right-hand plot reads the times CSV. Saved maxima are filled circles, saved minima filled squares. Tonight's result, not yet saved, is an open red marker. The horizontal line at zero is "exactly on time".

  • Points scattered around zero: the elements are good.
  • Points along a sloping straight line: the period is slightly off.
  • Points along a curve: the period is changing.

Until there is something to plot, the panel says The O−C diagram appears here once times are saved to the CSV.

Previous: 13. Look for a period · Next: 15. Submit to AAVSO

15. Submit to AAVSO

SHOBS-P writes reports in the AAVSO Extended File Format, ready to upload with the file tool of AAVSO WebObs. This chapter covers what to set up, how to save a report for one night or a whole series, what each field holds, and how to avoid sending the same observations twice.

Set up once

On the Input page, in the AAVSO report box:

  • Observer code: your AAVSO observer code. Without it the report says na, and WebObs will not accept it under your name.
  • Chart ID: the AAVSO chart your comparison magnitudes come from. When any comp or the check star is an AAVSO sequence star, SHOBS-P fills in the chart ID from the solve. If you typed one yourself, it is left alone. Use na for comps not from an AAVSO chart.

In the Output page's Notes box, the default note is OSC reduced; not transformed for a color camera, or Monochrome camera; not transformed for a mono camera. It goes into every line of the report. Add anything AAVSO's reviewers should know, but keep it short. New reduction on the Input page puts the note back to this default, so one star's notes do not travel to the next star's report.

Save the report

  1. On the Output page, press Save AAVSO report.
  2. Answer any questions (below).
  3. Choose where to save, unless an Output folder is set on the Input page; then the file goes there directly.

The file is named like AAVSO_SZ_Lyn_TG_14-OCT-2026_SHOBS-P.txt: the star, the filter code, and the UTC date (or a date range such as 16-SEP-2026_to_06-OCT-2026 for several nights). With an Output folder, an existing file is never overwritten; a new copy gets _2, _3 and so on.

Beside the report, SHOBS-P also saves a field image (…_field.png): the frame as shot, with yellow N and E arrows showing where north and east lie, and the target, comps and check star marked. When the AAVSO's chart server answers, the AAVSO chart of the same field is saved next to it (…_aavso_chart.png), so you can check that your comps are the chart's stars. The chart is fetched in the background after the report is saved; whatever happens to it goes into the log only, so the status line keeps Saved AAVSO_….txt — upload to AAVSO WebObs with Open AAVSO WebObs… on the Results row. If the chart server does not answer, the log says "AAVSO chart not saved (…): the field image is saved alone."; press Save field image later to fetch it.

It also saves a settings file (…_settings.txt, from 2.3.1): night by night, the aperture and sky radii, binning, comps and check measured, frames skipped and left out, calibration, gain, telescope aperture and stacking, with the report's own comps, check and scintillation. Keep it with the report: with it, a night can be reduced again the same way.

When a series file is open, saving the report also notes it in the series (for the Sent column), so the series file is rewritten: the status line ends with Series file updated: SZ_Lyn.cvseries. The series keeps its own site and observer code. If the Input page's latitude, longitude, elevation or observer code differ from the series', you are asked first (see chapter 10, "Report saves and the series file").

The AUID: when the star has none

WebObs files every observation under the star's AUID, the AAVSO's own ID for it. SHOBS-P gets it from VSX when you look the star up on the Input page. Some stars are in VSX but have no AUID yet, often newly found variables such as ASASSN-V J185009.93+281035.4. WebObs rejects a report for such a star.

SHOBS-P tells you before you get that far. In Variables mode, when VSX knows the star but gave no AUID, an orange line appears on the Output page, just under the Results row:

⚠ No AUID: WebObs will reject this report. Request one in VSX (open the star's VSX page, Request AUID).

You can still save the report: the status line then starts with the same ⚠ No AUID: … warning before Saved AAVSO_….txt — upload to AAVSO WebObs:, and the warning goes into the log too. Request the AUID first: open the star's page in VSX, press Request AUID, and wait for the AAVSO to assign one. Then look the star up again on the Input page and save the report again.

The line does not appear when the star is not in VSX at all (then there is nothing to request in VSX).

The No AUID warning, here for ASASSN-V J185009.93+281035.4.

Questions before saving

⚠ The notes name HD 219134, which is not in this reduction. The Notes box names a star that is not your target, planet, comps or check star, most likely left over from the star you reduced before. The question shows the notes and asks Save anyway? The default is No: the report is not saved, and the status line says ✖ Not saved: fix the Notes (Output page), then save again. Correct the Notes box and press Save AAVSO report again. Answer Yes only if you meant to name that star (for example, a nearby star you mention on purpose). The same question comes before an AAVSO exoplanet report or an ExoFOP package.

The notes name a star that is not in this reduction.

The Star ID is “hd219134”. AAVSO spells it “HD 219134” … Use “HD 219134” in the report? Answer Yes. WebObs may not match another spelling to the star. The report and its file name use the AAVSO spelling, but the Star ID box changes to it only once the report is saved. If you cancel anywhere afterwards, the old name stays. The log then says Star ID set to “HD 219134” (AAVSO's spelling), as used in the saved report.

Before you upload: followed by a list. Each item is something AAVSO may reject or misfile:

  • AAVSO observer code is empty, Chart ID is empty, Star ID is empty: fill them in on the Input page.
  • the series mixes filters: report each filter separately (chapter 10).
  • ensemble report (more than one comp) without a check star; AAVSO requires one: mark a check star and run again.
  • a comp has no star name: the comp would be called something like comp1. Give it its catalog name.

Answer No to stop and fix things, or Yes to save anyway.

The check star has no name in this series … The check star was never given a real name, so the report would say KNAME = check. Type its AUID (for example 000-BJR-417) or catalog name (for example TYC 4006-956-1). SHOBS-P remembers it with the series. If you leave it empty, the report is not saved.

One night or the whole series

If a night is highlighted in the night table (and the series has more than one night), SHOBS-P asks:

SZ Lyn · night of 5–6 Oct 2026 is highlighted.

Yes: save the AAVSO report for just that night. No: save the AAVSO report for the whole series (8 nights). Cancel: don't save.

With no night highlighted, the whole series is saved.

Report each night once. The simplest habit for a campaign: after you add a night, click it in the night table, save the report for just that night, and upload it.

The duplicate warning

Saving a report never warns: many reports are saved and never uploaded. The warning comes when you upload, that is when you press Open AAVSO WebObs… on the Output page.

  • When the upload page opens, SHOBS-P marks the nights of the last report you saved as sent. The night table's Sent column then shows where and when, for example ✓ WebObs 2026-10-18, and the log says Marked sent to AAVSO WebObs: ….

  • If the last saved report holds a night that was already sent in another report, SHOBS-P asks before the page opens:

    Night 5–6 Oct 2026 was already sent on 2026-10-06 (in AAVSO_…); AAVSO will hold duplicates. Open anyway?

    … holds it again. Upload only the nights not sent yet (save a report of just one night: highlight it first), or delete the earlier upload at AAVSO. A night that was never really sent can be unmarked: double-click it in the night table.

    No does not open the page.

  • Unmark a night. If an upload never went through, or you deleted it at the AAVSO, double-click the night in the night table. SHOBS-P asks Mark it as not sent?; Yes clears the mark, and the next upload of a report with that night will not warn.

The marks are saved in the series file. The same marks and warning apply to the transit uploads (chapter 17).

Which points go in

  • Only used points: flagged points are left out while Leave flagged points (sat, drift, cloud) out of the period and report … is ticked (chapter 12).
  • Frames flagged during a planet transit are always left out. The log says how many.
  • The magnitudes are as measured against your comps. The line-up checkboxes, the check-star correction and the airmass-corrected night means do not change the report.

If nothing is left, you see No usable points to report for that choice.

What is in the file

The file begins with # header lines: #TYPE=Extended, #OBSCODE=, #SOFTWARE=Shiloh Hill Observatory – Photometry (SHOBS-P) 2.3.0, #DELIM=,, #DATE=JD, #OBSTYPE=CCD, and a line saying when SHOBS-P created it. Then one line per point:

Field What SHOBS-P writes
NAME The Star ID
DATE Julian Date (UTC) at mid-exposure
MAG, MERR The magnitude and its error (photon noise, scintillation, and any extra for dew/haze)
FILT The filter code, e.g. TG, V, CV, SI
TRANS NO (not transformed)
MTYPE STD
CNAME, CMAG One comp: its name and instrumental magnitude. Two or more: ENSEMBLE and na
KNAME, KMAG The check star's name and instrumental magnitude
AMASS The airmass, or na without a site and pointing
GROUP na
CHART The Chart ID
NOTES Your note, then for an ensemble ens and the comps' names, then the night key (e.g. night JD2461319), then any flag words on a flagged point you chose to keep; for stacked points, points are stacks of 40 frames (effective exposure 30 s)

For stacked points (chapter 8, "Stacking when comps are weak"), each line is one stack: its time is the mean of its frames' mid-times, and the NOTES give the stack size and its effective exposure (the sum of its frames' exposures).

Stars from the AAVSO sequence appear under their AUID. Other stars appear under their catalog name. The comps named are the ones actually in use, including a choice made with Use comps… (chapter 11). For a Sloan filter, the notes also say which catalogs the comp and check magnitudes came from.

Upload with WebObs

After saving (and, without an Output folder, a Saved dialog), the status bar names the file (after the ⚠ No AUID warning, if the star has no AUID) and points to Open AAVSO WebObs…, beside Save AAVSO report in the Results row of the Output page. That button checks for nights already sent and marks the report's nights as sent (see "The duplicate warning" above).

  1. Press Open AAVSO WebObs… on the Results row. AAVSO's photometry submission page opens in your browser.
  2. Log in with your AAVSO account.
  3. Use the file upload, and choose the report file.
  4. Read WebObs's check of the file before you confirm.

Before uploading, check three things in the file: your observer code, the chart ID, and the comparison magnitudes.

Previous: 14. Time a maximum or minimum · Next: 16. Prepare for a transit night

16. Prepare for a transit night

A transit night is decided before you open the dome. You need a planet whose whole transit, plus a stretch of steady baseline on both sides, happens in the dark with the star well above the horizon. This chapter is about getting SHOBS-P ready for that night, and about the choices that make the transit fit work later.

This chapter covers:

  • entering a planet under any of its names (or by its host star),
  • the TESS TOI status and ExoClock priority lines,
  • choosing the night and how much baseline to take,
  • what to do about a meridian flip (or, on an alt-az mount, the zenith keyhole).

SHOBS-P 2.3.0 no longer has the Tonight's transits… and Transit planner… windows. SHOBS-P does not predict the coming transits for you: choose the night with the planning resource you normally use, and then use this chapter to set SHOBS-P up for it.

Before you start

  1. Click Transits in the sidebar (under MODE). The window turns green and the Input page shows the Planet (Transits) box.
  2. On the Input page, fill in the Site box: Latitude (°, north +), Longitude (°, east +) (west is negative) and Elevation (m). You type these once; SHOBS-P remembers them. The transit fit needs them for BJD times, and the airmass and scintillation in the error bars come from them too.
  3. Check Time zone in the same box: the site's time zone (it starts as this computer's own). The times SHOBS-P shows for your night (for example the Trim boxes in chapter 17) are the site's local times in that zone. Make sure the computer's clock is right.
  4. In the Optics box, set Mount to German equatorial or Alt-az. SHOBS-P uses it to handle a meridian flip (below) and the field rotation of an alt-az mount.

See 4. Before your first night for the Site and Optics boxes.

Enter a planet

You can type a planet under any of its catalog names. SHOBS-P asks the NASA Exoplanet Archive, which knows KOI, Kepler, TOI, TIC, EPIC, Gaia and 2MASS spellings. TOIs that are not in the Archive yet are looked up at ExoFOP.

To enter a planet:

  1. In the Planet (Transits) box, type the name in Planet, for example TrES-3 b, KOI-217 b, TOI-2046 b or EPIC 220504338 b.
  2. Press Enter (or Tab out of the box). The lookup starts by itself. While it runs, the busy bar at the left end of the bottom status strip says "Looking up planet TOI-2046 b at the NASA Exoplanet Archive…". The boxes stay as they are until the answer comes: an empty box stays blank, and a value already in a box stays until the lookup replaces it. When it is done, the line under the box says where the values came from. If the archive does not answer within 60 seconds, that line says so in red, for example "✖ TOI-2046 b: the NASA Exoplanet Archive did not answer within 60 s — type the values or Look up again." Type the values, or press Look up again later.
  3. Check the filled boxes: Period (d), T0 (BJD_TDB), Duration T14 (h), Depth (%), Rp/R*, a/R*, Impact parameter b and Star Teff (K) (used for limb darkening).

The lookup also fills the Star ID and RA/Dec of the host star when they are empty.

Start from the host star instead

You can also start from the star. Type the host star in Star ID (in the Target box), for example TrES-3, and press Enter. The busy bar then stays on the whole time, in three steps:

  1. "Looking up TrES-3 at SIMBAD and VSX…" (the star's position),
  2. "Looking up TrES-3's planets at the NASA Exoplanet Archive…",
  3. "Looking up planet TrES-3 b at the NASA Exoplanet Archive…".

The bar goes when the planet boxes are filled. A star with several planets opens a chooser instead (see below).

If the Archive lists no planet for the star, or did not answer, the line under the Planet box says in red: "✖ No planets found for TrES-3 at the NASA Exoplanet Archive (none listed under that name, or the Archive did not answer). Type the planet's name, or press Look up again on the Star ID." If the planet list gets no answer within 60 seconds, it says "✖ TrES-3: the NASA Exoplanet Archive did not answer within 60 s — type the planet's name, or Look up again on the Star ID."

Things worth knowing about the lookup

  • The ephemeris. SHOBS-P compares every published ephemeris for the planet and keeps the one with the smallest predicted uncertainty for tonight. The source line names it, for example "most precise of 4 published ephemerides, ±3 min now". This is the ephemeris your O − C is measured against later.
  • A star with several planets. If you type a star (or the Star ID is a star) with more than one known planet, a window asks "… has N known planets. Which one did you observe?". Click the planet. Planets that do not transit are marked (does not transit). Cancel stops the lookup.
  • A planet of another star. If the planet orbits a different star than the one now on the Input page, SHOBS-P starts a new reduction (chapter 5): it clears the old target, comparison stars, check star, watch stars and Use comps… choice, closes an open series (asking first, with a save offered), and fills in the new host. This keeps a previous field's stars from leaking into the new one. If a series is open and you answer No, the old star and its series stay; check the planet's name.
  • Typing values by hand. Every box can be edited. If the lookup fails (for example, no internet), type the period, T0 and duration yourself; the rest help the fit but are optional. If you type a depth that disagrees with Rp/R* by more than 20%, SHOBS-P notes it and compares against (Rp/R*)².
  • Look up again repeats the lookup, for example after a failed one.

TESS TOI status and ExoClock priority

Right after the planet's values are filled in, SHOBS-P checks two more things in the background, so the lookup is not slowed. While it checks, the line under the box says "Checking TESS TOI status and ExoClock…". Then two lines appear under the source line:

  • TESS TOI status, from the NASA Exoplanet Archive's TOI table (the TFOP Working Group's disposition). SHOBS-P finds the TOI by the star's TIC number, else by a TOI number in the planet's names, else by the star's position (within 10″). Many WASP, HAT-P and KELT planets are TOIs too.

    • A candidate: "TOI-1234.01 · PC (planet candidate) — consider uploading to ExoFOP-TESS (coordinate with TFOP SG1)". APC (ambiguous planet candidate) reads the same way. Your transit of a candidate is real follow-up science; coordinate with TFOP Sub Group 1 before you upload.
    • A confirmed or known planet: "TOI-2046.01 · CP (confirmed) — AAVSO / Exoplanet Watch is the right home" (or KP, known planet).
    • A false positive (FP), a false alarm (FA) or a planet that is not a TOI: no line.
    • If the Archive does not answer: "TOI status unknown (Archive did not answer)". It never says "not a TOI" when it could not ask.

    The TOI line is blue: click it to open the target's ExoFOP-TESS page.

  • ExoClock priority, from ExoClock's planet list (downloaded once a day), for example "ExoClock: HIGH priority (2 recent observations)". The priority is ALERT, HIGH, MEDIUM or LOW; ALERT and HIGH planets need timings most. If your telescope (Telescope aperture (mm) in the Optics box) is smaller than ExoClock asks for, the line adds "(needs ≥ 5″ telescope)". A planet ExoClock does not follow says "Not on ExoClock's list". Click the line to open the planet's ExoClock page.

When a service does not answer, SHOBS-P uses its last answer and says so, for example "(from cache, fetched 12 Oct 2026)". The same two lines are shown on the Transit fit page (chapter 17).

If the lookup fails, the line under the box says "Could not look up … Check the spelling and press Look up again, or type the values."

Choose the night

A good transit night has:

  • the whole transit (ingress to egress, Duration T14 (h)) in astronomical darkness,
  • the star well above the horizon the whole time, baseline included (30° or more is a good rule),
  • a baseline before ingress and after egress (next section),
  • the Moon not too close to the star, for a faint target.

The predicted mid-transit times follow from the boxes the lookup filled: a transit happens at T0 (BJD_TDB) plus a whole number of Period (d). BJD_TDB differs from a plain Julian date in UTC by at most about 9½ minutes (the light's travel time across the Earth's orbit, plus about a minute), so for planning, T0 + n × P read as a UTC Julian date is close enough; take a few minutes' more baseline on each side to cover it. Add the ephemeris's uncertainty (the "±… min now" in the source line) to both ends of your run.

When you run photometry later, SHOBS-P checks whether your frames cover a predicted transit of the planet. If they do not, it says so before it runs, and offers Variables mode if the run looks like variability work instead (chapter 8).

How much baseline

The baseline is the light curve before ingress and after egress. The fit uses it to learn the star's level and any airmass trend, so a transit with no baseline on one side can be faked or distorted by a slow trend.

  • Aim for at least an hour on each side. Half an hour on each side is a workable minimum.
  • Equal amounts on both sides are better than a long stretch on one side only.
  • If the ephemeris is uncertain, add that uncertainty to both ends.
  • Start a few minutes early: the first frames often go to focus and settling.

When the fit later finds less than about 20 minutes of baseline on a side, it warns you and may judge the depth unreliable (see 18. When the fit says "inconclusive" or "none").

Faint comps? If the field's comparison stars are much fainter than the target, their noise can swamp the transit in single frames. SHOBS-P can stack frames after the run to fix that (see "Stacking when comps are weak" in 8. Photometry page basics), but a stack hides anything faster than itself. Short exposures are fine; just take enough of them.

Meridian flips

On a German equatorial mount the telescope flips when the star crosses the meridian. The flip leaves a gap in the data, and the star lands on a different part of the sensor, which can leave a small step in the light curve.

What SHOBS-P does:

  • With Mount set to German equatorial, SHOBS-P finds the flip from the frames' headers (ROTATOR or PIERSIDE) or from the image itself, and splits the night there.
  • During photometry, frames on both sides of the flip are aligned and measured as usual; the alignment of each flipped frame is checked on up to 15 stars' own centers, so the comp and check apertures land on their stars. The Calibrate log notes where the flip happened ("Meridian flip before frame …"). If you reject the frames before the flip in Blink, the first kept frame becomes the reference (chapter 6).
  • The transit fit tests a flip step: a jump in the star's level at the flip. It fits the night with and without the step, and keeps the step only when it improves the fit clearly (ΔBIC 10 or more, the same bar as a detection). A kept step is fitted together with the transit, so its uncertainty is part of the depth's. See "A meridian flip in the night" in 17. Fit a transit and submit it.
  • If Mount is left on Alt-az but the field turns by about 180° between two frames, the photometry run warns you: "⚠ The field turned by 180° between frames … — that looks like a meridian flip. Is the Mount set right (German equatorial)? …". Set Mount to German equatorial and run photometry again, so the frames after the flip are treated as after it.

How to plan for it:

  1. Prefer a transit with no flip, if you have the choice.
  2. If a flip cannot be avoided, put it in the baseline, well away from ingress and egress, and keep a good stretch of baseline (30 minutes or more) on each side of it. This matters more now that the fit can fit a step at the flip: with too little baseline on one side, the step and the transit cannot be told apart, and the verdict is "inconclusive".
  3. Keep the flip short, so the gap is short.

You do not need a second set of flats for a meridian flip. After a flip the camera sits in the same place on the telescope; only the whole tube has turned. The dust shadows and vignetting the flat corrects turn with it. SHOBS-P knows this: rotator readings 180° apart count as the same camera position, so flats from either side of the pier match lights from both sides. A real rotator move (the camera turned on the telescope) is different, and the Calibrate page warns about it. See 7. Calibrate.

Alt-az mounts: the zenith keyhole

An alt-az mount never flips, so the flip step does not apply to it. Its trouble spot is the zenith: above about 85° the mount's azimuth speed and the field rotation get extreme, and frames there can trail or smear. When you choose the night, avoid transits during which the star climbs above 85°, or keep that stretch in the baseline.

When you scan the folders of such a night, with Mount set to Alt-az in the Optics box (Input page), the log says, for example, "Zenith keyhole: the field reached altitude 87.2° (above 85°), … Check them on the Blink page."

Checklist for the night

  • Planet entered on the Input page, with period, T0 and duration filled in.
  • Mount in the Optics box set to your mount.
  • Start and end times that cover the transit, at least 30–60 minutes of baseline on each side, plus any ephemeris uncertainty.
  • Flats for the camera angle you will use (one set covers both sides of a flip).
  • Bias (or dark flats) and darks that match the lights' exposure (chapter 4).
  • Comparison stars of similar brightness and color in the field (the Photometry page suggests them; see 8. Photometry page basics).

Previous: 15. Submit to AAVSO · Next: 17. Fit a transit and submit it

17. Fit a transit and submit it

After photometry in Transits mode, the Output step is the Output – Transit fit page. It fits a transit model to your light curve, tells you in plain words whether a transit was measured, and writes the reports.

This chapter covers:

  • getting to the fit,
  • reading the verdict, the numbers and the plot,
  • the comparison-star checks on this page (leave-one-comp-out, Comp health…, Use comps…),
  • leaving out a bad stretch with Trim,
  • a meridian flip in the night (the flip step),
  • the corner plot of the fit's uncertainties,
  • per-frame or stacked points,
  • saving and submitting the result (AAVSO, ExoFOP and ExoClock),
  • fitting several nights of one planet together (Multi-night…).

Get to the fit

  1. In Transits mode, enter the planet on the Input page (see 16. Prepare for a transit night).
  2. Work through Input, Blink and Calibrate, then mark the target and comparison stars and press Run photometry (see 8. Photometry page basics). A check star is optional for a transit.
  3. When photometry ends, SHOBS-P opens the Output step. The fit never starts by itself: the page says "… usable points. Set the detrend and press Fit transit." Set the detrend (and anything else) first, then press Fit transit. It needs at least 20 usable points and a planet. The same holds after Use comps…, after you pick another night, and after you open a report.

The fit has three stages. The busy bar at the left end of the bottom status strip names each one as it goes: a least-squares fit ("Fitting the transit: least-squares fit…"), then an MCMC run ("Fitting the transit: MCMC burn-in… 600 of 1,000", then "Fitting the transit: MCMC step… 1,200 of 3,000"), then a comparison-star check ("Checking the comps… 2 of 5"). The bar moves back and forth; it never fills, so read the counts in its words. When it is all done, the bar goes and the status line says "✔ Transit fit done; comps checked." How long it takes depends on the number of frames and comps.

Caution: The fit needs your site's latitude and longitude for BJD times. Without them it stops with "Enter your site's latitude and longitude on the Input page first (they are needed for BJD times)."

In 2.3.0 the tools row starts with Nearby-binary (NEB) check… (Tonight's transits and the Transit planner are gone), and the corner plot moved to the Results row as Save corner plot…. A running fit shows on the busy bar in the status strip; the page has no progress bar of its own.

The page, top to bottom

  • Planet line: the planet's period, duration, depth and shape, and where they came from.
  • Night: which night of your photometry is fitted (for example "KELT-16 b · night of 5–6 Oct 2026"). The newest night is chosen. Picking another one clears the fit; press Fit transit to fit it. The hint beside it counts the points: usable, in this fit, flagged, trimmed.
  • Open AAVSO exoplanet report (EXOTIC)…: fit someone else's report instead (see 19. Reports).
  • Detrend: airmass (on by default) and time slope (off). These are baseline trends fitted together with the transit. Add time slope only when the baseline clearly drifts and airmass does not explain it. With both ticked ("Both"), the two trends can together absorb a transit. When that happens, SHOBS-P refits with each trend alone, and the verdict says so, for example "No detection with Both: … Airmass alone detects it (ΔBIC 32). Untick time slope and fit again only if that trend is not real …".
  • MCMC: Quick (1,500 steps), Standard (3,000 steps) (default) or Thorough (8,000 steps). Thorough gives steadier error bars for a final result; Quick is fine while you try things.
  • Fit transit runs the fit again with the current settings. Cancel stops it.
  • The tools row: Nearby-binary (NEB) check…, Multi-night…, Comp health…, Use comps… (all below), the credible bands box and the Plot bins box (see "Read the plot").
  • Trim row (below).
  • The verdict, the quality line, the plot, the parameter table and the notes.
  • A result line above the Results row, once there is something to say: "✔ Done: Tc …" with the main numbers, "✖ Fit failed." or "Canceled.", and then the comp-check result (below). On a night with a meridian flip it also says what the flip step test decided, for example "; Flip step fitted: −14.4 ± 0.5 ppt (ΔBIC 629), kept" (see "A meridian flip in the night").
  • The Results row: Save AAVSO exoplanet report…, Open AAVSO Exoplanet Database…, Save ExoFOP package…, Open ExoFOP…, Save for ExoClock…, Go to ExoClock…, Save light-curve CSV…, Save plot PNG…, Save corner plot…, Save field image… and Fit the series' nights…, with Variable View ▶ at the right. Multi-night fit… is grayed unless a Transits series with two or more nights is open (see Fit several nights together below).
  • Under the Results row, once the planet is looked up: the planet's TESS TOI status and ExoClock priority (chapter 16), for example "TOI-2046.01 · CP (confirmed) — AAVSO / Exoplanet Watch is the right home" and "ExoClock: LOW priority (24 recent observations)". Click the TOI line to open the target's ExoFOP-TESS page, and the ExoClock line to open the planet's ExoClock page. They help you choose where to send the result (below).

Read the verdict

The bold line above the plot is the verdict. There are three:

  • ✓ Transit detected (green), for example "Transit detected: ΔBIC 48 against a flat line, Tc ± 1.9 min." The transit model beats a flat line clearly, the mid-transit time is pinned down, and the depth is believable.
  • ⚠ Inconclusive (orange): the dip beats a flat line, but something else explains it as well. "Tc may be usable; the depth is not."
  • ✗ No transit measured (red): "The numbers below are for reference only."

A detected transit can still carry "Timing usable; depth unreliable: …" (orange) when the depth is far from the published value and the night gives a reason, such as a one-sided baseline. Chapter 18 explains every reason and what to do about it.

Read the quality line

Under the verdict a single line sums up the noise, for example:

Fit rms 0.480% per point · 0.210% per 10-min bin · 68 points · red-noise β 1.12 · χ²ν 1.05 (raw errors)

  • Fit rms per point: the scatter of single frames around the model.
  • per 10-min bin: the scatter after averaging 10 minutes of frames. Compare it with the depth: a 1% transit measured with 0.2% bins is clear; a 0.5% transit with 0.4% bins is not.
  • red-noise β: above 1 means the noise is correlated in time (seeing, guiding, thin cloud). The fit widens its uncertainties by β, and the notes say so when β is above 1.5.
  • χ²ν (raw errors): how the scatter compares with the error bars from the photometry. About 1 is right. Well below 1 or well above 2 gets a note explaining the likely cause (often the camera gain, EGAIN, in the headers).

Read the table

The table under the plot has one row per quantity: Tc (BJD_TDB), O − C (min), Rp/R*, Depth (%), a/R*, Impact parameter b, Inclination (°), Duration T14 (h), the limb-darkening coefficients, the Airmass slope, the flip step on a night with a meridian flip (see "A meridian flip in the night"), and the noise figures. Its columns are:

  • SHOBS-P (MCMC): the result, value ± uncertainty. This is what goes in the report.
  • Free shape: a quick fit with a/R* and b left free. It is a cross-check: if Rp/R* moves a lot here, your data alone do not pin the shape down, and the result leans on the published shape. When the free fit runs away (for example to an impossible depth), the column says not constrained instead of numbers, and a note explains that the reported values use the published a/R* and b. From 2.3.2 one parameter can also run away on its own while the depth looks fine: then only that row says not constrained. That is a/R* or T14 more than 25 % above or below the published value (else the MCMC's), b more than 0.3 from it, or an error on a/R* larger than half of it. A wide error on b alone is normal (b and a/R* trade off on one night's light curve) and is not flagged.
  • Published / prior: the lookup's values. The published depth shown is (Rp/R*)², so it compares like with like. On the Tc row this column holds the predicted Tc for your epoch, with its uncertainty.
  • A fourth column with a report's own values appears when you fit an EXOTIC report.

Rows a caution talks about are shown in orange.

O − C

O − C (min) is your mid-transit time minus the time the ephemeris predicts, in minutes, and in σ, for example +5.38 ± 11.15 (+0.5σ). Its ± combines your timing error and the ephemeris's (in quadrature). The ephemeris's own uncertainty is propagated to your epoch, and shown in the Published column as 0 ± 11.10 (ephemeris). It is also right for an epoch before the ephemeris's T0. A note gives the whole story, for example "O − C +5.38 ± 11.15 min (+0.48σ; fit and ephemeris σ in quadrature) vs predicted Tc 2461328.625780 ± 0.007706 BJD_TDB (±11.10 min) at epoch 2362; ephemeris from …". The same line goes into the report's notes and the fit CSV. The note turns orange when O − C is more than 3σ from zero.

The fit looks for Tc anywhere within your data (plus 0.2 day either side), never only in a window around the prediction, so a late or early transit is found where it really is. If the prediction itself is uncertain by more than about 10 minutes, a note says the O − C "mostly tests the ephemeris", which is still useful: that is what timing observations are for.

Read the plot

  • Gray dots: each frame, detrended.
  • Black points with error bars: bins of several frames, also shown on the residual panel. The Plot bins box sets their width: Auto (10 minutes, or 3 frames' spacing when the frames are farther apart than 3⅓ minutes), 5 min, 10 min, 15 min or 30 min. A bin needs at least 3 frames, and the legend names the real width (for example "13-minute bins" on MicroObservatory frames). The bins are only for the eye: changing them redraws the plot without refitting, and the fit, the red-noise beta and the rms per 10-min bin line use every frame as before.
  • Green line: the SHOBS-P model, labeled with its Rp/R* and depth.
  • Blue dotted line: the published transit shape, for comparison.
  • Gray dashed line and band: the predicted mid-transit and its uncertainty.
  • Thin green lines: the fitted start and end of the transit.
  • Purple line at a meridian flip: dash-dot when the flip step was kept, labeled for example "flip: step −14.4 ± 0.5 ppt"; dotted when it was not needed, labeled "flip: no step needed".
  • Green shading around the model: the 68 % and 95 % credible bands, the range of models the MCMC allows. The lower panel shades the baseline's own spread the same way. Untick credible bands to hide them.
  • Lower panel (Resid. %): the residuals. They should look like noise, with no dip or step.

The horizontal axis is Hours from fitted mid-transit. The legend sits under the residual panel, never on the data.

Notes about the reduction

The notes under the table also say:

  • what this night was measured with, for example "Measured with r 8, sky 14–22 px (Bin 1).", in orange when the Photometry page's boxes have changed since (run photometry again to apply them);
  • the camera gain the error bars used and where it came from, for example "Gain 0.25 e-/ADU (header EGAIN)." When the headers have no gain, it is estimated from the sky's own noise, or 1 e-/ADU is assumed, in orange. On frames the camera binned (XBINNING above 1), SHOBS-P also works out from the sky's noise whether the camera summed or averaged the binned pixels, for example "Gain 0.768 e-/ADU (header EGAIN); camera bin 3x3 averaged, so 6.91 e- per stored ADU used (from the sky's noise)". See "Camera binning and the gain" in 7. Calibrate;
  • on stacked points, "Stacking: stacks of 40 frames (effective exposure 30 s) (sigma-clipped mean, whole-pixel shifts, never across a flip)" (see "Per-frame or stacked points" below).

The full set of reduction settings (aperture, binning, comps, check, frames skipped at photometry, frames left out, trimmed frames, detrending, calibration, gain, stacking) is written to the log after each fit, one line per fit, and saved with the report (see below), so a result can be reproduced later.

A meridian flip in the night

On a German equatorial mount the star lands on another part of the sensor after the flip, and its measured level can jump a little: a flip step. Left in, such a step drags the baseline on one side and can make the depth wrong (on one real night the target was 16 mmag fainter after the flip, and without a step the depth came out 14 ppt instead of the published 24 ppt).

When SHOBS-P knows the night has a flip (Mount set to German equatorial; the flip comes from the frames' headers or the image, chapter 16), the fit tests a step there:

  1. It fits the night twice by least squares, with and without a step. The step multiplies the baseline after the flip by (1 + step), so it is in relative flux, given in ppt. A target that gets fainter after the flip has a negative step.
  2. It keeps the step only when it lowers the BIC by 10 or more (ΔBIC ≥ 10, the same bar as a detection).
  3. A kept step is fitted together with the transit in the MCMC, so its uncertainty is part of the depth's. A step that is not needed is left out: the fit then has no step term at all.

The flip time itself is never fitted: it is halfway between the last frame before the flip and the first after it. An alt-az night has no flip and never gets the term.

Where you see the decision:

  • The log while fitting: "meridian flip: step −14.5 ± 0.6 ppt, ΔBIC 628.8 (kept at 10 or more): kept, fitted with the transit" or "… not needed, fitted without a step".
  • The result line: "; Flip step fitted: −14.4 ± 0.5 ppt (ΔBIC 629), kept" or "; Flip step not needed: +0.2 ± 0.6 ppt (ΔBIC −6; kept only at 10 or more)".
  • The table. With the step kept: a row Flip step (ppt), for example "−14.4 ± 0.5 (ΔBIC 629, kept)", then Depth without step (%) (what the depth would be with no step, from a least-squares fit) and Step–depth correlation (how much the step and the depth trade off in the MCMC; near ±1 they are hard to tell apart). With the step not needed: one row Flip step, for example "not needed (ΔBIC −6; test step +0.2 ± 0.6 ppt)".
  • The plot: the purple line at the flip (see "Read the plot").
  • The notes: for example "Meridian flip at BJD_TDB … (+2.57 h from mid-transit; the frames' flip segment): a baseline step there was fitted together with the transit: step −14.4 ± 0.5 ppt (kept: ΔBIC 629, at least 10 needed).", then the depth without the step, the step–depth correlation and the baseline on each side. When it is not needed: "… Flip step not needed: … The step is kept only at ΔBIC 10 or more, so this fit has no step term."

A night with a meridian flip: the purple dash-dot line marks the flip, and the table has the Flip step (ppt), Depth without step and Step–depth correlation rows.

The leave-one-comp-out check uses the main fit's decision (step or no step) for every refit. The reports carry the flip time either way (chapter 19), so a later refit of a report uses the same time.

For an opened report, which carries no flip segment, tick meridian flip at the largest gap to tell the fit that the report's largest gap is the flip; the same ΔBIC ≥ 10 rule then decides.

The corner plot

Save corner plot… (in the Results row) opens a window with the MCMC samples of every fitted parameter: a histogram per parameter on the diagonal (with its median and 16/84 % lines, and the value and error in the title), and each pair below it, with 1σ and 2σ contours. Tc is in minutes from its median. By default the window shows the shape parameters (Tc, Rp/R*, a/R*, b, and the flip step if fitted); tick Show all parameters (baseline, limb darkening) for the rest. Save PNG… saves the plot, and Close closes the window. From 2.3.2 the file carries the number of the fit's saved set (…_transit_2_corner.png beside …_transit_2.png), whichever you save first, and one fit keeps one corner plot: saving it again for the same fit (from this window or with Save plot PNG…) replaces it, in the view saved last, instead of adding a _2 copy. Without a fit, the button says "Fit a transit first: the corner plot shows that fit's MCMC samples."

How to read it: a round hump is a measurement. A histogram that piles up against the edge of its allowed range is set by that range, not by your data, so its error bar is not a measurement. SHOBS-P flags such a parameter with ⚠ in the window, in the page's notes and in the report's notes, for example "a/R* is limited by its prior range: its MCMC samples pile up against …, so the error bar is not a measurement." An impact parameter b against 0 is the exception: that is a central transit, and the note says to read b as an upper limit.

Check the comparison stars

A bad comparison star can make or hide a transit. The page checks this in three ways.

Leave-one-comp-out. With two or more comps, after every fit SHOBS-P refits once with each comp left out. The result line then adds, for example, "Comps checked: depth spread 0.12 %, Tc spread 45 s". If leaving one comp out moves the depth or Tc by more than its statistical error, the notes name that comp in orange, with a link to Comp health. Wait for this check to finish (a few seconds) before saving: saves made while it runs are refused, so that the files carry its result.

Comp health…. Opens the same comparison-star health window as in Variables mode: each star against the others, through the night. A comp marked SUSPECT should go. See 11. Check your comparison stars.

Use comps…. Tick the stars to use and click Apply. Every point is recalculated from the stored measurements (nothing is reduced again), and the old fit is cleared. The page says "Comps in use: C1, C3. Set the detrend and press Fit transit."; nothing is fitted until you press it.

These two buttons work only on this session's photometry. While an EXOTIC report is shown they are grayed out, and the hint beside them says why.

Leave out a bad stretch (Trim)

Use Trim when part of the night is clearly bad (a passing cloud, a dewed-up corrector, a tracking jump) and the flags did not catch it.

To trim a stretch:

  1. Drag across the plot from the start to the end of the bad stretch. The from and to boxes fill with the site's local times, and the status bar says how many points are inside. Or type the times yourself: the site's local clock time (0042 or 00:42, in the Time zone of the Site box on the Input page), or a BJD_TDB. Leave from empty to trim from the start, or to empty to trim to the end.
  2. Click Trim and refit (or press Enter in either box).

The trimmed points show as faint × in a gray band labeled trimmed (not fitted). They are left out of the fit, the verdict, the rms, the comp check and every saved file, and the report's NOTES record the trimmed span (in BJD_TDB, with the site's local times and their UTC offset). Each Trim and refit adds another span; Clear trim puts every point back and refits.

A trim needs at least 20 points left. It belongs to the night it was made on: choosing another night, opening a report or running photometry again clears it.

Caution: Trim to remove bad data, not to make a result look better. Never trim the ingress, egress or the bottom of the transit because they disagree with the model.

Check nearby stars (NEB check)

For a TESS candidate, a faint eclipsing binary next to the target can fake the transit in TESS's large pixels. Nearby-binary (NEB) check… lists every labeled star within Radius (arcmin) (2.5 by default), the depth it would need to cause the whole signal (Needed depth), and a Verdict:

  • cannot cause it (would need over 100%): too faint to matter.
  • needs checking: watch it and measure a transit night.
  • cleared: its measured drop at transit time is well below what it would need.
  • POSSIBLE SOURCE: dims by about the needed depth.
  • not cleared yet (too noisy); measure again.

To clear the neighbors:

  1. Click Watch the neighbors that need checking. They become watch stars.
  2. Run photometry on a transit night and fit the transit.
  3. Open the NEB check again. Each watched neighbor now has a Measured drop and a verdict.
