The check needs a solved image and the transit depth.

Per-frame or stacked points

When the comparison stars are much fainter than the target, the Photometry page can stack frames and measure the stacks (see "Stacking when comps are weak" in 8. Photometry page basics). The night then has two sets of points, and the Points box on the Photometry page (Per frame / Stacked) chooses which one the fit uses. The Results use line on the Photometry page, and on the Output page in Variable View, says which.

  • The fit of each set is kept. Switching back to a set fitted before shows its fit at once, and the result line says, for example, "✔ Fit of the stacked points (kept from before)." A set not fitted yet is fitted as soon as this page is on screen.
  • The fit needs 20 points or more. Stack asks first when the stacks would be fewer.
  • With stacked points the fit uses the stacks' effective exposure (the sum of their frames' exposures), and the settings record and the report's SHOBS-P settings: notes say "points are stacks of 40 frames (effective exposure 30 s)".
  • A stack hides anything faster than itself, so SHOBS-P suggests stacks no longer than a fifth of the ingress (or egress), from the planet on the Input page.

Look at the light curve in detail

Variable View ▶ (bottom right) shows the Variables-style Output page for the same photometry: the light curve in magnitudes, the check star, the night table and the comp tools. ◀ Transit View in the same corner brings you back. See 9. Reduce a variable-star night for that page.

Save and submit

When the verdict is "detected" and you are happy with the fit:

  1. Set an Output folder on the Input page if you have not, so every save goes there without asking.
  2. Click Save AAVSO exoplanet report…. The status bar names the file.
  3. Click Open AAVSO Exoplanet Database… (beside it on the Results row) and upload the report there, following the AAVSO's current instructions.
  4. For a TESS target, click Save ExoFOP package… and then Open ExoFOP…. The TOI line under the Results row tells you whether ExoFOP-TESS wants it: a PC or APC (planet candidate) is follow-up science there (coordinate with TFOP Sub Group 1 first); for a CP or KP (confirmed or known planet), AAVSO and Exoplanet Watch are the right home.
  5. Click Save plot PNG… for your own records. The corner plot is saved beside it, as …_corner.png. (Save corner plot… opens the corner plot on its own, with its own Save PNG….)
  6. Click Save light-curve CSV… if you want the numbers. With a fit shown, it saves two files: the light-curve CSV (the same as on the Variables Output page) and a fit CSV beside it, named like TrES-3b_TG_05-OCT-2026_SHOBS-P_transit_fit.csv. The fit CSV has the columns BJD_TDB, rel_flux, rel_flux_err, model, residual, flag, airmass, segment and (from 2.3.4) rel_flux_err_raw, one row per point in time order. rel_flux is the raw relative flux (divided by its median, not detrended); the baseline is in model. segment says before flip or after flip on a night with a meridian flip, and is blank otherwise. Trimmed points are rows too, flagged trimmed, with no error, model, residual or airmass. With an Output folder set, both files get the same number as the report and plot of this fit. For a fit of an opened EXOTIC report, only the fit CSV is saved. Under its first line, the fit CSV has comment lines starting with #: the planet, filter, fitted Tc and verdict, the O − C line, what the columns hold, and the time of the meridian flip when there was one.

Notes that name another star. Before it saves the AAVSO exoplanet report or the ExoFOP package of your own photometry, SHOBS-P reads the Notes box (in Variable View). If the notes name a star that is not in this reduction (often a leftover from the last star), it asks, for example "⚠ The notes name HD 219134, which is not in this reduction. … Save anyway?". No (the default) saves nothing and the status line says "✖ Not saved: fix the Notes (Output page), then save again." The target, planet, comps and check star never set it off. A new reduction puts the notes back to "OSC reduced; not transformed" (or "Monochrome camera; not transformed").

Saving the report also writes a …_settings.txt with the reduction settings, and a field image (…_field.png, plus the AAVSO chart of the field when it answers) beside it. Chapter 19 describes them.

Send it to ExoClock

ExoClock collects transit times of known planets to keep their ephemerides up to date, so the Ariel space mission knows when to look. Any timed transit is welcome there, TESS target or not. Uploading needs an ExoClock account. The ExoClock line under the Results row says how much ExoClock needs this planet (ALERT and HIGH most), how many recent observations it has, and whether it asks for a bigger telescope than yours.

  1. Click Save for ExoClock…. SHOBS-P writes two files:

    • ExoClock_<planet>_<YYYYMMDD>.txt, for example ExoClock_TOI-2046b_20261027.txt (the planet without spaces, and the UTC date of the first frame). This is the file you upload. It has three columns: the time in JD_UTC at mid-exposure, the relative flux (not detrended), and its error. Only the frames the fit used are in it.
    • ExoClock_info.txt: what to pick on ExoClock's upload form for that file.

    The status line says "✔ Saved. On the form, pick Mid-exposure (ExoClock_TOI-2046b_20261027.txt; what to pick is in ExoClock_info.txt)."

  2. Click Go to ExoClock…. The upload page opens in your browser. Log in.

  3. Fill in the form from ExoClock_info.txt:

    • Planet: the name with no space, for example TOI-2046b.
    • Time format: JD_UTC.
    • Time stamp: Mid-exposure. The form starts on Exposure start, so change it. This is the one most easily missed: left on Exposure start, every time is off by half an exposure.
    • Flux format: Flux.
    • Filter: as the info file says. A color camera's green channel (TG) is listed as Johnson V.
    • Exposure time: in seconds, as the info file says.

  4. Upload the ExoClock_…txt file.

The Results row on the Transit fit page, with Save for ExoClock… and Go to ExoClock… after Open ExoFOP….

The times need the target's RA/Dec and your site's latitude and longitude, as for every BJD time. Without them, SHOBS-P says "ExoClock file not saved: …".

Uploading twice. Open AAVSO Exoplanet Database…, Open ExoFOP… and Go to ExoClock… work like Open AAVSO WebObs… in chapter 15: when the page opens, the night of the report (or package, or file) you saved last is marked sent, and the night table's Sent column shows it (for example ✓ AAVSO exo 2026-10-06, ✓ ExoFOP 2026-10-06 or ✓ ExoClock 2026-10-27). If that night was already sent in another report, SHOBS-P asks before opening the page. Double-click the night in the night table to unmark it.

If the verdict is "inconclusive" or "none", SHOBS-P asks before saving a report and writes the verdict into its notes. Chapter 19 describes every file and the upload sites.

Fit several nights together (Multi-night…)

One night gives one transit time. Several nights of the same planet, fitted together, give a better shape, a time for every night, and a check of the ephemeris. There are two ways in:

  • Fit the series' nights… (Results row) when a Transits series is open with two or more nights. The window opens with every night of the series already in the table, one row per night ("series: night of 15–16 Oct 2026").
  • Multi-night… (tools row, under the fit options) for reports and fit CSVs, yours or other observers'.

Both open the same multi-night transit analysis window.

From a Transits series

A Transits series holds every night of one planet with one reduction: the same comps and apertures, each night with its own flats. Fit the series' nights… (or Use the series' nights in the window) takes each night from the series' own points: times put on BJD_TDB from the Input page's RA/Dec and your site, airmass, and the meridian-flip segment. You do not need to save a fit CSV for each night first.

When a night has a fit CSV or an AAVSO exoplanet report saved for it (Save light-curve CSV… or Save AAVSO exoplanet report… on this page), the series remembers the file, and its Tc fills the Single-night Tc column. For a night with neither, SHOBS-P fits that night alone in the background, as for an older fit CSV. If a remembered file is no longer on disk (moved or deleted), a line in orange under the buttons names it, for example "⚠ Night of 18–19 Oct 2026: TOI-2046b_CV_18-OCT-2026_SHOBS-P_transit_fit.csv is not found (moved or deleted); the night is loaded from the series' own points." The other nights load as usual. A night that cannot be made from the points (no RA/Dec or site, or too few points) is loaded from its file instead, or listed with ✖ and the reason.

After you save a report, fit CSV or ExoClock file for a night of a series with two or more nights, the line under the plot says, for example, "This series has 3 transit nights; Fit the series' nights… (Results row) fits them together."

A Variables-mode series never offers Fit the series' nights….

The steps

  1. Load the nights: Fit the series' nights… for a Transits series (above), or Add reports or fit CSVs… for AAVSO exoplanet reports (from SHOBS-P or EXOTIC, .txt) or SHOBS-P fit CSVs (…_transit_fit.csv). The night fitted on the Transit fit page joins them when Include the Transit fit page's night is ticked. (It is unticked when the series' nights are loaded, since that night is one of them.)
  2. Check the list. The Status column says ✔ ready for each usable night. A night of another planet, the same night twice, fewer than 20 points, or no transit predicted during the data is left out (✖ and the reason). A night in another filter than most gets a ⚠; consider Depth and limb darkening per filter. Leave out / put back (or a double-click) and Remove change the list. If the table is wider than the window, use the sideways scroll bar under it. The Single-night Tc (BJD_TDB) column gives each night's own Tc. For a fit CSV that does not carry one, SHOBS-P fits that night alone in the background: the column says fitting… until it is done, or not fitted if the quick fit fails.
  3. Check the Input ephemeris. It shows T0 and P with their ± and where they came from, for example "T0 2460000.50000 ± 0.72 min, P 1.4971842 ± 0.0000010 d — NASA Exoplanet Archive (Ivshina & Winn 2022)". It is filled from the planet (a series keeps its planet's lookup; the line then ends "(kept with the series)"); Look up (NASA Exoplanet Archive) fetches it again. Optionally type an Alternative ephemeris (T0, P, their ±, and a source, for example Exoplanet Watch's or ExoClock's current one) and press Apply. With the series' nights loaded, the alternative ephemeris is kept in the series file, so the next Fit the series' nights… fills it in again and the O − C compares with the same one.
  4. Choose Detrend and MCMC as on the page, and press Fit nights. At least two usable nights are needed. The window's own busy bar, at the lower left, says what the fit is doing, for example "Fitting 4 nights… MCMC burn-in 300 of 500". When it is done, the line under the fit options says "✔ Done: 4 nights, MCMC acceptance 0.31."

Nights with no airmass

Fit CSVs saved by SHOBS-P 2.2.20 or later carry each point's airmass. Older fit CSVs do not. With airmass ticked, a night with no airmass among nights that have it is fitted against time only, unlike the others, and its Tc may shift. SHOBS-P marks such a night with "⚠ No airmass in this file: its baseline is fitted against time only, unlike the other nights. Tc may shift." in its Status, in a line under the table (with the nights' dates) and in the Summary. The line under the fit options says, for example, "⚠ 4 nights in use; 1 with no airmass (see above)."

You have two ways out:

  • Open those nights again in SHOBS-P 2.2.20 or later and save their fit CSVs again (Save light-curve CSV…). They then carry airmass.
  • Press Fit every night against time only (beside the line under the table). It unticks airmass and ticks time slope, so every night is fitted the same way.

The window's layout

The window opens at least 900 × 700 and has two parts with a divider between them. Above it: the nights table, the ephemerides, the fit options, the result line, the timing table and the reading in plain words. Below it: the Plots and Summary and ephemerides tabs, which take any extra height when you make the window bigger. The divider opens below the timing table, so the nights table and Fit nights show at once (on a short screen it opens at a little over half the height). Drag the divider up to give the plots more room. The Results row along the bottom holds Save timing CSV…, Save plot PNG…, Corner plot… and Close.

The Multi-night window after a joint fit of three nights: nights table above the divider, plots below.

The joint fit uses one transit shape (Rp/R*, a/R*, b and limb darkening) for all nights, with each night's own mid-transit time, baseline and detrending. A night with a meridian flip gets its own flip step only when that night's own test kept it (the same ΔBIC ≥ 10 rule as above); the Nights part of the Summary says, per night, "flip step fitted: … ppt (ΔBIC …), kept" or "flip step not needed (ΔBIC …; kept only at 10 or more)", and the corner plot shows a kept step as step:N. Then the timing table lists each epoch: Tc (BJD_TDB) ± min, and the O − C against three ephemerides: O − C input (min and σ), O − C alternative, and O − C own fit, the nights' own updated linear ephemeris. Outlier nights are flagged.

Under the table, a few lines read the timing in plain words. For example, when all nights are late or early by about the same amount, and that offset is within what the input ephemeris can predict at these epochs, it says the offset is "most likely the ephemeris, not the data". (A constant offset can also come from a clock or time-system error.) A current ephemeris, typed as the alternative, settles it.

The Plots tab shows the O − C diagrams and the nights stacked with the joint model. One legend sits below the O − C panels: Tc ± fit σ (input, alternative, own), ephemeris ±1σ (shaded) and outlier (> 3σ). Each night is labeled in the right margin with its date, epoch and filter, and the bottom curve is all nights, 5-min bins. Pop out (top right of the tab) opens the plots in a window of their own, SHOBS-P — multi-night plots (planet), which you can make any size or move to another screen. It has its own Save plot PNG… and Close.

The Summary and ephemerides tab gives the joint shape, each ephemeris (the updated one with its uncertainties and their covariance) and notes per night. It also lists a combined ephemeris of these nights with the input T0 and P. That one is for comparison only: it is not valid if the input ephemeris already includes these nights.

The Summary also has one line, "Ephemeris used: T0 … ± … BJD_TDB, P … ± … d — " and the source, that names the input ephemeris the O − C was measured against.

Save timing CSV… writes one row per epoch (mid-time in BJD_TDB with its error, the three O − C, the flag) with the ephemerides and their sources in its header lines, ready for ExoClock. Nights from a series add a line "# Nights from the series TOI-2046b.cvseries". Save plot PNG… saves the plots, with the "Ephemeris used: …" line in small print at the lower left.

Corner plot… (grayed until the nights are fitted) opens SHOBS-P — multi-night corner plot (planet): the corner plot of the joint fit, read the same way as the single-night one above. Its title reads, for example, "TOI-2046 b: joint MCMC posterior, 4 nights". It shows Rp/R*, a/R*, b and one Tc panel per night (headed with the night's date). Tick Show all parameters (baselines, limb darkening) for the rest. A parameter that piles up against its prior range gets the same ⚠ as on a single night. Save PNG… saves it as <planet>_<first night>_to_<last night>_SHOBS-P_multinight_corner.png.

The joint corner plot of three nights: the shared shape, and one Tc panel per night.

Previous: 16. Prepare for a transit night · Next: 18. When the fit says "inconclusive" or "none"

18. When the fit says "inconclusive" or "none"

SHOBS-P does not take every dip for a transit. Before it says ✓ Transit detected it checks that:

  1. the transit model fits clearly better than a flat line (a ΔBIC of 10 or more),
  2. the mid-transit time Tc is pinned down (its uncertainty at most a quarter of the transit's duration),
  3. the depth is believable against the published one,
  4. a plain jump at a gap in the data does not explain the dip just as well.

When a test fails, the verdict line says which, in words. This chapter lists those words, what they mean, and what you can do. Often the honest answer is "this night cannot tell", and that is a useful result too.

The three verdicts

Verdict What it means What you can report
✓ Transit detected All four tests pass. Tc, depth and shape.
✓ … Timing usable; depth unreliable: … Detected, but the depth is far from published and the night gives a reason. Tc. Treat the depth with care.
⚠ Inconclusive The dip beats a flat line, but a jump at a gap explains it as well. Possibly Tc, with that caveat. Not the depth.
✗ No transit measured The dip is not significant, Tc is not pinned, or the depth is impossible. Nothing for submission.

With "No transit measured" the table rows turn gray and the plot title says NO TRANSIT MEASURED (model shown for reference).

The reasons, and what to do

"a flat line fits about as well as a transit (ΔBIC …; a detection needs 10 or more)"

The dip is not significant against the noise. Typical causes: the transit is too shallow for your telescope, the night was noisy, or much of the transit is missing.

  • Compare per 10-min bin on the quality line with the expected depth. A transit needs bins well below its depth.
  • Check that the transit is actually inside your data (the gray band on the plot is the predicted time).
  • Look at Comp health…: a noisy comp raises the scatter for every point.
  • Try fewer, better comps with Use comps… (the fit reruns at once).

"No detection with Both: …"

With both airmass and time slope ticked, the two trends can together absorb a transit, especially when airmass and time change together through the night. SHOBS-P then refits with each trend alone, and when one of them detects the transit, the verdict starts, for example: "No detection with Both: airmass and time trends are nearly the same over this night (correlation +0.97) and may absorb the transit together. Airmass alone detects it (ΔBIC 32). Untick time slope and fit again only if that trend is not real (a slope artifact can also read as a detection)."

"Tc is not pinned down (± … h for a …-hour transit)"

The fit cannot tell when the transit happened. This usually means ingress or egress (or both) were not observed, or the noise is as large as the dip. More baseline and full coverage on the next night is the fix.

"the depth, …%, is …x the published …; a real transit of this planet cannot be that deep (shallow)"

The fitted depth is more than three times (or less than a third of) the published depth, and far outside the error. Something in the light curve made the dip: a jump, a trend, a bad comp, or a calibration problem.

  • A much deeper dip almost always comes from the light curve itself. Check the comps (Comp health…), the flats, and the residuals panel for steps.
  • A much shallower dip can come from a neighbor star's light inside the aperture. Look at the image, and consider a smaller aperture (see 8. Photometry page basics).

"a plain …% jump after the …-minute gap at … h fits as well as the transit … so the dip may be the jump, not the planet"

This is the most common reason for ⚠ Inconclusive. Your data have a gap of 20 minutes or more (a meridian flip, a re-centering, a cloud), and a simple step in brightness at that gap explains the data as well as a transit does. A flip, a camera rotation or a guiding re-acquisition can all shift the star on the sensor and leave such a step.

  • If the gap is a meridian flip, the next time put the flip in the baseline, or choose a night with no flip (see 16. Prepare for a transit night).
  • Check Comp health… in the one-night view: a comp that jumps at the flip is a common cause. Replace it with Use comps….
  • Tc may still be usable; the depth is not.

When the step test is run but the transit wins, the notes say so as information: "Step check: a …% jump at the …-minute gap fits worse than the transit …".

On a night with a meridian flip that SHOBS-P knows about (Mount set to German equatorial), the flip gets its own test instead: the fit tries a flip step at the flip and keeps it only at ΔBIC 10 or more (chapter 17, "A meridian flip in the night"). When the step is kept, the flat line in the verdict test carries the same step, so the verdict reads, for example, "Inconclusive. The dip beats a flat line with the flip step (ΔBIC …), but …" or "Transit detected: ΔBIC … against a flat line with the flip step …". The plain-jump test above is then not run again at that gap. With too little baseline on one side of the flip, the step and the transit cannot be told apart, and the verdict is often inconclusive: put the flip well into the baseline next time.

"Timing usable; depth unreliable: depth …% vs published …, and …"

The transit was detected, but its depth is more than 3σ from the published one, and the night shows why it might be off:

  • "a …% jump at the …-minute gap fits nearly as well", or
  • "the baseline is one-sided (… min before, … min after)": less than 20 minutes of data on one side of the transit.

Report the timing; flag the depth. The report's NOTES carry this sentence automatically.

Notes that point at the cause

The notes beside the table add detail. The ones you will see most with a weak result:

  • "Little or no baseline before ingress (after egress) (… min before, … min after). A one-sided baseline lets a trend imitate depth or shift Tc." Take more baseline next time.
  • "A …-minute gap in the data inside the transit. Ingress, egress, or the bottom may be missing…"
  • "The data start … min after the fitted ingress: ingress was not observed." (or "The data end … before the fitted egress")
  • "Almost no points inside the predicted transit window: the transit was missed (clouds, a gap, or the ephemeris)."
  • "… = … is …σ from the published …. The light curve pulls hard against the known shape: look for a trend, a bad comp, or clouds."
  • "With the shape left free, Rp/R* goes to …. The data alone do not pin the shape…"
  • "With the shape left free the fit is not constrained (…): this light curve alone cannot fix a/R* and b, so the Free shape column is left empty. The reported values use the published a/R* and b." The free fit ran away. This is information, not a fault of the main fit.
  • "… is limited by its prior range: its MCMC samples pile up against …, so the error bar is not a measurement." (orange) The data did not constrain that parameter; its value and error come from the allowed range. Open Corner plot… to see it (chapter 17). The same sentence goes into the report's notes. For b against 0 the note is only information: a central transit, with b read as an upper limit.
  • "Leaving out C2 makes the transit … deeper (…σ)…": one comparison star moves the result. Check it in Comp health… (the link in the note opens it).
  • "The comp choice moves … by more than its statistical error (…). Treat the comp spread below as a systematic error, or try other comps."

Error-bar notes (χ²ν)

  • "The per-point error bars are about …x larger than the real scatter (reduced χ² …)." The error model is off, most often because the camera gain (EGAIN) is missing or wrong in the headers, or the frames were calibrated in other software. The fit keeps the larger error bars, so its uncertainties are on the safe side. The photometry log's Photon noise line says which gain was used.
  • "The scatter is about …x the per-point error bars (reduced χ² …)…" There is noise the error model leaves out: thin cloud, dew, seeing or tracking changes, a noisy comp. The fit scales its error bars up to match.

Cloud-flagged frames: "No usable baseline after egress: the 31 frames there were flagged cloud"

Photometry flags frames where the comparison stars lose light (see 12. Flags and bad data). The fit leaves those frames out. If they were the whole baseline on one side, the baseline note says so, naming the frames and their times.

When a night has cloud-flagged frames, a button appears next to Cancel: Refit with flagged frames (errors ×2). It puts those frames back with their error bars doubled and refits.

To try it:

  1. Click Refit with flagged frames (errors ×2).
  2. Read the verdict. It starts with [Includes N cloud-flagged frames (error bars ×2).], and the plot shows those frames as hollow orange circles.
  3. Compare it with the normal fit (click Fit transit to get it back).

Caution: Thin cloud dims the target and the comps by slightly different amounts. Report a fit that includes flagged frames only when it agrees with the fit without them, and say so. The report's NOTES record it.

You can also remove a short bad stretch by hand with Trim (see 17. Fit a transit and submit it).

"No transit of … is predicted during these data"

If no predicted transit falls inside your data, a fit cannot measure anything. When you press Fit transit, SHOBS-P says where the nearest transit is (for example "… 3.2 h after the last point") and asks Fit anyway?. No shows the raw light curve with an arrow pointing to the predicted transit. Check the planet on the Input page, the date of the lights, and the computer's clock. If this was really a variability run, use Variables mode.

Saving a weak result

You can still save the files.

  • For No transit measured, SHOBS-P asks "This fit did not measure a transit: … Save it anyway (for your records, not for submission)?".
  • For Inconclusive, it asks "This fit is inconclusive: … The report will say so in its notes. Tc may be worth reporting with that caveat; the depth is not. Save it?".

The verdict and its reason go into the report's NOTES, so anyone reading it later sees them.

Tip: A night of "no transit measured" on a predicted transit can be a real finding (for example, a planet whose transit time has drifted far from its ephemeris). Before you conclude that, rule out the ordinary causes above, and observe it again.

Previous: 17. Fit a transit and submit it · Next: 19. Reports

19. Reports

This chapter covers the files the Transit fit page writes and where they go:

  • the AAVSO exoplanet report, for the AAVSO Exoplanet Database,
  • the ExoFOP package, for TESS targets,
  • the ExoClock upload file, for ExoClock,
  • the plot PNG, the corner plot and the light-curve CSV,
  • the settings file and the field image saved with a report,
  • opening an EXOTIC report to compare its fit with SHOBS-P's.

All the save buttons are in the Results row at the bottom of the Transit fit page. They need a finished fit (otherwise: "Fit a transit first."). If the comparison-star check is still running, wait a few seconds: SHOBS-P asks you to save when it is done, so the files carry its result.

Where files go

If the Input page has an Output folder, every save goes there without a dialog, and the status bar says what was saved. A file already there is never overwritten: a new copy gets _2, _3 and so on. The AAVSO report, light-curve CSV, fit CSV, plot PNG, and the corner plot, settings file and field image saved beside them share one number, so they sort together. Without an Output folder, each save asks where to put the file.

See 22. Files SHOBS-P writes for the full list of names.

The AAVSO exoplanet report

To save and upload it:

  1. Fill in Observer code on the Input page (AAVSO report box). It goes in the report's #OBSCODE line.
  2. Optionally type a note in Notes (in Variable View); it goes in the report's NOTES. If the notes name a star that is not in this reduction, SHOBS-P asks before saving (chapter 17, "Notes that name another star").
  3. Click Save AAVSO exoplanet report…. The file is called AAVSO_<planet>_<DD-MON-YYYY>_SHOBS-P.txt, for example AAVSO_KELT-16b_05-OCT-2026_SHOBS-P.txt.
  4. The status bar says "Saved … — upload to the AAVSO Exoplanet Database (check its current instructions) with Open AAVSO Exoplanet Database… on the Results row." Click that button, beside Save AAVSO exoplanet report…, to open the submit page in your browser.
  5. Upload the file there.

When the page opens, SHOBS-P marks the report's night as sent (the night table's Sent column). If that night was already sent in another report, it asks first whether to open the page anyway, because the AAVSO would hold duplicates. See "The duplicate warning" in 15. Submit to AAVSO.

The report uses the same format as EXOTIC's (#TYPE=EXOPLANET): a header with your site, filter, exposure, binning, the priors and the fitted results, then one line per frame with the BJD_TDB time, the relative flux, its error and the airmass. Times are always BJD_TDB.

#BINNING is how the camera binned the frames, read from the frames' headers (XBINNING/YBINNING), for example 2x2 for MicroObservatory frames; 1x1 when the headers name no binning. SHOBS-P's own Binning box is not the camera's binning, so it goes into the NOTES instead, for example SHOBS-P processing bin 1 (OSC superpixel base: 2x2 camera pixels per measured pixel).

Note: the header carries your site latitude and longitude (#OBSLAT, #OBSLON), as the format requires, so they are uploaded with your data. Round them in the Site box before saving if you prefer not to publish your exact location; a few kilometers makes no difference to the BJD times.

SHOBS-P adds to the NOTES line, automatically, anything a reader should know:

  • the verdict, when it is not "detected" (for example SHOBS-P verdict INCONCLUSIVE: …),
  • "timing usable, depth unreliable", with the reason,
  • any trimmed span,
  • cloud-flagged frames put back into the fit,
  • times left in JD_UTC from a source report (see below),
  • SHOBS-P's processing bin (see above),
  • the O − C against the predicted Tc, for example SHOBS-P O - C +5.38 +/- 11.15 min (+0.48 sigma; …) vs predicted Tc … at epoch 2362; ephemeris from … (not for "no transit", or for times left in JD_UTC); when the Archive was down and SHOBS-P used its saved answer, the source ends , from cache (fetched 12 Oct 2026),
  • on a night with a meridian flip, the flip time and the flip-step decision, written whether or not the step was kept, for example SHOBS-P: meridian flip at BJD_TDB 2463218.67686; baseline step -1.45+/-0.05% (kept, dBIC 629) … or SHOBS-P: meridian flip at BJD_TDB …; Flip step not needed: …, no step in this fit. A later refit of the report (by SHOBS-P or another program) then uses the same flip time. #DETREND_PARAMETERS is unchanged: it lists data columns, and the step is in the NOTES,
  • one line of reduction settings, starting SHOBS-P settings: (aperture, comps, check, frames left out, trimmed frames, detrending, gain, calibration, and, for stacked points, points are stacks of 40 frames (effective exposure 30 s)),
  • a parameter limited by its prior range (chapter 17, "The corner plot").

The settings file and the field image

Beside the report, SHOBS-P writes:

  • …_settings.txt: the full settings of the reduction, one per line: Source, Aperture (what the night was measured with), Binning (camera and processing), Comps, Check, Frames skipped at photometry, Frames left out (by flag and frame number), Frames trimmed, Detrend, Calibration (the masters, and the dark's exposure) and the Gain with where it came from, Scintillation (on or off, with the telescope aperture and elevation the error bars used), Telescope aperture box when the night was measured (nights measured with 2.3.1 or later), and Stacking for stacked points. With it, you (or anyone you send it to) can reduce the night again the same way. Some rows in more detail:
    • Comps lists only what this night really measured, for example Comps: C1, C2 (Use comps choice; measured: C1, C2, C3 (missing in 14 frames: off the frame edge), SP (as spare)). A comp no frame of the night measured is not listed. Without a Use comps choice, the (measured: …) part is added only when it says something new.
    • Frames skipped at photometry (reason: frame numbers): frames the photometry run could not measure, for example every comp lost (off the frame edge): 158-213, or none. Frames left out (flag: frame numbers) is a different list: frames the fit left out because of their flags.
    • Calibration names the dark flat master when there is one, and with no bias adds dark flat used as the bias (no bias frames).
    • Gain on camera-binned frames says how the camera binned, for example Gain 0.768 e-/ADU (header EGAIN); camera bin 3x3 averaged, so 6.91 e- per stored ADU used (from the sky's noise) (chapter 7).
    • Stacking: stacks of 40 frames (effective exposure 30 s) (sigma-clipped mean, whole-pixel shifts, never across a flip) (chapter 8).
    • On an alt-az night where the field turned by about 180° between two frames, a row Warning: field turned 180 deg between frames … on an alt-az night (a meridian flip? check Mount) (chapter 8).
  • …_field.png: the reference frame exactly as shot (never turned or mirrored), filling the picture on a smooth dark-gray background. The target, comps and check star are marked with their apertures to scale, with a scale bar at the bottom right. Yellow N and E arrows in the top-right corner show where north and east lie on the frame, from the plate solution. On a frame that is not solved, the corner says "Orientation unknown (the image is not solved)" instead. The line under the title (frame name, size, plate scale, apertures, chart ID) wraps onto a second line when it is long. When the target is itself an AAVSO sequence star (HD 219134 is one), only its own marker is drawn, with no extra sequence ring beside it. The log says "Field image saved (as shot, N/E arrows): …". When the AAVSO's chart server answers, the AAVSO chart of the same field and size is saved next to it as …_aavso_chart.png. That chart is north up and east left, so use the arrows to match the two. If the chart server does not answer, the log says so and Save field image… fetches it again later. No field image is made for an opened EXOTIC report's fit, nor from another star's frame after Load series… (the log says "Reduce a night of … first; the field image comes from a reduction …"; chapter 10).

Caution: The AAVSO's exoplanet section sets its own rules for what it accepts. Check its current instructions before your first upload.

The ExoFOP package (TESS targets)

ExoFOP-TESS collects follow-up observations of TESS targets (TOIs and TIC stars). Uploading needs an ExoFOP account.

To save the package:

  1. Click Save ExoFOP package…. Without an Output folder, SHOBS-P asks for a folder ("Folder for the ExoFOP package").
  2. SHOBS-P writes a folder named ExoFOP_<planet>_<YYYYMMDD>_<observer code>_SHOBS-P (the date is the UTC date of the first frame). Inside it:
    • …_lightcurve.csv: columns BJD_TDB, rel_flux, rel_flux_err, airmass, detrended_flux, model, residual.
    • …_fit.txt: the fitted values and the notes ExoFOP asks for (telescope and camera, pixel scale, binning (the camera's, and SHOBS-P's processing bin), aperture, transit coverage, and the comparison-star spread as a systematic error). When the planet's TOI status and ExoClock priority were looked up (chapter 16), the summary at the top has a TESS TOI line (with the target's ExoFOP-TESS address) and an ExoClock line. On a night with a meridian flip it has the Meridian flip lines and the Flip step … line, and Detrending says airmass, meridian flip step when the step was kept (chapter 17).
    • …_plot.png: the transit plot.

The package is a plain folder, not a zip, and it does not contain the AAVSO report; save that separately if you want to upload it too. Saving the same fit again into the same place makes nothing new ("ExoFOP package unchanged: …").

To upload it:

  1. Click Open ExoFOP…. SHOBS-P opens the target's ExoFOP-TESS page, found by its TOI number, else its TIC number. If neither is known, ExoFOP-TESS's home page opens and you search for the star there.
  2. The first time, a message explains the upload: log in, use Add new in the Time Series section for the light curve and fit notes, and Add new under Files for the plot, with the same data tag.
  3. The data tag has the form YYYYMMDD_username_description_n, for example 20261015_jsmith_SHOBS-P-transit_1. The description may use only letters, numbers and hyphens.

As with the AAVSO pages, opening ExoFOP marks the night of the package you saved last as sent, and warns first if it was already sent in another package.

The ExoClock file

ExoClock (exoclock.space) collects transit times to keep planets' ephemerides up to date for the Ariel mission. Save for ExoClock… writes two files:

  • ExoClock_<planet>_<YYYYMMDD>.txt, for example ExoClock_TOI-2046b_20261027.txt: the planet's name with no spaces (as ExoClock spells it) and the UTC date of the first frame. A few header lines start with # (planet, time format, filter, exposure, observer code, number of frames). Then one line per frame with three columns: the time in JD_UTC at mid-exposure, the relative flux (divided by its median, not detrended; ExoClock fits its own baseline) and its measurement error (ExoClock scales the errors itself). Only the frames the fit used are in it, as in the AAVSO report; flagged frames you put back with Fit with flagged are left out too.
  • ExoClock_info.txt: what to pick on ExoClock's upload form for that file: Planet (for example TOI-2046b), Time format JD_UTC, Time stamp Mid-exposure, Flux format Flux, Filter (a color camera's TG is listed as Johnson V), Exposure time in seconds, and the upload page's address.

Caution: ExoClock's form starts on Exposure start. Change it to Mid-exposure, or every time is off by half an exposure. The status line reminds you: "✔ Saved. On the form, pick Mid-exposure (…; what to pick is in ExoClock_info.txt)."

Go to ExoClock… opens the upload page (https://www.exoclock.space/upload/) in your browser; log in and upload the ExoClock_…txt file. As with the other upload pages, the night of the file you saved last is marked sent (Sent shows ExoClock and the date), and SHOBS-P warns first if that night was already sent. Chapter 17 walks through the form.

The plot PNG and the light-curve CSV

  • Save plot PNG… writes <planet>_<filter>_<date>_SHOBS-P_transit.png: the plot with a header above it holding the planet line, the verdict (in its color), the quality line, the parameter table and the notes. It works for report fits too. It is the best single picture of a result to share or keep. The legend sits under the residual panel.
  • The corner plot of the fit is saved beside it, as <planet>_<filter>_<date>_SHOBS-P_transit_corner.png (chapter 17). Save corner plot… (Results row) opens the corner plot window, whose Save PNG… saves it on its own.
  • Save light-curve CSV… writes the general light-curve CSV, the same as in Variables mode: one row per frame with magnitudes, errors, airmass, flags and much more (see 9. Reduce a variable-star night). It is in magnitudes, not the transit fit's relative flux.
  • With a fit shown, the same button also writes the fit CSV beside it: <planet>_<filter>_<date>_SHOBS-P_transit_fit.csv, with the columns BJD_TDB, rel_flux, rel_flux_err, model, residual, flag, airmass, segment and (from 2.3.4) rel_flux_err_raw. rel_flux is the raw relative flux (not detrended); segment says before flip or after flip on a night with a meridian flip. rel_flux_err is the error bar the fit used: the raw error (rel_flux_err_raw, from photon noise, sky and scintillation) times the scale S, and a # Errors: line above the columns gives S, the raw reduced chi-square and the red-noise β, so a reduced chi-square rebuilt from rel_flux_err (about 1 by design) is not mistaken for the raw one. Trimmed points are included and flagged trimmed. Without an Output folder it is named after the light-curve CSV you chose (for example mine.csv and mine_transit_fit.csv). For a report fit only the fit CSV is written; if the report's times stayed in JD_UTC, the first column is JD_UTC. For the detrended flux, use the CSV in the ExoFOP package. Under the # Created line, comment lines starting with # give the planet, filter, fitted Tc and verdict, the O − C, what the columns hold, and the time of the meridian flip if there was one. Multi-night… reads the night back from them (chapter 17).
  • Save field image… saves the field image on its own (see above).

The field image of HD 219134: the frame as shot, with N and E arrows at the top right and the stars marked.

Compare with an EXOTIC report

You can open an AAVSO exoplanet report written by EXOTIC (or by SHOBS-P, or any program using that format), and SHOBS-P refits its light curve and shows both results side by side. This is useful to check a reduction, or to look again at an older observation.

To open a report:

  1. On the Transit fit page, click Open AAVSO exoplanet report (EXOTIC)… and choose the .txt file.
  2. The old plot clears and the page says "Report loaded. Set the detrend and press Fit transit …" (a report without an ephemeris first has one looked up). Press Fit transit. A brown banner above the plot says what is being fitted, for example "Fitting EXOTIC report: WASP-12 b, 05-OCT-2026, 210 points from AAVSO_WASP-12b.txt. Your … photometry is kept; choose its night above (or Close report) to go back."
  3. When the fit is done, the table has a fourth column with the report's own values (headed with its software, for example EXOTIC 4.3.2 report), and the plot shows the report's model as a pink dashed line.

Things to know:

  • The planet. SHOBS-P uses the report's planet, unless the Input page names the same planet.
  • The ephemeris. If the report has no predicted mid-transit, SHOBS-P looks one up (NASA Exoplanet Archive, then an EXOTIC inits file next to the report). Without any, no O − C is given.
  • Time systems. BJD_TDB is used as it is. HJD is moved to BJD_TDB (+69.184 s). JD_UTC is converted when the star's position is known from the Input page or the lookup. If it is not, the times are kept as JD_UTC and a warning says Tc may be off by up to 8 minutes, with no usable O − C; enter the star on the Input page and open the report again.
  • What is not available. Comp health…, Use comps…, Refit with flagged frames and the comp check need your own photometry, so they are off for a report.
  • Going back. Click Close report, or pick one of your nights in Night. Your own photometry was kept.

You can save the AAVSO report, ExoFOP package, fit CSV and plot of a report fit like any other.

Previous: 18. When the fit says "inconclusive" or "none" · Next: 20. Scan a field

20. Scan a field

Discovery mode measures every star in the field on every frame, and points out the ones that vary more than stars of the same brightness. You need no target: any series of frames of one patch of sky, with calibration frames, will do.

This chapter covers:

  • setting up a field scan,
  • the scan settings (faintest magnitude, detection threshold, candidate level),
  • reading the scan table and plots,
  • the kinds of candidate and the "check first" warnings.

21. Follow a candidate covers what to do with a candidate next.

Tip: A candidate is a lead, not a discovery. Most are explained by something ordinary: a bright neighbor, a bad pixel, saturation, a meridian flip. The scan tells you which to check first.

Set up the scan

  1. Click Discovery in the sidebar. The window turns plum.
  2. On the Input page, choose the Bias, Dark, Flat and Lights folders and press Scan folders, as in any mode (see 5. Input). There is no Target box in Discovery mode.
  3. If the lights carry neither a plate solution nor the telescope's pointing in their headers, a box Field center (only needed when the headers do not say) appears. Type roughly where the frames point, in RA (hours) and Dec (degrees). A few arcminutes off is fine. Your planetarium program or mount can tell you.
  4. Make sure the Optics box (focal length, pixel size, binning) is right: the solve depends on the plate scale.
  5. Blink the lights and reject the bad ones (see 6. Blink).
  6. On the Calibrate page, press Build masters and calibrate (see 7. Calibrate).

When calibration ends, SHOBS-P opens the Photometry page and solves the field straight away.

The Photometry page in Discovery mode

The page title is Photometry: field scan. The step strip shows 1 Solve → 2 Scan field.

Once solved, catalog stars are circled and known variables (from VSX and SIMBAD) are marked with red circles or ×. The Circles box defaults to APASS DR9 in this mode, because an AAVSO sequence covers only charted variables.

The Field box on the right sums up the solve: the plate scale and center, how many catalog stars were placed, how many stars were detected, how many are saturated, and how many known or suspected variables lie in the frame. Saturation is judged on the frame's raw pixels, where binning and calibration cannot hide a clipped core; the line reads, for example, "3 saturated on this frame (raw pixels; measured, not candidates)." (It shows "…" for a moment while the raw frame is read.)

If the solve fails, the step strip says 1 Not solved. Check the field center on the Input page and the Optics box, then press Solve again (top right). You can scan an unsolved field, but then:

  • magnitudes are instrumental, not on a catalog scale,
  • there is no check against VSX and SIMBAD, so every candidate is "unchecked",
  • a typed Faintest magnitude is ignored.

Apertures

The scan uses the Aperture r, sky in and sky out boxes, like photometry. Press Suggest… and then Use these aperture sizes to set them from the field's seeing (see 8. Photometry page basics). Suggest… works in Discovery too: its aperture test uses bright, isolated, unsaturated field stars, since there are no comps. Also check raw sat ADU: saturated stars are measured but never listed as candidates.

Watch or leave out stars

Above the image, Click a star to offers two choices:

  • ● Watch it: the star is always measured and listed in the scan (as a "W" row), with its own light curve and period search.
  • ✕ Exclude it: the star is left out of the scan, for example a star you know is a ghost or sits on a bad column.

Right-click a marker to undo. Both lists appear in the Watched and excluded stars box, where Remove selected also undoes one. Known variables in the field… lists the known variables SHOBS-P found in the frame.

The scan settings

The Scan settings box on the right holds:

Setting Default What it does
Faintest magnitude blank The faintest catalog magnitude that can be a candidate. Blank: SHOBS-P stops where the field's typical scatter reaches 0.25 mag, and says where in the log.
Detection threshold (σ) 5.0 How far above the sky noise a star must be to be measured. Lower finds fainter stars, and more noise.
Candidate at (× normal) 3.0 How many times more than normal a star must scatter to be a candidate.
Most stars to measure 800 The brightest stars first, up to this number. The log says when the limit is reached; raise it to go fainter.
Period search (days) 0.02 to 20 The period range of the candidates' periodograms. Remembered between sessions. (A range left at the old default, 0.05, moves to 0.02 once.)
Leave out crowded stars (neighbor in the aperture or sky ring) on Skip stars where a neighbor's light reaching the aperture is 1 % or more of the star's own.

"Normal" means the typical scatter of stars of the same brightness in this field. A star at × normal 3 scatters three times as much as its neighbors in magnitude. Saturated stars and stars at the edge of the frame are measured but never listed as candidates.

Saturated means the star's raw core reached raw sat ADU on 2 or more frames (or it was near saturation on the reference frame). A star clipped on one frame only (a cosmic ray or a hot pixel can do that) is still a candidate, with a caution. The log counts both.

Crowded is worked out from how much of each neighbor's light falls inside the star's aperture, using every detected star and the catalog stars on the frame. At 1 % or more of the star's own light, the star is left out (when the box is ticked). At 0.2 % or more, a star that is kept (box unticked, or a watch star) gets a blended neighbor: check caution.

The Period search (days) range is Discovery's own; it does not change the Variables Output page's range. After a scan, change a box and press Enter (or click into another box): the candidates' periods are searched again over the new range, without scanning again. The busy bar says Searching the candidates' periods over 0.02–20 d… meanwhile, and the status bar then says ✔ Period search 0.02–20 d done. Before a scan, the range is kept for the next one.

Run the scan

  1. Press Scan field (top right).
  2. Wait. The busy bar at the bottom of the window (chapter 3) says Scanning 412 stars in 38 frames…, then counts the frames, for example Field scan… 12 of 38, about 1:20 left. Each frame is calibrated, aligned and every star measured, so a scan takes about as long as a photometry run with many stars. Cancel run stops it.
  3. When the scan ends, SHOBS-P opens the Output step, now the Output – Field scan page, and the status bar says "Field scan: N candidate variable(s)."

Before measuring each star's scatter, SHOBS-P lines its light curve up across any meridian flip or rotator move, so a flip cannot pass for a variable star. On an alt-az mount there are no flips: the frames are aligned following the field rotation.

Scan stacks of frames (optional)

After a scan, the Photometry page shows the same Stack row as the other modes (chapter 8, "Stacking when comps are weak"). Discovery has no comps, so its line is never orange; it only offers stacking, for example "Stacking (optional): stacks of 6 frames (18 s each); 60 frames → 10 points. Time cap 5.0 min: a stack hides events faster than itself." Stacking lets fainter stars into the scan, but a stack of 5 minutes cannot show an eclipse or flare shorter than that.

To scan the stacks, set Stack N and press Stack. SHOBS-P makes the stacks, then runs the field scan on them instead of the single frames. A scan needs at least 10 stacks; with fewer, Stack says "That makes only K stacks; a field scan needs at least 10. Use a smaller N." The per-frame scan is kept: the Points box (Per frame / Stacked) on the Stack row switches between the two scans.

Read the Field scan page

The hint under the title sums up the scan: the number of stars and frames, how the magnitudes were calibrated (for example "on the catalog V … scale (zero point from n field stars, ± 0.012; untransformed)"), and how many stars reached the candidate level. If none did, the ten nearest misses are listed as ~1, ~2 and so on.

The plots

  • Scatter vs brightness (left): every star, scatter against magnitude, on a log scale. The blue line is the typical scatter. Candidates are red circles with their rank number; known variables are orange squares; saturated stars are gray ×. Click a point to see that star; click it again to clear the selection. You can also pick several stars on the plot (chapter 21).
  • The selected star's light curve (top right): red for a candidate, blue otherwise. Dashed purple lines mark a flip, rotator move or gap.
  • Lomb-Scargle periodogram (bottom right): the red line is the best period. Periods longer than about half the data span are grayed ("longer than the data can test").

The table

Column Meaning
# Rank by how much the star exceeds normal. ~1, ~2 are near misses; W are watch stars.
Star / position SHOBS-P's label (S012), the catalog name if any, and RA/Dec when solved.
Mag Magnitude on the catalog scale (or instrumental if unsolved).
Scatter The star's scatter in magnitudes, robust against outliers.
× normal Scatter divided by the typical scatter at that magnitude.
Best period (d) The best Lomb-Scargle period. no period (drift) means the best peak sat at the long end of the search: a slow drift, not a period. at the short end: search shorter means it sat at the short end.
B−V Color, when measured.
Check first Reasons to doubt the candidate (below).
VSX KNOWN (VSX): name type, or not in VSX or SIMBAD — possible new variable. When VSX could not be reached it reads VSX not reached — possible new variable (only SIMBAD was checked; solve and scan again later).

Click a row to draw that star. You can select several rows. Click the selected row again, or press Esc, to clear the selection.

Tip: The period search range comes from the Period search (days) boxes in Scan settings (0.02 to 20 days by default). See "The scan settings" above. When a known variable in the list has a VSX period outside that range, an orange line under the table says so (⚠ VSX period outside the search (…): …), with a button such as Search 0.02–40 d instead to widen it in one click.

Check first

The Check first column lists warnings that often explain a candidate away:

  • near saturation: check linearity: the star's peak came close to saturation in some frames.
  • clipped on 1 frame: check linearity: the star's raw core reached saturation on one frame (or a few, for a star not counted as saturated).
  • blended neighbor: check: a neighbor's light reaches the aperture (see "The scan settings").
  • VSX period … d outside the search: a known variable whose period the search could not find.
  • change begins at flip: check / change begins after a gap: check: the light curve changes level at a flip, rotator move or gap, which points at the instrument rather than the star.
  • noise begins at flip: check / noise begins after a gap: check: the same for a jump in noise.

The candidate kinds

Mark candidates on image switches to the Photometry page and draws numbered squares on the candidates. With rows selected in the table, it marks only those (Mark N selected on image). The style of each square says its kind; hover over it for the reason:

  • Possible new variable (bright magenta): not in VSX or SIMBAD, and nothing to check first.
  • Known variable (VSX/SIMBAD) (pale purple).
  • Candidate to check first (tan): it has a Check first warning.
  • Unchecked (no sky positions) (gray): the field was not solved.

Tick Candidates only in the View box to hide everything else on the image. Press the button again (now Clear candidate marks) to remove the marks.

Known variables

  • Known variables measured… lists every VSX and SIMBAD variable in the frame and what the scan saw for each, in the column In this scan: VARIED: a scan candidate, steady tonight, within the noise, somewhat more scatter than normal, measured on too few frames, saturated: clipped, so not usable tonight, or not measured (faint, saturated, blended, or not detected). It is a quick way to see whether your setup catches the variables you know are there.
  • Hide known variables removes known variables from the table, the plot and the marks on the image, so the new ones stand out. Their rank numbers are kept, so the table may jump from #1 to #3, matching the marks on the image. It also works when the field's variable-star check finishes after the scan.

Save the results

The Results row holds:

  • Save plot PNG…: the three plots with a one-line summary, <field>_<filter>_<date>_SHOBS-P_scan.png (<field> is the Input page's Star ID if one is left from another mode, otherwise field).
  • Save CSV…: one row per measured star (position, magnitude, scatter, expected scatter, excess, candidate rank and kind, period, VSX name).
  • Export candidates CSV…: one row per candidate, with color and VSX period, field_scan_candidates.csv.
  • Export all light curves CSV…: every star's light curve, one row per star per frame, field_scan_lightcurves.csv.
  • Back returns to the Photometry page.

See 21. Follow a candidate for what to do with them.

Previous: 19. Reports · Next: 21. Follow a candidate

21. Follow a candidate

A field scan gives you leads. One night is never enough to call a star variable: you want to see it change again, on another night, with the same behavior. This chapter covers:

  • deciding which candidates are worth following,
  • putting them on the watch list,
  • measuring them on more nights,
  • exporting their light curves, for your own analysis or a VSX submission.

Decide which candidates are worth following

Work down the scan table (see 20. Scan a field) and, for each candidate:

  1. Read Check first. A candidate whose change begins at a flip or a gap, that is near saturation or clipped, or that has a blended neighbor, is most likely instrumental. Follow it only if its light curve also changes well away from the flip or gap.
  2. Read VSX. KNOWN (VSX): … is already cataloged. It may still be worth following if the catalog lacks a period or type, but it is not a new variable. Tick Hide known variables to take them out of the table, the plot and the image while you work through the new ones.
  3. Look at its light curve (click the row). A real variable usually changes smoothly, or repeats; a single-frame spike is usually a cosmic ray, a satellite or a hot pixel. A Best period of no period (drift) means the periodogram's best peak sat at the long end of the search: the star drifts slowly, which may be the star or may be the instrument. Click the row again (or press Esc) to clear the selection.
  4. Click Mark candidates on image and look at the star in the image. A bright neighbor, a diffraction spike, a column defect or the frame edge can each make a star "vary".
  5. Look at × normal together with Scatter. A faint star at 3× normal may scatter 0.1 mag from noise alone; a bright star at 3× normal with 0.02 mag scatter is a cleaner lead.

Pick stars on the scatter plot

Not every interesting star reaches the candidate level. On the Scatter vs brightness plot you can pick any measured star:

  • Click a point to pick that star (and draw its light curve); click it again to clear.
  • Ctrl+click adds a star to the picked set, or takes it out.
  • Drag a box to pick every star inside it.

Picked stars are ringed on the plot. Press Mark on image to mark them on the Photometry image: candidates keep their rank number, other stars are labeled with SHOBS-P's label (for example S066) and drawn as Picked on the scan plot. From there you can look at each one, and watch it (below).

Put a candidate on the watch list

Watch stars are measured on every later run, with their own light curves. There are three ways to add one:

  • On the Output – Field scan page, click the candidate's row (or its point on the scatter plot) and press Watch selected star. The status bar says "Watching …".
  • In Known variables measured…, select a known variable and press Watch selected. This works even for one the scan could not measure (it is watched by its sky position).
  • On the Photometry page, with ● Watch it chosen, click the star in the image.

A watch star is remembered by its catalog name and sky position when the field is solved. A star that is not a labeled catalog star on an unsolved image is remembered only by its pixel position; the status bar warns "It is not a labeled catalog star, so click it again on later nights."

Measure another night

Night after night in Discovery mode

  1. On the Input page, change the Lights folder (and the calibration folders, if they differ) to the next night's frames and press Scan folders.
  2. Blink and calibrate as before. The field is solved again, and the watch stars are found by name and sky position. The log says how many were found, for example "Watch stars: 2 of 3 found on this frame".
  3. Press Scan field. Watch stars appear in the table as W rows (with their periodogram), whether or not they reach the candidate level that night.

Compare the two nights. A star that is a candidate again, with similar behavior, is a much stronger lead.

Discovery watch stars are remembered between sessions, with their sky positions. After the next solve, the ones that fall on the new frame are watched again, and the log says, for example, "Watch stars remembered for this field (2): …". A star clicked on an unsolved frame has no sky position, so it cannot be found again and is not remembered. Right-click a watch star (or Remove selected) to stop watching it.

Tip: Discovery keeps the watch list, but not each night's measurements. To keep a candidate's history across nights, follow it in Variables mode with a series, as below.

Over many nights (Variables mode)

A series in Variables mode keeps the watch list and every night's measurements in one file. There are two ways to use it.

The candidate as the target (best when one candidate matters most):

  1. Switch to Variables mode. Marked stars and calibrated frames are kept.
  2. On the Input page, type the candidate's catalog name in Star ID (the name in brackets in the scan table), or type its RA (hours) and Dec (degrees).
  3. On the Photometry page mark it as the Target, choose comparison stars and a check star, and press Run photometry.
  4. Save series on the Output page. On each new night, Load series… first, then reduce the night and answer Yes to add it.

You get the full Variables-mode tools: the period search with its significance tests, the comparison-star health check, Time of max/min and an AAVSO report. See 9. Reduce a variable-star night, 10. Build a multi-night series and 13. Look for a period.

The candidate as a watch star (best for several candidates in one field):

  1. In Variables mode, pick any good star in the field as the target, with comps and a check star.
  2. Make sure the candidates are on the watch list (they stay on it when you switch modes in the same session).
  3. Run photometry and save the series. The watch list is saved with it.
  4. On later nights, Load series… before reducing; the watch stars are found again after the solve and measured on every run.
  5. On the Output page, click Watch stars… to see each watch star's light curve across all nights, measured against the comparison stars in use.

Export the light curves

What you want Where File
One row per measured star, with scatter, excess and period Save CSV… on the Field scan page <field>_<filter>_<date>_SHOBS-P_scan.csv
One row per candidate, ready to start a VSX new-variable submission Export candidates CSV… field_scan_candidates.csv
Every star's light curve from one scan (label, JD, magnitude) Export all light curves CSV… field_scan_lightcurves.csv
Watch stars' light curves across a series Export watch light curves CSV… in the Watch stars window watch_stars.csv
The target's full light curve Save light-curve CSV or Export… on the Variables Output page see chapter 22

Scan magnitudes are on the catalog's scale (untransformed) when the field was solved, and instrumental otherwise. Every CSV starts with a # Created … line.

Before you report a new variable

  • See it vary on at least two nights, ideally with the same period or the same kind of change.
  • Rule out a neighbor: look at the image at full zoom, and try a smaller aperture.
  • Check the comparison stars: a variable comp makes every other star look variable in the opposite sense.
  • Check that the star is not already cataloged under another name (SIMBAD, VSX search by position).
  • Follow the VSX submission guidelines. The candidates CSV gives position, magnitude, scatter, period and color as a starting point.

Previous: 20. Scan a field · Next: 22. Files SHOBS-P writes

22. Files SHOBS-P writes

SHOBS-P never changes your frames. It writes only the files listed here: saved masters next to your calibration frames, the results you save, series files, and a few small files in your user folder.

Where results go

The Input page's Output folder decides where results go:

  • With an Output folder, every Save button writes there without asking. The status bar says what was saved where, for example "Saved AAVSO_HD_219134_TG_05-OCT-2026_SHOBS-P.txt to Results."
  • Without one, each Save opens a dialog with a suggested name, and a message confirms where the file went. The dialog opens in the folder you last saved a result to in this session, with the next free name (and number) filled in. A name typed with a number after its extension (…_SHOBS-P.txt.2) is tidied to …_SHOBS-P_2.txt; if that name is taken, the next free one is used and the status bar says so.
  • Nothing is overwritten. If a file of that name exists, the new one gets _2, _3 and so on. One exception (from 2.3.2): a transit fit's corner plot saved again for the same fit, this session, replaces that fit's own corner plot instead of adding a copy.
  • Matching numbers. The report, light-curve CSV and plot PNG of one set of results share one number, so they sort together (for example …_SHOBS-P_2.txt, …_SHOBS-P_lightcurve_2.csv, …_SHOBS-P_lightcurve_2.png). The files saved beside them (corner plot, settings file, field image, AAVSO chart) keep that number too, and a number counts as taken when any of them exists. A new result starts a new number.
  • The Output folder is not remembered between sessions. Set it again each time you start SHOBS-P. Series files are the exception: they always ask where to go, and save back to their own file after that.

Names

Most result files follow one pattern:

<star>_<filter>_<date>_SHOBS-P…

  • <star>: the Star ID with spaces turned into _ (the planet's name, without spaces, for transit reports).
  • <filter>: the AAVSO filter code, such as TG, V, CV or SI.
  • <date>: the UTC date of the first point, as DD-MON-YYYY (for example 05-OCT-2026). A save covering several UTC dates gives the first and last: 16-SEP-2026_to_06-OCT-2026.

The "Created" line

Every text file SHOBS-P writes (reports, CSVs, fit notes) carries a line such as:

# Created 121153Z OCT 26 by SHOBS-P

The time is a date-time group in UTC: day 12, 11:53 Z, October 2026. Plot PNGs carry Created … · SHOBS-P 2.3.0 in the picture. The line tells you, and anyone you send the file to, when it was made and with which program.

Result files by mode

Variables mode (Output – Variability page)

Button File
Save AAVSO report AAVSO_<star>_<filter>_<date>_SHOBS-P.txt: AAVSO Extended File Format, ready for WebObs. Beside it, AAVSO_<star>_<filter>_<date>_SHOBS-P_settings.txt: what the reported points were measured with, night by night (aperture and sky radii, binning, comps and check measured, frames skipped and left out, calibration, gain, telescope aperture, stacking, the SHOBS-P version), and the report's comps, check, scintillation and line-up choices.
Save light-curve CSV <star>_<filter>_<date>_SHOBS-P_lightcurve.csv: every point, flagged ones included with their flag.
Save plot PNG <star>_<filter>_<date>_SHOBS-P_lightcurve.png: the four plots with the summary above them.
Save field image (and with every Save AAVSO report) <star>_<filter>_<date>_SHOBS-P_field.png: the reference frame as shot, with N and E arrows showing where north and east lie, and the target, comps and check star marked; and, when the AAVSO's chart server answers, …_aavso_chart.png (sometimes .jpg or .svg, as VSP sends it) of the same field.
Export… <star>_<time>_<mag or flux>.csv: the columns you choose; its header records every choice.
Time of max/min… → Save to times CSV <star>_times_of_extremum.csv, next to the series file. Each new time is added; a re-measured one replaces the old row.

When a night is highlighted in the night table, Save AAVSO report and Save light-curve CSV first ask whether to save that night only or the whole series. See 15. Submit to AAVSO.

Button File
Save AAVSO exoplanet report… AAVSO_<planet>_<date>_SHOBS-P.txt (for example AAVSO_KELT-16b_05-OCT-2026_SHOBS-P.txt), and beside it AAVSO_<planet>_<date>_SHOBS-P_settings.txt (the reduction settings) and the field image <planet>_<date>_SHOBS-P_field.png (with …_aavso_chart.png).

Comment lines starting with # under the Created line give the planet, filter, Tc, verdict and O − C, a # Columns: line saying what the columns hold, and # Meridian flip at BJD_TDB … when there was a flip. It gets the same number as the report and plot.
Save ExoFOP package… A folder ExoFOP_<planet>_<YYYYMMDD>_<observer code>_SHOBS-P holding …_lightcurve.csv, …_fit.txt and …_plot.png.
Save for ExoClock… ExoClock_<planet>_<YYYYMMDD>.txt (for example ExoClock_TOI-2046b_20261027.txt; the planet without spaces, the UTC date of the first frame): # header lines, then JD_UTC at mid-exposure, relative flux (not detrended) and error, one line per frame the fit used. Beside it, ExoClock_info.txt: what to pick on ExoClock's upload form. Both get _2, _3 … when the name is taken.
Multi-night… → Save timing CSV… <planet>_<first night>_to_<last night>_SHOBS-P_multinight_timings.csv: one row per epoch (mid-time in BJD_TDB with its error, O − C against each ephemeris), with the joint shape and the ephemerides in # header lines
Multi-night… → Save plot PNG… <planet>_<first night>_to_<last night>_SHOBS-P_multinight.png
Multi-night… → Corner plot… → Save PNG… <planet>_<first night>_to_<last night>_SHOBS-P_multinight_corner.png
