Save CSV… <field>_<filter>_<date>_SHOBS-P_scan.csv: one row per measured star.
Export candidates CSV… field_scan_candidates.csv
Export all light curves CSV… field_scan_lightcurves.csv

The two fixed names get _2, _3 … like any other file, so give them a folder of their own per field if you scan many fields.

Any mode

Button File
Credits and sources → Save credits as text… SHOBS-P_credits.txt

Series files

A series (.cvseries) holds every night of one star in one mode: all points, flags, the comparison and check stars, night notes, the watch list and the settings of each night. It is a text file (JSON).

  • Save series always asks where to save it, suggesting <star>.cvseries.
  • After that, many actions save it again by themselves: adding or removing a night, night notes, Use comps…, planet-transit flags, catalog magnitudes typed in Use comps…, and report bookkeeping (saved reports and the nights marked sent). The status bar says so ("Auto-saved to …").
  • The backup copy. The first time SHOBS-P writes to a series file in a session, it keeps the previous version as <name>_prev.cvseries. Later writes in that session leave the _prev file alone, so it always holds the series as it was before this session. If something goes wrong, close the series and load the _prev file.
  • Star positions on the image are not stored. You mark the target and comparison stars again on each new night.
  • Stacked nights. A night whose points are stacks (the Photometry page's Points: Stacked, chapter 8) stores only the stacked points, and its settings carry a "stack" entry: the stack size N, the number of stacks and frames, how many were partial, the effective exposure and the frames' own exposure, and how far the field turned, how much was clipped and the largest shift. A per-frame night has none.
  • A series records its mode. Loading it switches SHOBS-P to that mode.

See 10. Build a multi-night series.

Saved calibration masters

When SHOBS-P builds a master bias, dark, dark flat or flat, it saves it in a subfolder SHOBS-P masters inside the folder of the raw frames:

  • SHOBS-P_master_bias_bin1.fits
  • SHOBS-P_master_dark_bin1_120s.fits (the dark's exposure is in the name)
  • SHOBS-P_master_darkflat_bin1_0.5s.fits (the dark flats' exposure is in the name)
  • SHOBS-P_master_flat_bin1.fits

The next time you calibrate with the same frames and binning, the master is reused, which saves minutes. It is rebuilt automatically when the frames change, or when you tick Rebuild masters (ignore saved ones). Keeping the masters in a subfolder means other programs that take "every FITS file in the folder" never pick them up. You can delete the subfolder at any time; it is rebuilt when needed. See 7. Calibrate.

Files in your user folder

These live in your user folder (on Windows C:\Users\<you>, on Linux and the Mac your home folder ~). The names start with a dot, so Windows Explorer and the Mac's Finder may hide them; type the name into the address bar, or turn on hidden items (on a Mac, Cmd+Shift+period in Finder).

File What it holds
.shobs_p_settings.json Your remembered settings: mode, site, telescope, aperture, focal length, pixel size, mount, observer code, scintillation choice, linear limit, stretch, Blink speed, window places, Discovery's period range and watch stars, and similar. Not the folders.
.shobs_p.log Every line of the Log window, each with the date and time in front, and the full details of any error. Past 5 MB it is renamed .shobs_p.log.1 (one old file is kept) and a new log starts.
.shobs_p_cache\catalogs\ Star catalogs fetched for your fields, kept for 30 days so solving the same field again is faster.
.shobs_p_cache\stacks\ The stacks of the last Stack run (chapter 8): one 32-bit FITS file per stack, stack_0001.fits and on. Only the newest set is kept, because each stack is a full frame. Your Lights folder is never written to.
.shobs_p_cache\lookups\ The last good answer from each online lookup: simbad.json and vsx.json (Star ID lookups), vsx_cone.json (the variable-star check), vsp.json (AAVSO charts and sequences), nea.json (planet lookups), and more from other services. Used only when the service does not answer; a live answer always replaces it. Only public catalog values are kept, never your site, observer code or data. Clear cached catalogs (Credits page) empties this folder and catalogs.
.shobs\onsky\ One small JSON file per finished reduction, <night>__<target>__<run id>.json, for the SHOBS-FLUX app: the setup, the aperture and each frame's star fluxes, sky, seeing and airmass. Your site is never written to it. Since 2.3.0 it is format version 2: each star in each frame also has e_per_unit (electrons per SHOBS-P image unit, with the gain, binning, superpixel and flat included), and the measurement part has e_per_unit_median, e_per_unit_source, e_per_unit_note, flat_applied and camera_bin_mode. With no gain known, or on pre-calibrated frames, e_per_unit is empty (null) and the note says why. Nothing on screen changes.
.shiloh_photometry_exports.json Named setups from the Export… window.
.shiloh_photometry_colorcal.json The color term saved from a field color calibration, reused on later nights.

Deleting any of these is safe: SHOBS-P starts again with defaults (and re-downloads what it needs). You will have to type your site again if you delete the settings file. A .shobs_p_transit_catalog.csv left from SHOBS-P 2.2.21 or earlier is no longer read or written (it served Tonight's transits, which is gone); you can delete it.

Your site is stored only here, and only after you type it (or say Yes when SHOBS-P offers the one in your FITS headers). It is never shipped with the program. A series file carries the site of its own nights, but loading it never replaces your own site.

The self-test log

Run self-test.bat (Windows), Run self-test.command (Mac) and ./run_selftest.sh (Linux) write selftest_log.txt in the program folder and a copy, SHOBS-P_selftest_log.txt, in your Downloads folder (when you have one).

SHOBS-P's own Python environment is not in the program folder either: it is in %LOCALAPPDATA%\SHOBS-P on Windows, ~/.local/share/SHOBS-P on Linux and ~/Library/Application Support/SHOBS-P on a Mac (chapter 2). See 24. Troubleshooting.

The setup log (Mac and Linux)

On a Mac or Linux, the first start sets up SHOBS-P's own Python environment and downloads the packages it needs. What the installer (pip) said is kept in pip_log.txt in that same SHOBS-P environment folder: ~/Library/Application Support/SHOBS-P on a Mac, ~/.local/share/SHOBS-P on Linux. If setup fails, attach this file to your problem report. To start the setup afresh, delete that SHOBS-P folder; it is set up again on the next start.

Previous: 21. Follow a candidate · Next: 23. Messages explained

23. Messages explained

SHOBS-P talks to you in four places:

  • The status bar at the bottom of the window: the latest news, one line. While SHOBS-P works, the busy bar at its left end says what it is doing (chapter 3); when the job ends, the line says how it went, starting with ✔ (done), ✖ (failed) or ⚠ (done, but look). After a report save it also holds a button for the upload page.
  • The log: on the Calibrate page, and under the image on the Photometry page (Show log / Hide log). Every status line goes here too, often with more detail. When something looks wrong, read the log first.
  • Message boxes: a question (Yes / No, sometimes Cancel) when SHOBS-P needs a decision, a warning when it found a problem, an error when it cannot go on.
  • Notes and cautions on the Output pages: lines starting with ⚠ are cautions about the result.

Most questions have a default that is the safe choice; just read the whole message before you answer. The messages below are the ones new users meet most often, grouped by step. "…" stands for the names and numbers filled in.

Starting on Linux or a Mac

These appear in the Terminal window the first time SHOBS-P starts (chapter 2).

Message What it means What to do
Looking for a Python that can run SHOBS-P... then Trying … and usable: Python 3.13.x or not usable: … SHOBS-P checks each Python it finds, 3.13, 3.12 and 3.11 first. Nothing; it picks the first one that works.
⚠ Some packages SHOBS-P needs have no ready-made copy for Python 3.15.x yet (they would have to be compiled, and SHOBS-P never compiles). Trying the next Python. A brand-new Python has no ready-made packages yet. Nothing, if another Python follows. Otherwise install Python 3.13.
✔ SHOBS-P is set up with Python 3.13.x. The setup worked. Nothing; SHOBS-P opens.
✖ SHOBS-P could not find a Python it can use (each one tried is listed above, with the reason). No Python 3.11 or newer with Tkinter was found. Mac: install Python 3.13 from python.org. Linux: install Python and its Tkinter package (the message names it).
✖ SHOBS-P could not set up its packages with any Python on this computer (each one is listed above). None of the Pythons could install the packages, often because the only one is too new. Do what the next lines say (Mac: install Python 3.13 from python.org; your other Pythons can stay). pip's own words are in pip_log.txt (chapter 24).
✖ The packages could not be downloaded. Check the internet connection and try again. No internet, or the download was blocked. Check the connection, then start again.

Anywhere

Message What it means What to do
Another step is still running. Wait for it to finish. Only one job runs at a time. Wait, or press Cancel / Cancel run.
Wait for the current run to finish (or cancel it) before switching modes. Same, for the mode buttons. Wait or cancel.
Something went wrong: … Details were written to ….shobs_p.log. An unexpected error. Note what you were doing and see 24. Troubleshooting.
Could not open a browser: go to … The upload or ExoFOP page could not open. Copy the address into your browser.
The output folder could not be used (…); choose where to save. The Output folder is missing or read-only. Pick another folder on the Input page.

Input

Message What it means What to do
✖ Could not look up … (…). Type RA/Dec, or let the plate solution fill them, or press Look up again. SIMBAD and VSX did not find the name, or there is no internet. Check the spelling (the Input page may suggest the AAVSO spelling: Use it), or type RA/Dec.
The … folder holds N file(s) SHOBS-P cannot read … It reads FITS and XISF Camera RAW, TIFF or JPEG files. Save the frames as FITS or XISF in your capture program.
This Lights folder holds frames pointing at more than one place: … Use only the N frames near the Input target …? Two fields in one folder. Yes sets the other field aside (Blink can bring frames back).
These frames are DY Peg, not BL Cam. … Start a new reduction? … The lights' pointing (or their OBJECT keyword) says they are another star than the Input page's. Yes starts a new reduction: the open series is closed (save offered), the old star's target, planet, comps, check and watch stars are cleared, and the frames' star is looked up. No keeps yours. See 5. Input.
Start a new reduction? … target, planet, comps, check and watch stars, Use comps choice, VSX period and rejected frames are cleared … You pressed New reduction. Yes to start over for another star; folders, site and optics stay. The Output page notes go back to their default (the log lists the Output page notes among what was cleared when you had typed your own).
no such folder (count beside a Frames row) The path typed in the box is not an existing folder (yet). Finish typing the path, or use Browse. The count updates a moment after you stop typing, or when you press Enter.
SIMBAD from cache (fetched 12 Oct 2026); the live service did not answer (503). (end of the status line, and in the log; also VSX from cache …, NASA Exoplanet Archive from cache … under the planet boxes) The service did not answer, so the last good answer for this name was used. Nothing; a live answer replaces it next time. See "When a service is down" in 24. Troubleshooting.
✖ …: SIMBAD and VSX did not answer (…) — type RA/Dec, or Look up again. The lookup services did not answer (no connection, or the service is down). Type RA/Dec, or press Look up again later. The planet lookup says the same about the NASA Exoplanet Archive.
✖ TOI-2046: SIMBAD and VSX did not answer within 60 s — type RA/Dec, or Look up again. (under RA/Dec and on the status bar) The lookup has waited 60 seconds with no answer; the busy bar stops. Type RA/Dec, or press Look up again later. If the answer still comes in, it is used. For a planet the line is under the planet boxes and says … the NASA Exoplanet Archive did not answer within 60 s — type the values or Look up again.
⚠ Darks 60 s, lights 120 s: the master dark is scaled ×2 … (under the Frames box) The darks' exposure differs from the lights'. ✖ in red when it would be scaled more than 3 times. Point Dark at darks of the lights' exposure if you have them.
⚠ No bias and no dark flats: darks can't be scaled correctly without bias or dark flats (110 s darks to 55 s lights scales the camera's offset too). … (under the Frames box) The darks must be scaled, and nothing tells SHOBS-P the camera's offset. Add bias frames or dark flats (Dark flats row), or use darks at the lights' exposure. See 7. Calibrate.
⚠ Dark flats 2 s, flats 0.5 s: dark flats should match the flats' exposure. Take dark flats at 0.5 s. The dark flats do not match the flats. Take dark flats at the flats' exposure. They are still used meanwhile.
Dark flats folder cleared. Calibration will skip it. (status bar) You pressed ✕ on the Dark flats row. Nothing.
✖ No planets found for … at the NASA Exoplanet Archive (none listed under that name, or the Archive did not answer). Type the planet's name, or press Look up again on the Star ID. (Transits, under the Planet box) A Star ID lookup found no planet of that star. Type the planet's name in Planet, or look up again later. See 16. Prepare for a transit night.
✖ …: the NASA Exoplanet Archive did not answer within 60 s — type the planet's name, or Look up again on the Star ID. The star's planet list did not come within 60 seconds. Type the planet's name, or press Look up again later.
These lights come from …. Last time you measured frames from that telescope the Telescope aperture was … mm; the box says … mm. Use … mm for these frames? The header gives no aperture, and you measured this telescope before with another one. Yes, unless you know the box is right.
These lights come from …, another telescope than the last frames you measured (…). The Telescope aperture box still says … mm from those frames. Is … mm right for this telescope? New telescope, no aperture in the header. Yes keeps the value; No puts the cursor in the Telescope aperture (mm) box: type the aperture.
These lights were taken at latitude …, longitude …, N km from your site. The frames come from another observatory (shared or downloaded data). Yes uses their site and optics for these frames only; your own come back with your own frames.
… is not a master SHOBS-P built. A master from other software was chosen. Choose the folder of raw frames instead; SHOBS-P builds and reuses its own master.
Color images: These N lights are color images that other software has already debayered (three channels: red, green and blue). … Which channel should be measured? The lights are finished color images, not raw frames. Usually Green (TG). They are measured without a debayer or calibration; change the channel later in the Filter box (Calibrate page).
Color or monochrome camera? No Bayer keyword, and neither the flats nor the lights' sky can tell. Answer Color camera or Monochrome camera. For a color camera, check the pattern on the Calibrate page.
Log: Mount: Alt-az from the headers (…); the Mount box says German equatorial … The headers clearly show another mount than the Mount box. If these are your own lights, set Mount in the Optics box.

Calibrate

Message What it means What to do
2 dark frames (60 s) are in the Lights folder, and the Dark box is empty. Use them as darks? Calibration frames were saved with the lights. Yes uses them for this run (the files stay where they are). See 7. Calibrate.
Dark folder '…': The darks are 30 s and the lights 120 s, so the master dark is scaled x4 … The darks are far from the lights' exposure. Yes calibrates without the dark (fine for most cooled cameras); Cancel lets you pick better darks.
The flats were taken at rotator angle X° and the lights at Y°. The camera was turned between flats and lights; the flat corrects the wrong pattern. Use flats taken at the lights' angle if you have them. On a German equatorial mount a meridian flip (180°) does not cause this. On an alt-az mount without a derotator (the message adds alt-az mount, so no meridian flip explains it) it is a real turn.
Log: Caution: flats taken with the derotator at sky angle … An alt-az mount with a derotator. Nothing: the message explains that this is fine.
Log: These lights are color images (already debayered and calibrated): the bias, dark and flat entries are not used for them. Information. Nothing.
The Flat folder mixes camera angles: … Flats from more than one camera angle. Keep only the flats taken at the lights' angle.
These lights look already calibrated: … Skip bias, dark, and flat for this run? (Recommended) The files show signs of earlier calibration. Yes, or they are calibrated twice.
Calibration failed: The masters do not fit these lights: … Size or binning differs between masters and lights. Pick matching frames, or tick Rebuild masters (ignore saved ones).
Calibration failed: … is not a master SHOBS-P built … (or … is a master dark, not a master flat.) A Bias, Dark or Flat row points at a file that is not the right SHOBS-P master. The same line is in the log. Pick the right master with Master file…, or the folder of raw frames.
No light frames found. The Lights folder is empty or every frame is rejected. Check the folder and the Blink page.
Monochrome camera found (no color mask in the flat): Bayer pattern MONO. (or … in the lights' sky background …) Information (status bar and log). If your camera is in fact a color camera, choose its pattern on the Calibrate page.
Log: Meridian flip before frame … (…° to …°) Information: where the flip happened. Nothing; one set of flats covers both sides.
Log: Calibration: lights = …; flats = …. How the lights and flats are calibrated, from the rows you filled in. Nothing, unless a ⚠ follows it.
Log: ⚠ Darks can't be scaled correctly without bias or dark flats. … No bias and no dark flats, and the darks are scaled to the lights. Add bias frames or dark flats, or use darks at the lights' exposure.
Log: ⚠ The flats (0.5 s) get the master dark (55 s) scaled ×0.00909, and with no bias that scales the camera's offset too … Put dark flats … in the Dark flats box, or add bias frames. The flats would lose the wrong amount of offset, so the master flat would be wrong. Put the dark flats in the Dark flats row (or add bias frames) and calibrate again.
Log: The Bias box holds dark flats (their IMAGETYP says so). They are used as the bias, as before 2.3.0; the Dark flats box is now the place for them … Information: the old way still works. Next time, put them in the Dark flats row.
Log: N dark flats found in the Flat folder (IMAGETYP or name): used as the dark flats, and left out of the master flat. Dark flats saved beside the flats. Nothing.
Log: Photon noise: … The camera binned 3x3 and averaged the binned pixels (the sky's noise says so), so each stored ADU holds 9 x EGAIN electrons … (or … summed the binned pixels …, or … cannot tell whether it summed … or averaged …) How the error bars count electrons on camera-binned frames. Nothing. When SHOBS-P cannot tell and the Transit fit's χ²ν is well under 1, the camera averaged. See 7. Calibrate.

Photometry

Message What it means What to do
Calibrate first (Calibrate page). Star positions are measured on the calibrated, debayered image. You clicked the image before calibrating. Calibrate.
Step strip 1 Not solved, or Not solved: no lock (h of n catalog stars matched). The plate solve failed. Press Solve again for the full diagnosis. Check the Optics box (focal length, pixel size, binning) and the RA/Dec. You can still click stars and type magnitudes.
Solve needs to know roughly where the frame is: … No pointing in the headers and no RA/Dec typed. Type the target (or field center) on the Input page.
… is a known or suspected variable: … Use it anyway? A comp or check you clicked is in VSX or SIMBAD. Usually No, and pick another star.
The aperture (r = 12 px) is much larger than the stars on this frame (FWHM 3.2 px). The aperture does not suit the seeing. No, then Suggest… to set sizes from the stars.
Defocused stars: the aperture (r = … px) clips the ring — use r ≥ … px. … Run with the current aperture anyway? The stars are donuts and the aperture is too small for them. No, then Suggest… (it sizes the aperture to 95 % of the light).
There is no star under the Target marker at (x, y). The marker is on blank sky. No, and click the star again.
These have a name in the Stars panel but no marker on this image … A name left from an earlier night or series. Yes clears the names; Cancel lets you mark them.
With 3 comps the AAVSO report is an ensemble, and AAVSO requires a check star … No check star in Variables mode. Mark a check star (or continue if you will not report this run).
No transit of … is predicted during these lights … Transits mode, but the frames miss the transit. Check the planet and date; use Variables mode for a variability run.
… hosts …, and a transit of … is predicted during these lights … Variables mode on a planet host during a transit. Yes switches to Transits mode.
Log: ⚠ Check marker at (x, y) is N px from the center of the star there … Before the run: that marker would be measured beside its star, so the star would read faint. Clear the marker, zoom in and click the star again, unless you meant it.
Binning is 2 but the masters were built at 1. Binning changed after calibration. Calibrate again, or set binning back.
Log: skipped …: could not align this frame (N stars found; clouds? trailing?) One frame was unusable. Nothing, unless many are skipped; then check those frames in Blink.
No frames produced a magnitude. The Calibrate page log says why each frame was skipped. Every frame failed. Read the log.
Log: AAVSO sequence did not answer (503 from apps.aavso.org); asking again in the background … The AAVSO chart server is slow or down. The solve goes on without it. Wait: the log says if it answers, and then Solve again uses it.
Log: AAVSO sequence blocked: 403 (Cloudflare check page) from apps.aavso.org: AAVSO's site refused the request … AAVSO's site turned the request away. Solve again later.
Log and status line: AAVSO sequence from cache (fetched 12 Oct 2026); the live service did not answer (503). Chart X28123AB (goes in the AAVSO report). AAVSO's chart server did not answer, so the last sequence for this field was used, with its chart ID. Nothing; the report is as usual.
Variable-star check (SIMBAD; VSX not reached): … (Field box and log) VSX could not be reached (VizieR and AAVSO's VSX both down, nothing saved for this field). Only SIMBAD was checked. Solve again later to check against VSX.
Log: Variable-star check: VSX from AAVSO's VSX API (VizieR did not answer). or VSX from cache (fetched …) … VizieR's copy of VSX did not answer; AAVSO's own VSX, or the last saved list for this field, was used. Nothing.
⚠ raw peak … ADU is over the linear limit (…): the camera's response bends there, so this star reads too faint … (Stars panel) The star is too bright for your camera's linear range (linear ADU). Pick a fainter comp; for the target, shorter exposures or more defocus.
⚠ only … px from the frame edge: its sky ring … needs the star at least … px in … (Stars panel) The comp or check star is too near the edge. Pick a star further in (for the check, the line may name a green ring S1, S2 … farther in).
Log (under Catalogs: …): II/336/apass9 from vizier.cfa.harvard.edu looked wrong (40 of 40 stars brighter than V 4 …); not used, asking the next mirror. A VizieR mirror sent a damaged copy of APASS (from 2.3.3 such an answer is turned away: two stars brighter than V 4, more than 10 brighter than V 6, most stars brighter than V 6, or mostly 99.999 / 999999 placeholders). Nothing; the next mirror is asked. If every copy that answered was damaged, APASS's stars are missing this time: Solve again later.
Log: APASS DR9 saved copy for this field looked wrong (…); not used, asking again. A copy saved before 2.3.3 from a damaged APASS answer. Nothing; a fresh answer replaces it. If you ran SHOBS-P on 10 Nov 2026 with CDS down, redo the comp step of that night.
Log: Rejected frames (N): 12, 15, #1 (name.fits), #1 (other.fits) … Two different frames share a label (frames whose names hold no number are labeled by position); from 2.3.3 each such entry carries its file name. Nothing.
Star catalogs did not answer, so the frame could not be solved …: Gaia DR3 (…) … did not answer: a catalog service outage … Gaia, APASS or Tycho-2 did not answer (or Gaia sent no stars). Not your settings. Solve again in a few minutes.
Log: Target: … is on frame … (found by …), not on frames …. Frame … is the reference now … The first frames missed the target; SHOBS-P moved the reference to the first frame that holds it. Nothing; the frames without the target are skipped and listed.
Log: … not on the frame in N of M images (frames …); those frames were skipped. Frames on which the target was off the frame. With ⚠ (a large share): check the pointing, or reject those frames in Blink.
Log: ⚠ After the meridian flip the … apertures were off their stars by about (…) px together, while the target's was not … An aperture-placement error at the flip, not the stars. See 12. Flags and bad data. Look at the positions in the light-curve CSV.
Log: ⚠ Linear limit … ADU: raw peak at or above it in … frames … Some frames of these stars were over your camera's linear limit. Check Comp health…'s Over linear limit column; prefer stars that stay under it.
C3 left out of 14 frames (off the frame edge; frames 27–40); those frames use the other comps. (orange, under "Measured with", and in a box after the run) A comp drifted off the frame edge in those frames. The frames are kept and measured with the other comps. Nothing; pick a comp further in next time. See "Comps near the frame edge" in 8. Photometry page basics.
… out of the whole night (missing in most frames) The comp was measured in fewer than half of the night's frames (or fewer than 5). Nothing; the night uses the other comps.
check star left out of 18 frames (off the frame edge; …); no check value there. The check star drifted off the edge. Nothing; those frames have no check value.
check … comes within … px of the frame edge … The check star was off the frame in N frames (…). A better check star stays farther in: … Pick it as the check next time. (in the same box after the run) The check drifted off the edge; SHOBS-P names the spare nearest its brightness that stayed in all night. Pick that star as the check next time.
Log: …: spare … comes within … px of the frame edge … (a spare: only measured, not used; nothing to do). A spare sat near the edge. Nothing.
⚠ 16 frames skipped: every comp lost (off the frame edge) (frames 25–40). No comp was left in those frames. Choose comps further from the edge, or keep the field better centered.
⚠ N frames were skipped because of …, which is no longer a comp — Run photometry again to measure them. Every comp was lost in those frames, and Use comps… has since dropped those comps; Use comps does not measure frames. Press Measure skipped frames at the right of the line.
The N skipped frames of … are not in the Lights folder on the Input page now (or no image is loaded), so they cannot be measured on their own. … Measure skipped frames needs that night's frames. Open that night's frames, or press Run photometry to measure the whole night again.
⚠ The field turned by 180° between frames … — that looks like a meridian flip. Is the Mount set right (German equatorial)? … Mount is Alt-az, but the field turned half a turn between two frames. If it was a flip, set Mount to German equatorial (Input page, Optics box) and run photometry again.
⚠ Comps are weak (comp noise 4.7× the target's per frame). Stack 20 frames …? (Stack row) The comps' noise, not the target's, limits each point. Press Stack, or choose brighter comps. See "Stacking when comps are weak" in 8. Photometry page basics.
⚠ Comps are weak (…), but the time resolution allows no stacking (…). Brighter comps, or longer exposures, would help. A stack would hide the signal. Use brighter comps or longer exposures next time.
Comps are fine per frame (…): stacking is not needed. Type N to stack anyway. Information. Nothing.
Stacks of N frames last about …, longer than the time resolution allows (…): they may blur the signal. Stack anyway? The N you typed is longer than the time cap. No, and use a smaller N, unless you know the signal is slow.
That makes only K stacked points; the transit fit needs 20 or more. Stack anyway? Transits: too few stacks for the fit. No, and use a smaller N.
That makes only K stacks; a field scan needs at least 10. Use a smaller N. Discovery: too few stacks for a scan. Use a smaller N.
Run photometry first (Scan field in Discovery): the Stack step uses that run's frames, alignment and noise. Stack before any run. Run photometry first.
Log: ⚠ Aperture check on the stacks: … The stacked stars are more than 15 % wider than in one frame. Run Suggest… with Points: Stacked, set the new aperture and press Stack again (the stacks are reused).

Output, Variables mode

Message What it means What to do
Add these N points as the night of … to the M points already in the series? A series is open. Yes adds the night. Read any Careful: lines first (other comps, filter or equipment).
… frames of … lost light …, but every measured star … faded by the same amount …: transmission loss (dew/haze?), not cloud. Use these points in the light curve …, or leave them out? Dew or haze, not cloud. Leave them out (the default) leaves them flagged; Use these points keeps them. See 12. Flags and bad data.
…: N frames fall in planet b's transit … Leave those frames out …? A known planet transits the variable during your frames. Yes (the default) follows the campaign rule.
Before you upload: • AAVSO observer code is empty … The report is missing something AAVSO needs. Fix the listed items and save again.
⚠ No AUID: WebObs will reject this report. Request one in VSX (open the star's VSX page, Request AUID). (orange, under the Results row; also on the status line after a save, and in the log) VSX knows the star but has not given it an AUID yet, and WebObs files reports by AUID. Open the star's page in VSX and press Request AUID. When it has one, look the star up again on the Input page and save the report again. See 15. Submit to AAVSO.
⚠ The notes name …, which is not in this reduction. Notes: "…" Save anyway? The Notes box names a star that is not the target, planet, comps or check: usually a leftover from the last star. Catalog names (HD, HIP, TYC, TIC, AUIDs, WASP-…) and GCVS names such as SZ Lyn, V1441 Cas or XX Cyg are recognized; the target's own names are not flagged. Also asked before an AAVSO exoplanet report or ExoFOP package. No (the default) and fix the Notes box. Yes only if you meant to name that star.
Saved … — upload to AAVSO WebObs with Open AAVSO WebObs… on the Results row. Series file updated: ….cvseries. (status line) The report was saved, and the open series file was rewritten to note it (for the Sent column). Nothing. See 10. Build a multi-night series.
This series was observed from …; the Input page has …. Keep the series' site? A report save is about to rewrite the series file, and the Input page's site or observer code differ from the series' own. Yes keeps the series' site and observer code in the file (usually right). No writes the Input page's into the series.
Reduce a night of … first; the field image comes from a reduction (the Photometry page's frame). Save field image (or a report save) after Load series…: the frame on the Photometry page is another star's, or there is none. Calibrate a night of the series' star, then save the field image again. With a report, the line is in the log and the report itself is saved.
Log: AAVSO chart not saved (…): the field image is saved alone. Save field image again later to fetch the chart. The AAVSO chart server did not answer (time-out, no internet). After a report save this goes to the log only; the status line keeps the saved line and the upload button. Press Save field image later.
✖ Not saved: fix the Notes (Output page), then save again. You answered No to the notes question. Correct the Notes box on the Output page and save again.
Night … was already sent on … (in …); AAVSO will hold duplicates. Open anyway? Asked by Open AAVSO WebObs… (and the exoplanet and ExoFOP upload buttons): the report you saved last holds a night already sent. Upload only the nights not sent yet. If that night was never really sent, double-click it in the night table to unmark it. See 15. Submit to AAVSO.
⚠ No reliable period … / No significant period … The best peak does not pass the significance tests. See 13. Look for a period.
Too short for a period search: one night of … h covers under two cycles of even the shortest period searched … One night is too short to test any period. Add nights, or observe longer.
This night's meridian-flip step is … mag ⚠ … Save … with this night now? The flip step looks like an aperture problem, not the star. No keeps the file on disk unchanged; Save series keeps the night later. See 9. Reduce a variable-star night.
Log: Not saved: … has a flip step ⚠ — Save series to keep it. An automatic save skipped the night you held back. Press Save series when you want it in the file.
Log: ✖ Use comps choice (…) not applied to …: … not measured on any of its frames (another field's comps?) … The series' Use comps… stars are not in this night's field. For another star, use New reduction on the Input page.
Range ⚠ … outside the search (Period box) The VSX or marked period lies outside the period search range. Press Search … d instead.

Output, Transits mode

Message What it means What to do
Enter your site's latitude and longitude on the Input page first … Needed for BJD times. Type your site once.
…: N usable points. Run photometry in Transits mode first (or open an exoplanet report). Fewer than 20 points. Run photometry, or check the flags.
The photometry, the comps, or the planet changed since the last fit, so that fit was cleared. The fit no longer matches the data. Press Fit transit.
The comps are still being checked (a few seconds). Save the … when that is done … The leave-one-comp-out check is running. Wait a moment and save again.
⚠ Inconclusive …, ✗ No transit measured … See 18. When the fit says "inconclusive" or "none".
The per-point error bars are about …x larger than the real scatter … Usually the camera gain (EGAIN) in the headers. Check the photometry log's Photon noise line.
This fit did not measure a transit: … Save it anyway …? Saving a "none" result. Save for your records only.
… is limited by its prior range: its MCMC samples pile up against …, so the error bar is not a measurement. The data did not constrain that parameter. Open it with Save corner plot…; don't quote that error bar as a measurement. See 17. Fit a transit and submit it.
No detection with Both: … alone detects it (ΔBIC …). … Airmass and time slope together absorbed the transit. Untick one trend only if it is not real. See 18. When the fit says "inconclusive" or "none".
With the shape left free the fit is not constrained (…) … The free-shape cross-check ran away. Nothing; the result uses the published shape.
Gain 1 e-/ADU assumed (no EGAIN in the headers, no usable sky estimate) … (orange) The error bars' photon noise used a guessed gain. Add EGAIN to the headers if you know the camera's gain.
; Flip step fitted: −14.4 ± 0.5 ppt (ΔBIC 629), kept (result line) The night has a meridian flip, and a step there improved the fit clearly; it is fitted with the transit. Nothing. See "A meridian flip in the night" in 17. Fit a transit and submit it.
; Flip step not needed: +0.2 ± 0.6 ppt (ΔBIC −6; kept only at 10 or more) (result line) The step test did not improve the fit enough; the fit has no step term. Nothing.
Log: meridian flip: step … ppt, ΔBIC … (kept at 10 or more): kept, fitted with the transit (or … not needed, fitted without a step) The same decision, while fitting. Nothing.
Table: Flip step (ppt) … (ΔBIC …, kept), or Flip step not needed (ΔBIC …; test step … ppt) The flip step's value, or why there is none. With a kept step, also read Depth without step (%) and Step–depth correlation.
Fit a transit first: the corner plot shows that fit's MCMC samples. Save corner plot… with no fit. Press Fit transit first.
✔ Fit of the stacked points (kept from before). (or per-frame) You switched Points to a set fitted before; its fit is shown again. Nothing.
A joint fit needs at least two usable nights of the same planet (see the Status column). Multi-night…: fewer than two nights are ready. Read each night's Status and add nights.

Discovery mode

Message What it means What to do
Field not solved: these lights do not say where they point. No pointing in the headers. Type the field center on the Input page and Solve again.
Fewer than 8 stars found in the frame; a field scan needs more. Too few stars detected. Check focus and exposure, or lower Detection threshold (σ).
Field scan failed: only N frames could be measured; a scan needs at least 10 Too many frames could not be aligned. Blink out bad frames; check that the frames are of one field.
Log: Field scan: the 800-star limit was reached … Only the brightest stars were measured. Raise Most stars to measure.
VSX and SIMBAD could not be reached during the scan. Scan again when online. No check against known variables. Scan again when online.
VSX not reached (the table's VSX column), and … already known (SIMBAD; VSX not reached) in the log SIMBAD answered but VSX did not, so a star there may still be a known VSX variable. Solve again and scan again later.
Check first: clipped on N frames: check linearity The star's raw core reached saturation on a frame or two. Look at its light curve at those frames.
Check first: blended neighbor: check A neighbor's light reaches the aperture (0.2 % or more). Look at the image; a neighbor can fake variability.
⚠ VSX period outside the search (…): … (under the table) A known variable's period is outside the scan's period range. Press Search … d instead.

Previous: 22. Files SHOBS-P writes · Next: 24. Troubleshooting

24. Troubleshooting

This chapter covers:

  • the self-test, which checks that SHOBS-P and its Python packages work on your computer,
  • the log file, where error details go (and pip_log.txt, for setup problems on Linux and the Mac),
  • typical problems and their fixes,
  • how to report a problem.

The self-test

The self-test runs SHOBS-P's own checks with your Python and your copies of Astropy, photutils and Tkinter. It changes nothing: no settings, no data.

To run it:

  1. Open the SHOBS-P program folder.
  2. Double-click Run self-test.bat (Windows) or Run self-test.command (Mac), or type ./run_selftest.sh in a terminal in that folder (Linux). A console window opens.
  3. Wait a minute or two. The transit fits are the slow part.
  4. Read the last line. It should be RESULT: ALL PASSED.

The full log is written to selftest_log.txt in the program folder, with a copy, SHOBS-P_selftest_log.txt, in your Downloads folder (on Linux and the Mac, when you have one). On Linux without a screen, the window test runs on a hidden screen when xvfb-run is installed.

What the self-test does, in sections:

  1. imports the program files and prints the versions of SHOBS-P, NumPy, Astropy and photutils;
  2. checks many helper calculations (masters, star sizes, times, names, flags);
  3. runs a real period search;
  4. runs a transit regression on sample reports, when they are present (in a selftest_reports folder next to selftest.py; see chapter 2);
  5. opens the main window and closes it again, with nothing clicked.

Lines marked ok passed; FAIL did not. Some lines say a step was skipped: for example section 4 when there are no sample reports ((no sample transit reports to run on: skipped)), or the planet-name checks when the NASA Exoplanet Archive cannot be reached. A skipped step is not a failure.

If the result is N FAILED, send selftest_log.txt with your report (see below).

The log file

SHOBS-P started from its desktop shortcut has no console window. When something goes wrong inside the program, it shows "Something went wrong: … Details were written to ….shobs_p.log." and appends the full error to .shobs_p.log in your user folder (C:\Users\<you>; your home folder ~ on Linux and the Mac). Messages that would have gone to a console go there too.

The file also holds every line of the program's own log (below), each with the date and time in front, so you can look back at an earlier session. When it grows past 5 MB, it is renamed .shobs_p.log.1 and a new one starts; only that one older file is kept.

The file name starts with a dot, so Windows Explorer may hide it. Type %USERPROFILE%\.shobs_p.log into the Explorer address bar to open it.

The program's own log (the dark box on the Calibrate page, and under the image on the Photometry page) is different: it records everything SHOBS-P did in this session, step by step. Read it whenever a result looks odd. Its lines are also written to .shobs_p.log, as above.

pip_log.txt (Linux and Mac setup)

On Linux and the Mac, the first start downloads SHOBS-P's Python packages with pip. The Terminal shows only a short line per package; pip's own detailed messages go to pip_log.txt, next to SHOBS-P's environment:

  • Mac: ~/Library/Application Support/SHOBS-P/pip_log.txt
  • Linux: ~/.local/share/SHOBS-P/pip_log.txt

Each setup attempt starts with a line === <date> <the Python used>. When the setup fails, the Terminal names this file. Attach it to your problem report. (On a Mac, the Library folder is hidden in Finder: in Finder choose Go → Go to Folder… and paste ~/Library/Application Support/SHOBS-P.)

Starting with the console

run_windows.bat in the program folder starts SHOBS-P with a console window. It checks the Python packages first, and if SHOBS-P cannot start, the console shows why and stays open ("The app did not start. Leave this window open and copy the message above."). Use it when the desktop shortcut does nothing.

On Linux and the Mac, SHOBS-P always starts from a terminal (./run_linux.sh, or the Terminal window that Start SHOBS-P.command opens), so any error is shown there: "SHOBS-P did not start, or stopped with an error. Copy the message above."

Typical problems

Starting

Problem Fix
Double-clicking the shortcut does nothing. Start run_windows.bat and read the console. If the Python SHOBS-P used was removed or upgraded, run Create shortcuts.bat again: it finds a working Python and rebuilds SHOBS-P's own environment.
"SHOBS-P could not find a Python it can use." Each Python it tried is listed above that line with the reason. Install Python 3.11 or newer from python.org (tick Add python.exe to PATH), then try again.
The shortcut stopped working after you moved the folder. Run Create shortcuts.bat again from the new place.
matplotlib is not installed. where the image should be. The packages did not install. Run run_windows.bat, which installs them, and read any error it shows.
Linux: "These Pythons are missing Tkinter, the window toolkit" Install the package it names (Debian, Ubuntu, Mint: sudo apt install python3-tk; Fedora: sudo dnf install python3-tkinter; Arch: sudo pacman -S tk), then run ./run_linux.sh again.
Linux: "SHOBS-P could not make its own environment …" and a line about the venv package Debian, Ubuntu, Mint: sudo apt install python3-venv, then try again.
Linux: "This is a Wayland session without XWayland (DISPLAY is not set) …" or "No screen found (DISPLAY is not set) …" Tk needs X to draw its windows. Install XWayland (package xwayland) or log in with an "on Xorg" session; or start SHOBS-P from your desktop session, not over a plain SSH login.
Mac: macOS refuses to open Start SHOBS-P.command (a file from an unidentified developer, or one Apple could not check). Right-click (or Control-click) it, choose Open, then Open again. Once is enough. On macOS 15 and newer, if there is no Open, press Open Anyway in System Settings → Privacy & Security. Or type xattr -d com.apple.quarantine *.command *.sh in Terminal in the SHOBS-P folder; that clears every start file at once (also Run self-test.command).
Mac: "✖ SHOBS-P could not find a Python it can use …" Install Python 3.13 from python.org/downloads/macos (it includes Tk) and double-click again. A Homebrew Python needs its Tk package; the message names it (for example brew install python-tk@3.13).
Linux or Mac: "⚠ Some packages SHOBS-P needs have no ready-made copy for Python 3.15.x yet …" Not an error: that Python is too new for some packages. SHOBS-P tries the next Python by itself. If it was the only one, install Python 3.13 (Mac: from python.org; Linux: 3.13, 3.12 or 3.11 with Tkinter) and start again. You do not need to remove the newer Python.
Linux or Mac: "✖ SHOBS-P could not set up its packages with any Python on this computer …" Read the lines above it: each Python tried is listed with the reason. Usually installing Python 3.13 fixes it. If not, report the problem with pip_log.txt attached (see above).
Linux or Mac: "✖ The packages could not be downloaded. Check the internet connection and try again." Check the internet connection (and any proxy or firewall), then start again.
Mac: a window asks to install Apple's "command line developer tools". SHOBS-P 2.2.20 and newer never compile anything, so this should not happen. If you see it while SHOBS-P sets up, choose Cancel, install Python 3.13 from python.org, delete the SHOBS-P folder in ~/Library/Application Support and start again. Report it with pip_log.txt.
Mac: SHOBS-P closed when you closed the Terminal window. Keep the Terminal window open while SHOBS-P runs.
"SHOBS-P is being set up in another window …" (Linux, Mac) A second start while the first setup was still running. Wait for it to finish, then start again.
The install seems broken after an update or a Python upgrade. Start afresh: delete SHOBS-P's own environment folder and start again (chapter 2, "Starting afresh"). Your settings are kept.
The window runs off the screen, or text is cut. Maximize the window. On a scaled display (125–150%) the lower rows of the Output pages need the full height. In a narrow window (for example half the screen), the Input page's boxes and the Photometry page's View box wrap onto more rows instead of hiding.

Input and calibration

Problem Fix
The folders are empty every time you start. That is by design: folders are not remembered. Your site, optics and observer code are.
A folder shows 0 files. SHOBS-P reads FITS (.fits, .fit, .fts, also compressed) and XISF. Save camera RAW, TIFF or JPEG frames as FITS.
A monochrome camera's frames look like a checkerboard. The Bayer pattern is wrong. On the Calibrate page choose MONO (SHOBS-P sets it by itself from raw flats, or from the lights' sky background when there are bias or dark frames or the sky is bright; otherwise it asks Color or monochrome camera? at Scan folders).
Calibration takes a long time every night. It should reuse saved masters. If your flats or darks change every night, that is expected.
Results look worse after replacing calibration frames. Tick Rebuild masters (ignore saved ones) once.
You have no bias frames, only dark flats. Put the dark flats in the Dark flats row and leave Bias empty: they stand in for the bias (chapter 7). If they sit in the Flat folder, SHOBS-P finds them there by their IMAGETYP or file name.
The Input page says "⚠ No bias and no dark flats: darks can't be scaled correctly …". Add bias frames or dark flats, or use darks at the lights' exposure. Without either, a scaled dark takes off the wrong amount of the camera's offset.

Solving and stars

Problem Fix
1 Not solved. Press Solve again to see the diagnosis. Check Focal length (mm), Pixel size (µm) and Binning on the Input page; a wrong plate scale is the usual cause. Check that RA/Dec are near the field.
Frames from another telescope do not solve. Set that telescope's focal length and pixel size in the Optics box.
The check star (or a comp) reads much fainter than its catalog magnitude, or scatters a lot. The marker is probably a few pixels off the star's center, so every frame is measured beside the star. Before each run SHOBS-P warns in the log ("⚠ Check marker at … is … px from the center of the star there …"). Clear the marker, zoom in, and click the star again.
A star's name and magnitude do not fill in when clicked. The image is not solved yet, or the star is not in the catalogs. Type the magnitude yourself.
The image is too small to click stars. Wheel to zoom, drag to pan, or Pop out the image into its own window. At 125–150 % display scaling a small frame (MicroObservatory) can stay small even after the log shrinks: press Pop out in the View box, or Hide log for more room.
The AAVSO sequence is missing ("AAVSO sequence did not answer (…)" in the log). The AAVSO chart server (VSP, apps.aavso.org) was slow or down. SHOBS-P asks again in the background; if the log later says it answered, press Solve again. Otherwise Solve again later.
The target marker is not placed, and the log says the target is not on this frame. The first frames may miss the target; SHOBS-P looks at the next frames for it (chapter 8). If it cannot find it, reject the frames without the target in Blink and calibrate again.
A lookup ends with "✖ …: SIMBAD and VSX did not answer within 60 s …". The service is slow or down, or the internet is. Type RA/Dec yourself, or press Look up again later; a late answer is still used.
The solve or lookups fail at night. The catalogs and lookups need the internet. Calibration and photometry do not. Catalog stars fetched for a field are kept for 30 days, which often lets the same field solve again without the internet.
The log or status line says "… from cache (fetched 12 Oct 2026); the live service did not answer (503)." A service was down, so SHOBS-P used its last good answer (see "When a service is down" below). Nothing to do; check the values look right, or try again later for a live answer.
The variable-star check says "VSX not reached". VizieR (CDS, then CfA) and AAVSO's own VSX could not be reached, and no earlier answer for this field was saved. Only SIMBAD was checked, so a variable that is only in VSX is not marked. Solve again later.

When a service is down

SHOBS-P asks several online services: SIMBAD and VSX (Star ID lookup and the variable-star check), VizieR (catalogs), AAVSO's chart server (the sequence and chart ID), and the NASA Exoplanet Archive (planets). Every time one answers, SHOBS-P keeps that answer in your user folder (.shobs_p_cache\lookups, chapter 22). When a service does not answer later, SHOBS-P uses the kept answer for the same star, field or planet and says so in the log and on the page, for example "AAVSO sequence from cache (fetched 12 Oct 2026); the live service did not answer (503)." A live answer always wins and replaces the kept one.

  • Star ID lookup: RA/Dec, VSX period and AUID come from the kept answer; the status line ends with the note.
  • AAVSO sequence: the kept sequence and its chart ID are used, and the chart ID still goes in the AAVSO report. The note is in the log and the status line only; the report itself is not changed (WebObs format).
  • Variable-star check: VizieR is asked first (CDS, then CfA; the Cape Town mirror does not carry VSX), then AAVSO's own VSX, then the kept list. A server that just failed is asked last for the next 10 minutes, so a dead server costs one wait, not one on every solve.
  • Planet lookup: the kept planet values are used; the line under the planet boxes ends with the note, and the transit report's ephemeris line says "from cache (fetched …)". A star with several planets still needs the live Archive to list them.

A damaged catalog copy. On 10 Nov 2026, with CDS down, the CfA mirror's copy of APASS sent impossible magnitudes (every star V 0–2, B 999999), and SHOBS-P 2.3.2 and older used and saved them: comps and the check got V values near 0.6, colours far off, and comp suggestions said "very red" for nearly every star. From 2.3.3 such an answer is never used or saved; the log says so in one line and the next mirror (CDS, CfA, then Cape Town) is asked, and a copy saved earlier from a damaged answer is asked again. If you reduced a night on 10 Nov 2026 with CDS down, redo the comp step (catalog magnitudes, comp suggestions and the AAVSO report's comp magnitudes); transit depths and times use only measured flux ratios and are not affected.

To start fresh, press Clear cached catalogs on the Credits page (chapter 3).

Results

Problem Fix
Error bars much larger than the scatter (χ²ν far below 1). Usually the camera gain: the EGAIN keyword is missing or wrong in your headers. The photometry log's Photon noise line says which gain was used. On camera-binned frames, check that line's second sentence: SHOBS-P 2.3.0 tells from the sky's noise whether the camera summed or averaged the binned pixels (chapter 7). Nights reduced with 2.2.21 or earlier keep their old error bars until you run photometry again.
The comps are much fainter than the target, and the points are noisy. Read the Stack row's line on the Photometry page. If it says "⚠ Comps are weak …", press Stack (chapter 8, "Stacking when comps are weak"), or choose brighter comps.
Frames are missing from the light curve after Use comps… dropped a comp. Every comp was lost in those frames at photometry, so they were skipped. Press Measure skipped frames on the Photometry page's orange line (chapter 8).
A comp near the edge cost many frames in an older version. Run photometry again in 2.3.0: a comp off the frame edge is now left out of those frames only (chapter 8, "Comps near the frame edge").
Steps between nights in a series. Different comps, apertures or equipment between nights. Read the Careful: lines when adding a night, and use Use comps… to put every night on the same comps.
A step at the meridian flip. Look at Comp health…: a comp that jumps at the flip is a common cause. Read the log after the run: a line "⚠ After the meridian flip the … apertures were off their stars …" means an aperture problem, not the stars (chapter 12). Try Pier side as segments (Variables mode).
One star's comps turned up on another star's night (points flagged nocomp). Press New reduction on the Input page before you start on another star (chapter 5).
Frames on an alt-az mount are lost or misaligned through the night. Set Mount to Alt-az in the Optics box (Input page), so the alignment follows the field rotation.
The run warns "⚠ The field turned by 180° between frames … — that looks like a meridian flip." Mount is set to Alt-az, but the frames come from a German equatorial mount (often a setting left from another telescope's data). Set Mount to German equatorial and run photometry again.
A transit's depth is far off on a night with a meridian flip. Check that Mount is German equatorial, so the fit can test a flip step, and read the Flip step row (chapter 17). Keep 30 minutes or more of baseline on each side of the flip.
The transit fit does not start. It needs a planet on the Input page and at least 20 usable points. Look at the hint under the page title.
The Output pages show an old result. Run photometry again: an old transit fit is cleared as soon as the data change.
You lost a night from a series. Close the series and load <name>_prev.cvseries, the version from before this session.

Reporting a problem

Problems and ideas go to the project's GitHub Issues page (github.com/SHOBS-S/SHOBS-P/issues). The forms ask for:

  • the SHOBS-P version (bottom of the sidebar, for example Version 2.3.0),
  • your computer's system (Windows, macOS or Linux, and which version),
  • your camera, and whether it is color or monochrome,
  • the mode and the step,
  • what you expected and what happened,
  • a screenshot,
  • selftest_log.txt from the self-test, and .shobs_p.log from your user folder,
  • for a setup problem on Linux or a Mac, pip_log.txt (see above).

A copy of the program's own log (from the Calibrate page) around the time of the problem helps too.

Previous: 23. Messages explained · Next: 25. Glossary

25. Glossary

Terms used in SHOBS-P and in this manual, in alphabetical order. Words in bold inside an entry have their own entry.

a/R* — The planet's orbital distance divided by the star's radius. With the impact parameter, it sets the transit's shape and duration. The fit holds it near the published value.

AAVSO sequence — The comparison stars the AAVSO publishes for a variable star's chart, with checked magnitudes. SHOBS-P draws them as thin dashed cyan rings and suggests them first. Their labels are magnitudes without the decimal point: 91 is V 9.1.

Airmass — How much atmosphere the light passes through: 1 at the zenith, about 2 at 30° altitude. Stars dim and redden with airmass. SHOBS-P works it out from the frame time, the target's position and your site.

Alt-az mount — A mount that moves in altitude and azimuth, unlike a German equatorial mount. It never makes a meridian flip, but the field slowly turns on the sensor through the night (field rotation), unless a derotator turns the camera to follow it. Set Mount to Alt-az in the Optics box (Input page). See also zenith keyhole.

Alias (one-day alias) — A false period in a period search, caused by observing only at night, once a day. A period near 1 day, or one whose frequency differs from the true one by 1 per day, is suspect. SHOBS-P warns about it.

Aperture, sky ring — The circle of radius Aperture r in which a star's light is summed, and the ring between sky in and sky out where the sky background is measured and subtracted.

AUID — AAVSO Unique Identifier, such as 000-BBC-123. Every star in the AAVSO's charts and VSX has one. SHOBS-P puts it in AAVSO reports when a comp or check star comes from the AAVSO sequence.

Baseline — In transit work, the out-of-transit light curve before ingress and after egress. The fit learns the star's level and trends from it. See 16. Prepare for a transit night.

Bayer pattern, OSC, MONO — A one-shot-color (OSC) camera has a mosaic of red, green and blue filters over its pixels in a pattern such as RGGB. A monochrome camera has none (SHOBS-P calls it MONO) and uses a filter wheel.

ΔBIC — A score that compares two models (here: a transit against a flat line), with a penalty for the extra parameters. SHOBS-P needs a ΔBIC of 10 or more to call a transit detected.

Binning — Adding neighboring pixels together. SHOBS-P's Binning (Input page) adds same-color pixels only, so colors stay separate. It is separate from any binning done in the camera. A transit report's #BINNING is the camera's binning; SHOBS-P's own "processing bin" goes into the report's notes. A camera can sum its binned pixels or average them; SHOBS-P tells which from the sky's noise, because it changes how many electrons one ADU holds (chapter 7).

BJD_TDB — Barycentric Julian Date in Barycentric Dynamical Time: the time the light would have reached the center of mass of the solar system, on a uniform time scale. The standard for exoplanet timing. It differs from JD by up to about ±8 minutes, depending on the star's position and the time of year.

Check star — A steady star measured like the target, to show whether the comparison stars are behaving. The AAVSO requires one for ensemble reports.

Chart ID — The ID of the AAVSO chart (from the Variable Star Plotter) whose comparison stars you used. na when the comps are not from an AAVSO chart.

χ²ν (reduced chi-square) — How the scatter of the points around the model compares with their error bars. About 1 is right. Well below 1: the error bars are too big. Well above 1: there is noise the error bars leave out.

Cloud flag, cloud edge — Flags on frames where the comparison stars lost more light than the Cloud flag limit (Output page), or that sit at the edge of a cloudy stretch. Flagged frames are left out of the period search, reports and the transit fit.

Comp (comparison star) — A steady star of known magnitude in the same frame. The target's magnitude is measured against it, so changes in the sky affect both and cancel. SHOBS-P takes up to ten, C1 to C10.

Corner plot — A grid of plots of an MCMC fit's samples: one histogram per fitted parameter, and every pair of parameters against each other. It shows each parameter's posterior, and whether two parameters trade off against each other. Save corner plot… in the Transit fit page's Results row.

Credible band — The range of model curves the MCMC allows: the 68 % band holds the middle 68 % of the sampled models at each moment, the 95 % band the middle 95 %. Shaded around the transit fit.

Defocus, donut — Defocusing the telescope on purpose spreads each star's light over many pixels, so bright stars don't saturate and pixel-to-pixel errors average out. A strongly defocused star looks like a ring (a donut), often with a dark center from the secondary mirror. SHOBS-P finds such fields by itself and centers each star on its light.

Derotator — A rotator on an alt-az mount that turns the camera through the night to follow the field rotation, so the stars stay put on the sensor. SHOBS-P recognizes one from a rotator reading that drifts smoothly in the headers.

Dark flat — A dark taken at the flats' exposure. SHOBS-P takes the dark flats off the flats, and with no bias frames they also stand in for the bias. They go in the Dark flats row of the Input page (chapter 7).

Detrending — Removing a slow trend from a light curve, for example with airmass, while fitting. In the transit fit the trend is fitted together with the transit.

DTG — Date-time group, the stamp on every file SHOBS-P writes: 121153Z OCT 26 means the 12th, 11:53 UTC, October 2026.

Effective exposure — The exposure of a stack: the sum of its frames' exposures. Reports of stacked points give it as their exposure time.

EGAIN — The FITS header keyword with the camera gain in electrons per count (ADU). SHOBS-P uses it to work out the photon noise in each point. Without it, the gain is estimated from the sky. EGAIN is the gain of one native pixel; on frames the camera binned by averaging, one stored ADU holds bin² × EGAIN electrons.

Ensemble — Using the average of several comparison stars. In an AAVSO report the comparison name is then ENSEMBLE.

Ephemeris, T0, epoch — A planet's (or variable's) timetable: a reference time T0 and the period. Epoch is the number of periods since T0. The predicted mid-transit is T0 + epoch × period.

EXOTIC — The transit-fitting program of NASA's Exoplanet Watch project. SHOBS-P reads and writes the same AAVSO exoplanet report format.

ExoClock — A project (exoclock.space) that collects transit times of known planets from small telescopes to keep their ephemerides up to date for ESA's Ariel space mission. Save for ExoClock… writes its upload file; Go to ExoClock… opens its upload page.

ExoFOP (ExoFOP-TESS) — The website where follow-up observations of TESS targets (TOIs) are collected.

FAP (false-alarm probability) — The chance that noise alone would produce a peak as high as the one found in a period search. SHOBS-P calls a period significant at 1% or less, counted both by points and by nights.

Flat, bias, dark — Calibration frames. A bias is a zero-length exposure (the camera's electronic offset), a dark is an exposure with the shutter closed (heat signal), and a flat is an evenly lit exposure that maps vignetting and dust shadows.

Flip step — In the transit fit: a jump in the star's level at a meridian flip, fitted as the baseline after the flip times (1 + step), in ppt. SHOBS-P keeps it only when it lowers the BIC by 10 or more (ΔBIC); a kept step is fitted together with the transit. In Variables mode, the flip step of a night is the offset in magnitudes that lines up the two pier sides (chapter 12).

FWHM — Full width at half maximum: the size of a star's image, a measure of seeing and focus, in pixels.

Impact parameter (b) — How far from the star's center the planet crosses, in units of the star's radius. 0 is straight across the middle; near 1 is a grazing transit.

Ingress, egress, T14, Tc — Ingress is when the planet starts to cross the star, egress when it has left. T14 is the total duration from first to last contact. Tc is the time of mid-transit.

JD, HJD — Julian Date, a running day count used in astronomy (JD 2461000 is in 2025). HJD is the time the light would have reached the Sun's center. AAVSO variable-star reports use JD; timing work uses HJD or BJD_TDB.

JD_UTC — A Julian Date counted in UTC, the clock time at your telescope, with no correction for where the Earth is. ExoClock's upload file uses it; SHOBS-P converts its BJD_TDB times back to JD_UTC for that file.

Limb darkening — A star looks darker near its edge than at its center, which rounds the bottom of a transit. SHOBS-P sets it from the star's temperature and the filter.

Linear limit — The raw level (in ADU) up to which your camera's response stays proportional to the light. Above it, but below saturation, a star reads too faint. Typed in the linear ADU box on the Photometry page.

Lomb-Scargle periodogram — A period search for unevenly spaced data. Its plot shows how well each trial period fits; the highest peak is the best period.

MCMC — Markov-chain Monte Carlo: a way to find each fitted value's uncertainty by exploring many parameter combinations near the best fit. Quick, Standard and Thorough set how long it runs.

Meridian flip — On a German equatorial mount, the telescope swings to the other side of the pier when the target crosses the meridian. The field ends up rotated 180° on the sensor. One set of flats still covers both sides.

Mid-exposure — The middle of a frame's exposure: its start time plus half the exposure time. SHOBS-P always times a frame at mid-exposure. On ExoClock's upload form, set Time stamp to Mid-exposure.

NEB (nearby eclipsing binary) — A faint eclipsing binary star close to the target that can fake a transit in a low-resolution survey such as TESS. The NEB check measures the neighbors to rule them out.

O − C — Observed minus calculated: the measured time of a transit (or a variable's maximum or minimum) minus the time the ephemeris predicts. A changing O − C shows a period change or a wrong ephemeris. SHOBS-P gives it in minutes and in σ, where σ combines your timing error and the ephemeris's own uncertainty at that epoch. A constant O − C over several nights usually means the ephemeris is out of date.

Plate solution (WCS) — The mapping between pixels in the image and positions on the sky. Many capture programs write one into the FITS header; otherwise SHOBS-P finds it by matching star patterns to the catalogs.

Points: Per frame / Stacked — The switch on the Photometry page that chooses whether a night's points are its single frames or its stacks. Every result, report and saved file uses the chosen set; the Results use line says which.

Posterior — What a fit learns about a parameter: the spread of values the data allow, here the MCMC samples. A round hump is a measurement; one piled up against the end of its allowed range (the prior) is not.

Red noise, β — Noise that is correlated in time (seeing, tracking, thin cloud) rather than random from frame to frame. β above 1 means the binned scatter falls more slowly than random noise would; the fit widens its uncertainties by β.

Rp/R*, depth — The planet's radius divided by the star's. The depth SHOBS-P reports is (Rp/R*)²; the actual dip at mid-transit is somewhat deeper because of limb darkening.

Scintillation — Twinkling: fast brightness changes caused by the atmosphere. It adds noise that grows with airmass and is larger for small telescopes. SHOBS-P includes it in the error bars when Include scintillation (atmospheric twinkling) in the error bars is ticked and the telescope aperture is entered.

Series — A file (.cvseries) holding several nights of one star in one mode, with every setting needed to add more nights. See 10. Build a multi-night series.

Spare comp — A catalog star near the comps' brightness that SHOBS-P measures on every frame, so you can swap it in later with Use comps… without reducing again.

Stack — A sigma-clipped mean of N calibrated frames in a row, lined up by whole pixels, measured as one point. Stacking lowers the photon noise of weak comps by about √N but hides any change faster than the stack. SHOBS-P suggests N from the comps' noise and a time cap (chapter 8). A stack never spans a meridian flip or a gap.

Superpixel — A way of turning a color camera's mosaic into an image: each 2×2 group of pixels becomes one pixel of the chosen color channel. The image has half the width and height of the sensor, and no colors are mixed.

Transmission loss (dew/haze?) — A smooth fading of every star together under a steady sky, typical of dew on the optics or high haze. SHOBS-P tells it apart from patchy cloud and asks whether to keep those points.

Untransformed — Magnitudes measured through your own filter and camera without converting them to the standard system. SHOBS-P's magnitudes are untransformed; reports say so.

VSX, SIMBAD — The AAVSO's Variable Star Index and the CDS astronomical database. SHOBS-P uses them to look up targets and to warn when a comp, check or candidate star is a known variable.

Watch star — A star you ask SHOBS-P to measure on every run, with its own light curve, without it being the target. See 21. Follow a candidate.

× normal — In a field scan: a star's scatter divided by the typical scatter of stars of the same brightness. 3 or more makes a candidate by default.

Zenith keyhole — The small patch of sky around the zenith (above 85° in SHOBS-P) where an alt-az mount must turn very fast in azimuth and the field rotation gets extreme, so frames there can trail or smear. When you scan the folders of an alt-az night that went above 85°, the log says so (chapter 16).

Zero point — The offset that turns instrumental magnitudes into catalog magnitudes, measured from comparison stars or, in a field scan, from all the catalog stars in the field.

Previous: 24. Troubleshooting · Next: 26. Credits and how to cite SHOBS-P

26. Credits and how to cite SHOBS-P

SHOBS-P stands on a great deal of other people's work: Python libraries, star catalogs, online services and published methods. When you publish results made with it, please credit those sources, and SHOBS-P itself.

The Credits and sources page

To see every source SHOBS-P uses:

  1. Click Credits and sources at the bottom of the sidebar.

  2. At the top, How to cite SHOBS-P shows the suggested citation for the version you are running. Click Copy to put it on the clipboard (the status bar says Citation copied to the clipboard.). See "How to cite SHOBS-P" below.

  3. Read the list under it. It has three parts:

    • SOFTWARE: Astropy, photutils, ccdproc, NumPy, SciPy and Matplotlib.
    • CATALOGS AND SERVICES: Gaia DR3, APASS, Tycho-2, AAVSO VSX, the AAVSO Variable Star Plotter, SIMBAD, the NASA Exoplanet Archive (planet values and TESS TOI status), ExoFOP, ExoClock (planet priorities) and VizieR.
    • METHODS: the published methods behind the period search, the color transformations, the transit model, the MCMC sampler, the red-noise estimate, the error bars, the timing methods, the Sun's position (for the approximate BJD_TDB of a report that has no site) and more.

    For each source the page says what SHOBS-P uses it for and gives the reference. Sentences in quotes are the acknowledgements each source asks for, in its own words.

  4. To keep a copy, click Save credits as text…. It writes SHOBS-P_credits.txt (into the Output folder if one is set), with the citation at the top.

  5. Click Back to return to the step you came from.

The status bar's second line is a short version of the same credits, always on screen.

What to include in a paper or article

Include the quoted acknowledgement and reference of each source your result actually used. For example:

  • any result: Astropy, photutils, NumPy, and the catalogs your comparison stars came from (the Photometry page says which catalog each magnitude came from);
  • a light curve with comparison stars from the AAVSO sequence: the AAVSO Variable Star Plotter;
  • a transit fit: the NASA Exoplanet Archive (for the planet's parameters) and the transit and limb-darkening methods; ExoClock too if you used its priority list or ephemeris;
  • a field scan: VSX and SIMBAD (for the known-variable check).

When in doubt, include the whole list from the saved text file in an appendix or a data note.

An AAVSO report or an ExoFOP upload does not need these credits; they are for published work.

How to cite SHOBS-P

SHOBS-P is archived on Zenodo. Each release has its own DOI, so you can cite the exact version you used. One DOI always points to the latest version:

DOI: 10.5281/zenodo.23417063

The suggested citation, exactly as the Credits page shows it (and copies with Copy):

Armbrust, J. W., and Shiloh Hill Observatory, SHOBS-P: Shiloh Hill Observatory Photometry, version 2.3.0, Zenodo, doi:10.5281/zenodo.23417063

The version in it is always the one you are running.

In BibTeX:

@software{shobs_p,
  author    = {Armbrust, John W. and {Shiloh Hill Observatory}},
  title     = {{SHOBS-P: Shiloh Hill Observatory Photometry}},
  version   = {2.3.0},
  year      = {2026},
  publisher = {Zenodo},
  doi       = {10.5281/zenodo.23417063},
  url       = {https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23417063}
}

Always give the version you used. It is at the bottom of the sidebar (Version 2.3.0), in the citation on the Credits page, and in the #SOFTWARE=SHOBS-P 2.3.0 line of every AAVSO report SHOBS-P writes.

To cite one specific release rather than the latest, open the DOI above. The Zenodo page lists every version with its own DOI; use the one for your version.

CITATION.cff

The program's GitHub repository contains a file CITATION.cff with the same information in a standard, machine-readable form: title, authors (John W. Armbrust and Shiloh Hill Observatory), version, release date, DOI, license and a short description. On the repository's GitHub page, the Cite this repository button (in the right-hand sidebar) turns it into an APA or BibTeX reference you can copy. Reference managers such as Zotero can read it too.

License

SHOBS-P is free software under the GNU General Public License, version 3 or later. You may use it, share it and change it under that license's terms. It comes with no warranty. The full text is in the LICENSE file in the program folder.

How SHOBS-P was made

SHOBS-P was designed, tested and checked against real observations at Shiloh Hill Observatory. Most of the code was written with the help of an AI assistant (Anthropic's Claude), working to the observatory's specifications and checked against its data.

Bug reports, questions and ideas are welcome on the project's GitHub Issues page (see 24. Troubleshooting).
